Stable Harmonic Analysis and Stable Transfer
Matthew Sunohara

Abstract
. Langlands posed the question of whether a local functorial transfer map of stable tempered characters can be interpolated by the transpose of a linear operator between spaces of stable orbital integrals of test functions. These so-called stable transfer operators are intended to serve as the main local ingredient in Beyond Endoscopy, his proposed strategy for proving the Principle of Functoriality. Working over a local field of characteristic zero and assuming a hypothesis on the local Langlands correspondence for p p -adic groups, we prove the existence of continuous stable transfer operators between spaces of stable orbital integrals of Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions, test functions, and K K -finite test functions. This is achieved via stable Paley–Wiener theorems for each of the three types of function spaces. The stable Paley–Wiener theorem for Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions is new and includes the result that stable tempered characters span a weak- ∗ * dense subspace of the space of stable tempered distributions, a result previously unknown for p p -adic groups.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
22E50 (primary); 22E30, 22E35 (secondary)

1.
Introduction

Beyond Endoscopy is the strategy proposed by Langlands for proving his Principle of Functoriality in general
[
Lan00
]
(see also
[
Lan04
,
Lan07
]
). The strategy involves developing new refinements of the stable trace formula and comparisons between them using stable transfer operators. These operators, which form the main local ingredient of Beyond Endoscopy and are our focus here, were introduced by Langlands in
[
Lan04
,
Lan13
]
. We refer to the articles
[
Art17
,
Sak23
]
by Arthur and Sakellaridis for an overview of the strategy of Beyond Endoscopy. In
[
Art17
]
, Arthur explains the formal analogy between the role of stable transfer operators in Beyond Endoscopy and the role of endoscopic transfer operators in the theory of Endoscopy.

Throughout this paper, we fix a local field
F
of characteristic zero and fix an algebraic closure
\overline{F}
of
F
. Let
W_{F}
be the Weil group of
F
and let
L_{F}
be the complex version of the local Langlands group of
F
, that is,
L_{F}=W_{F}
if
F
is archimedean and
L_{F}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times W_{F}
if
F
is non-archimedean.

Let
H
and
G
be connected reductive groups over
F
and let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an injective
L
-homomorphism (taken up to
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy). Throughout this paper, we use the Weil form of the
L
-group. We assume that
G
is quasisplit, so that we have a well-defined pushforward map

\xi_{*}:\Phi(H)\longrightarrow\Phi(G)

of
L
-parameters, called functorial transfer. Furthermore, we assume that
\xi
is tempered in the sense that
\xi|_{W_{F}}
is a tempered
L
-parameter of
G
, so that
\xi_{*}
restricts to a map
\xi_{*}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\to\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
of tempered
L
-parameters.

Let
\Pi(H)
denote the set of equivalence classes of irreducible admissible representations of
H(F)
. A local Langlands correspondence for
H
is a surjective “reciprocity” map

\rec_{H}:\Pi(H)\longrightarrow\Phi(H)

with finite fibres satisfying various desiderata
[
Bor79
,
KT
]
. The fibre
\Pi_{\phi}
over
\phi\in\Phi(H)
is called the
L
-packet of
\phi
. A local Langlands correspondence is known and uniquely characterised for real groups
[
Lan89
,
AV16
,
AK
]
. A local Langlands correspondence is known in various forms for
p
-adic groups
[
HT01
,
Hen00
,
Sch13
,
Art13
,
Mok15
,
Mg14
]
.

Beyond Endoscopy is intended to be built upon the theory of Endoscopy and the local Langlands correspondence. Thus, we will assume the existence of a local Langlands correspondence for
p
-adic groups satisfying a subset of the usual desiderata. See
Hypothesis

4.2
for the precise form of our hypothesis on the local Langlands correspondence for
p
-adic groups. Part of the local Langlands correspondence is the assignment to each
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
of a stable tempered character
\Theta_{\phi}
. The distribution
\Theta_{\phi}
is a positive integral linear combination of the tempered characters
\Theta_{\pi}
for
\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}
. The functorial transfer
\xi_{*}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\to\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
of tempered
L
-parameters thus determines a functorial transfer map
\xi_{*}:\Theta_{\phi}\mapsto\Theta_{\xi_{*}\phi}
from the set of stable tempered characters of
H
to the set of stable tempered characters of
G
. Langlands posed the following question
[
Lan13
, Questions A & B]
.

Question 1.1
.

For each
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G(F))
, does there exist a function
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(H(F))
, called a
stable transfer of
f
, such that

\langle\Theta_{\phi},\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f\rangle=\langle\xi_{*}\Theta_{\phi},f\rangle

for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
?

In
[
Lan13
]
, Langlands gave an affirmative answer to
Question

1.1
in the case when
G=\SL_{2}
,
H
is a maximal torus of
G
, and
\xi
is a natural
L
-embedding
[
Lan13
, §2.1–§2.4 (p. 182–210)]
. This case had previously been studied by Gelfand–Graev in
[
GG63
]
, where an explicit formula for the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
is given. The Gelfand–Graev formula was also established in
[
Sak22b
, §10.5]
. Johnstone gave an affirmative answer and obtained a formula for
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
in the case when
G=\SL_{\ell}
or
G=\GL_{\ell}
with
\ell
an odd prime,
H
is an elliptic maximal torus of
G
, and
\xi
is a natural
L
-embedding
[
Joh17
,
Joh22
]
. The arguments in these works involve detailed computations with explicit stable character formulas, which are not available in general. The existence of stable transfers of test functions has also been explored in
[
JL20
,
Tho20
]
. Sakellaridis has studied stable transfer for relative functoriality, and in certain low rank cases has proved that they exist and obtained explicit formulas for them
[
Sak13
,
Sak19a
,
Sak19b
,
Sak22a
,
Sak22b
]
.

The existence of stable transfers is useful in global applications of the stable trace formula that lie outside the theory of Endoscopy but do not belong to the program of Beyond Endoscopy. In
[
DGG22
]
stable transfers are used to prove statistics of automorphic representations for unramified unitary groups, with applications to the Sato–Tate Conjecture, the Sarnak–Xue Conjecture, and the cohomology of locally symmetric spaces. Extensions of this work to other unitary groups and other classical groups require a better understanding of the properties of stable transfer. We expect that stable transfer may have other applications outside of Beyond Endoscopy, both in global and local harmonic analysis.

In order to give a more natural formulation of
Question

1.1
, we recall the basic notions of stable harmonic analysis. For brevity, we write
C_{c}^{\infty}(H)=C_{c}^{\infty}(H(F))
and we write
\mathcal{C}(H)=\mathcal{C}(H(F))
for the space of Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions, both equipped with their usual locally convex topologies, which makes the inclusion
C_{c}^{\infty}(H)\to\mathcal{C}(H)
continuous with dense image. We recall that a distribution on
H(F)
, i.e. an element of the strong dual
C_{c}^{\infty}(H)^{\prime}
, is tempered if it extends continuously to an element of
\mathcal{C}(H)^{\prime}
. Since stable tempered characters are tempered distributions, it is thus natural to consider the analogue of
Question

1.1
where
C_{c}^{\infty}
is replaced by
\mathcal{C}
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(H)
. For a regular semisimple stable conjugacy class
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(H)
in
H(F)
, we denote the normalised stable orbital integral of
f
at
\delta
by
f^{H}(\delta)=|D^{H}(\delta)|^{1/2}O_{\delta}(f)
, where
D^{H}(\delta)
is the Weyl discriminant. We denote the resulting quotient space of stable orbital integrals by

\mathcal{S}(H)=\{f^{H}:\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(H)\to\mathbb{C}:f\in\mathcal{C}(H)\}.

A tempered distribution
u\in\mathcal{C}(H)^{\prime}
is said to be stable if it descends to an element of
\mathcal{S}(H)^{\prime}
.

Stable tempered characters are stable tempered distributions. For
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
, we write
\widehat{f^{H}}(\phi)=\Theta_{\phi}(f)
. The resulting function
\widehat{f^{H}}
is the stable Fourier transform of
f
. In the literature,
\widehat{f^{H}}(\phi)
is often denoted by
f^{H}(\phi)
, and thus
f^{H}
is used to denote both the stable orbital integrals and the stable Fourier transform of
f
. We will also follow this practice below. We denote the resulting quotient space of stable Fourier transforms by

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(H)=\{\widehat{f^{H}}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\to\mathbb{C}:f\in\mathcal{C}(H)\}.

The stable Fourier transform of
f
only depends on its stable orbital integrals
f^{H}(\delta)
for
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(H)
(in fact, even for
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(H)
), so the map
\mathcal{C}(H)\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(H),f\mapsto\widehat{f^{H}}
descends to a surjective continuous linear operator

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}(H)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(H).

By replacing the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space
\mathcal{C}(H)
with the space of test functions
C_{c}^{\infty}(H)
, we obtain spaces
\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{c}}(H)
, which are the test function analogues of
\mathcal{S}(H)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}(H)
. The stable Fourier transform restricts to a continuous surjective linear operator
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{c}}(H)
. For a maximal compact subgroup
K
of
H(F)
, we have analogous spaces
\mathcal{S}_{f}(H)
and
\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{f}}(H)
defined in terms of the space
C_{c}^{\infty}(H,K)
of
K
-finite test functions, and the Fourier transform restricts further to a continuous surjective linear operator
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{f}(H)\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{f}}(H)
.

Stable spectral density for test functions tells us that the stable tempered characters of
H
span a weak-
*
dense subspace of the space of stable distributions on
H(F)
. Equivalently, for
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(H)
, the function
f^{H}
is determined by
\widehat{f^{H}}
, that is, we have a continuous linear isomorphism

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{c}}(H).

It follows that an equivalent formulation of
Question

1.1
is whether there exists a linear operator

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}:\mathcal{S}_{c}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)

such that

\Theta_{\phi}\circ\mathcal{T}_{\xi}=\Theta_{\xi_{*}\phi}

for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
.

Our main result is as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

If
F
is non-archimedean, we assume
Hypothesis

4.2
for
H
and
G
. There exists a continuous linear operator

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}:\mathcal{S}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(H)

whose transpose

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(H)^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(G)^{\prime}

satisfies

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime}\Theta_{\phi}=\Theta_{\xi_{*}\phi}

for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
restricts to continuous linear operators
\mathcal{S}_{c}(G)\to\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)
and
\mathcal{S}_{f}(G)\to\mathcal{S}_{f}(H)
.

This provides an affirmative answer to
Question

1.1
in general (conditional on
Hypothesis

4.2
in the
p
-adic case). We call the operator
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
the stable transfer operator attached to
\xi
.

Stable transfer should not be confused with endoscopic transfer, which maps from the space
\mathcal{I}(G)
of invariant orbital integrals of Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions to the space
\mathcal{S}(H)
, where
H
is an endoscopic group of
G
. Stable transfer has also been called functorial transfer
[
Art08
]
and stable-stable transfer
[
Tho20
]
in order to avoid confusion with endoscopic transfer.

With a view towards applying stable transfer to the stable trace formula, an important problem remains of understanding how the stable orbital integrals
(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G})(\delta_{H})
for
\delta_{H}\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(H)
relate to the stable orbital integrals
f^{G}(\delta_{G})
for
\delta_{G}\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
. Spectrally, the relation between
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G}
and
f^{G}
is very simple. The stable Fourier transform of
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G}
is the pullback of the stable Fourier transform of
f^{G}
along the functorial transfer map
\xi_{*}
:

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G})=\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}(f^{G})\circ\xi_{*}.

Comparing this with the Fourier-restriction formula for the classical Radon transform suggests that stable transfer operators are the analogues in stable harmonic analysis of Radon transforms in classical harmonic analysis.

There is a natural Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
, and similarly there are natural Paley–Wiener spaces
PW^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
and
PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
. These will be defined in
Section

4.4
. We prove the following result, which contains stable Paley–Wiener theorems for Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions, test functions, and
K
-finite test functions.

Theorem 1.2
.

If
F
is non-archimedean, we assume
Hypothesis

4.2
for
H
. The stable Fourier transform is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}(H)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)

and restricts to isomorphisms of topological vector spaces
\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)\to PW^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
and
\mathcal{S}_{f}(H)\longrightarrow PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
.

The stable Paley–Wiener theorem for Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions is new. In the case where
H
is quasisplit, Arthur proved the stable Paley–Wiener theorem for test functions on
p
-adic groups in
[
Art96
]
, while Moeglin–Waldspurger proved the stable Paley–Wiener theorems for test functions and
K
-finite test functions on real groups in
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV]
. (See the paragraph below
Theorem

4.2
for further discussion on these results and their relation to this work.) The injectivity of the stable Fourier transform on
\mathcal{S}(H)
is equivalent to stable spectral density for Harish-Chandra Schwartz functions. This result is new for
p
-adic groups; for real groups, it was established by Shelstad
[
She79
, Lemma 5.3]
.

Granting
Theorem

1.2
, to prove
Theorem

1.1
it then suffices to prove that pullback along
\xi_{*}
gives a well-defined continuous linear operator

\xi^{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)

which restricts to continuous linear operators
PW^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\to PW^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
and
PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\to PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)
. Indeed, we may then simply define
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
to make the following diagram commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{*}}

and then
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
has the properties claimed. The proof that pullback along
\xi_{*}
gives the well defined maps
\xi^{*}
makes use of the fact the
\xi_{*}
is
\pi_{0}
-finite, that is, the fibres of the map

\pi_{0}(\xi_{*}):\pi_{0}(\Phi(H))\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\Phi(G))

are finite. We prove this as
Theorem

5.4
.

In
Section

2
, we set up the basic notation and conventions for the remainder of the paper. In
Section

3
, we recall the results from invariant harmonic analysis that we will use: the theory of
R
-groups, Arthur’s virtual tempered representations, and invariant Paley–Wiener theorems. In
Section

3.4
, we define abstract Schwartz and Paley–Wiener spaces in a form suitable for invariant and stable Paley–Wiener theorems. In
Section

4
, we recall the fundamentals of stable harmonic analysis, including our hypothesis on the local Langlands correspondence for
p
-adic groups (
Hypothesis

4.2
), and prove the stable Paley–Wiener theorems contained in
Theorem

1.2
. In
Section

5
, we prove
Theorem

1.1
and give some simple examples of stable transfer operators.

2.
Notation and conventions

Recall that we have fixed a local field
F
of characteristic zero and an algebraic closure
\overline{F}
of
F
. If
F
is non-archimedean, we let
q_{F}
denote the cardinality of the residue field of
F
. Let
|\cdot|_{F}
denote the canonical absolute value of
F
, which is defined for all
a\in F
by
\!\mathop{}d{(ax)}=|a|_{F}\!\mathop{}d{x}
, for any Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d{x}
on
F
. We also denote its canonical extension to an absolute value on
\overline{F}
by
|\cdot|_{F}
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
. We will say that
G
is a real group if
F
is archimedean and a
p
-adic group if
F
is a
p
-adic field. We will use
X^{*}(G)
(resp.
X_{*}(G)
) to denote the group of algebraic characters
G\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
(resp. cocharacters
\mathbb{G}_{m}\to G
) over
F
. Thus, the group of algebraic characters (resp. cocharacters) of the base change
G_{\overline{F}}
will be denoted by
X^{*}(G_{\overline{F}})
(resp.
X_{*}(G_{\overline{F}})
).

We have the real vector spaces

\mathfrak{a}_{G}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(G),\mathbb{R})

and

\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}=X^{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}.

Note that we have a natural perfect pairing between
\mathfrak{a}_{G}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
, realising
\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
as the dual space of
\mathfrak{a}_{G}
. Let
A_{G}
be the split component of the centre of
G
. The restriction gives an injective homomorphism
X^{*}(G)\to X^{*}(A_{G})
with finite cokernel. By functoriality, we obtain isomorphisms
\mathfrak{a}_{A_{G}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{a}_{G}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathfrak{a}_{A_{G}}^{*}
. In this way we identify
\mathfrak{a}_{A_{G}}=\mathfrak{a}_{G}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{A_{G}}^{*}=\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
.

We have the Harish-Chandra logarithm homomorphism
H_{G}:G(F)\to\mathfrak{a}_{G}
defined by

\langle H_{G}(x),\chi\rangle=\log|\chi(x)|_{F}

for all
x\in G(F)
and
\chi\in X^{*}(G)
. Let

G(F)^{1}=\ker H_{G}=\bigcap_{\chi\in X^{*}(G)}\ker|\chi|_{F}.

Note that
H_{A_{G}}
is the restriction of
H_{G}
, and therefore
A_{G}(F)^{1}=A_{G}(F)\cap G(F)^{1}
. Let

\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}=H_{G}(G(F))=G(F)/G(F)^{1}

and

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}=\mathfrak{a}_{A_{G},F}=H_{G}(A_{G}(F))=A_{G}(F)/A_{G}(F)^{1}.

We define

\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F},2\pi i\mathbb{Z})

and similarly

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F},2\pi i\mathbb{Z}).

We have the inclusions

\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{G}.

If
F
is archimedean, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}=\mathfrak{a}_{G}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!=\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!=0
. If
F
is non-archimedean, then
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
are (full) lattices in
\mathfrak{a}_{G}
, and
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!\subseteq\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}\!
are lattices in
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
.

We denote the group of unramified characters of
G(F)
by

X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)=\Hom(G(F)/G(F)^{1},\mathbb{C}^{\times})

and the subgroup of unitary unramified characters by

X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)^{1}=\Hom(G(F)/G(F)^{1},\mathbb{C}^{1}).

We have a surjective homomorphism

\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\longrightarrow X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)

defined by
\lambda\mapsto|\cdot|_{G}^{\lambda}
, where
|g|_{G}^{\lambda}=e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(g)\rangle}
. If
\lambda=\theta\otimes s\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, then
|g|_{G}^{\lambda}=|\theta(g)|^{s}
. The surjection
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
descends to an isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
. Thus, if
F
is archimedean, then the map
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
is an isomorphism and
X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
is a complex vector space. If
F
is non-archimedean, then
X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
is a complex torus and the homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
factors through the complex torus
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\frac{2\pi i}{\log q_{F}}X^{*}(G)
with finite kernel
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\frac{2\pi i}{\log q_{F}}X^{*}(G)
.

2.1.
Parabolic and Levi subgroups

We recall the following standard notation.

•

\mathcal{P}(M)=\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
is the set of parabolic subgroups of
G
with
M
as a Levi factor; and

•

\mathcal{L}(M)=\mathcal{L}^{G}(M)
is the set of Levi subgroups of
G
containing
M
.

If
P
is a parabolic subgroup of
G
, we denote the unipotent radical of
P
by
N_{P}
. We refer to a pair
(P,M)
consisting of a Levi subgroup
M
of
G
and a parabolic subgroup
P\in\mathcal{P}(M)
as a parabolic pair of
G
.

Let
M\subseteq G
be a Levi subgroup of
G
. We have
A_{G}\subseteq A_{M}
. The restriction homomorphism
X^{*}(G)\to X^{*}(M)
is injective, so gives rise to a linear injection

\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}=X^{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}=X^{*}(M)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}

and a dual linear surjection

\mathfrak{a}_{M}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(M),\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{G}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(G),\mathbb{R}).

The restriction homomorphism
X^{*}(A_{M})\to X^{*}(A_{G})
is surjective, so gives rise to a linear surjection

\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}=X^{*}(A_{M})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}=X^{*}(A_{G})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}

and a dual linear injection

\mathfrak{a}_{G}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(A_{G}),\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{a}_{M}=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(X^{*}(A_{M}),\mathbb{R}).

Let
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}=\ker(\mathfrak{a}_{M}\to\mathfrak{a}_{G})
. The homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G}\to\mathfrak{a}_{M}
is a section of
\mathfrak{a}_{M}\to\mathfrak{a}_{G}
. Thus, we have a split short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and
\mathfrak{a}_{M}=\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{G}
. We also have the dual exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G})^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

and
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}=(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G})^{*}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
.

We fix a minimal parabolic pair
(P_{0},M_{0})
of
G
. We will often use a subscript or superscript “0” instead of “
M_{0}
” to indicate dependence on
M_{0}
. For example, we write
N_{0}=N_{P_{0}}
and
A_{0}=A_{M_{0}}
. With respect to
M_{0}
, a parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
is called standard (resp. semistandard) if it contains
P_{0}
(resp.
M_{0}
). A Levi subgroup of
G
is said to be semistandard if it contains
M_{0}
. If
P
is a semistandard parabolic, then it has a unique semistandard Levi factor
M_{P}
.

Let
(P,M)
be a parabolic pair. We denote the set of simple roots of
(P,A_{M})
(or equivalently
(N_{P},A_{M})
) by
\Delta(P,A_{M})
, which is a basis of
(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G})^{*}
. There is an associated set of simple coroots
\Delta^{\vee}(P,A_{M})
, which is a basis of
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}
, and there is a bijection
\Delta(P,A_{M})\to\Delta^{\vee}(P,A_{M}),\alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\vee}
. For
(P,M)
semistandard, we write
\Delta_{P}=\Delta(P,A_{M})
and
\Delta_{P}^{\vee}=\Delta^{\vee}(P,A_{M})
. (Cf.
[
MW18
, §1.3]
.) We define the open cone

(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*})^{P,>0}=\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}:\langle\lambda,\alpha^{\vee}\rangle>0,\,\forall\alpha\in\Delta(P,A_{M})\},

which plays a role in the Langlands classification. We denote its closure by
(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*})^{P,\geq 0}
. Similarly, we have the open cone

(\mathfrak{a}_{M})^{P,>0}=\{H\in\mathfrak{a}_{M}:\langle\alpha,H\rangle>0,\,\forall\alpha\in\Delta(P,A_{M})\}

and its closure
(\mathfrak{a}_{M})^{P,\geq 0}
.

2.2.
Maximal compact subgroups

A maximal compact subgroup
K
of
G(F)
is said to be in good position (or admissible) relative to a Levi subgroup
M
of
G
if the following holds.

•

When
F
is archimedean, the Lie algebra of
K
and
A_{M}(F)
are orthogonal with respect to the Killing form of
G
.

•

When
F
is non-archimedean,
K
is the stabiliser of a special vertex in the apartment attached to a maximal split torus of
M
.

We recall from
[
Art81
, §1]
that if
K
is in good position relative to
M
, then

(1)

G(F)=P(F)K
for any
P\in\mathcal{P}(M)
;

(2)

any coset in
G(F)/M(F)
which normalises
M
has a representative in
K
;

(3)

P(F)\cap K=(M_{P}(F)\cap K)(N_{P}(F)\cap K)
for any parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
containing
M
; and

(4)

if
L\in\mathcal{L}(M)
, then
K_{L}\coloneqq K\cap L(F)
is a maximal compact subgroup of
L(F)
that is in good position relative to the Levi subgroup
M
of
L
.

If
K
is in good position relative to
M
, then
K
is in good position relative to every Levi subgroup containing
M
.

We fix a maximal compact subgroup
K
of
G(F)
that is in good position relative to our fixed minimal Levi subgroup
M_{0}
of
G
. In particular, the Iwasawa decomposition
G(F)=P_{0}(F)K
holds.

Let
P
be a semistandard parabolic subgroup of
G
. We can extend
H_{M_{P}}:M_{P}(F)\to\mathfrak{a}_{M_{P}}
to a homomorphism
H_{P}:P(F)\to\mathfrak{a}_{M_{P}}
by composing with
P(F)\to M_{P}(F)
. We have the decompositions
G(F)=P(F)K=M_{P}(F)N_{P}(F)K
. For each
x\in G(F)
we choose elements
p_{P}(x)\in P(F)
and
k_{P}(x)\in K
such that
x=p_{P}(x)k_{P}(x)
, and let
m_{P}(x)\in M_{P}(F)
and
n_{P}(x)\in N_{P}(F)
be the unique elements such that
p_{P}(x)=m_{P}(x)n_{P}(x)
. We extend
H_{P}
to a function
H_{P}:G(F)\to\mathfrak{a}_{M_{P}}
by
H_{P}(x)=H_{P}(p_{P}(x))=H_{M_{P}}(m_{P}(x))
.

Let
\Delta_{0}=\Delta_{P_{0}}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\geq 0}=(\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{P_{0},\geq 0}
, and define
M_{0}(F)^{\geq 0}
to be the set of
m\in M_{0}(F)
such that
H_{0}(m)\in\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{\geq 0}
. Then
G(F)=KM_{0}(F)^{\geq 0}K
. (See
[
MW18
, §1.1]
.)

2.3.
Weyl groups

For
T
a torus of
G
, we make use of the following Weyl groups:

•

the absolute Weyl group
W(G,T)\coloneqq N_{G}(T)/C_{G}(T)
;

•

the relative Weyl group
W_{F}(G,T)\coloneqq N_{G(F)}(T)/C_{G(F)}(T)
; and

•

the stable Weyl group
W(G,T)(F)\coloneqq(N_{G}(T)/C_{G}(T))(F)
.

We have

W_{F}(G,T)\subseteq W(G,T)(F)\subseteq W(G,T).

By Galois descent, we can express the stable Weyl group as

W(G,T)(F)=\{g\in N_{G}(T)\mid g^{-1}\sigma(g)\in C_{G}(T),\,\forall\sigma\in\Gamma_{F}\}/C_{G}(T),

and therefore
W(G,T)(F)
consists of all
w\in W(G,T)
such that

\Int(w):C_{G}(T)\to C_{G}(T)

is defined over
F
.

If
T
is a maximal torus of
G
, then
C_{G}(T)=T
and
W_{F}(G,T)
is equal to
W(G(F),T(F))\coloneqq N_{G(F)}(T(F))/C_{G(F)}(T(F))
. For a Levi subgroup
M
of
G
, we write
W^{G}(M)
for the relative Weyl group
W_{F}(G,A_{M})
. That is,

W^{G}(M)=N_{G(F)}(A_{M})/C_{G(F)}(A_{M})=N_{G(F)}(M)/M(F).

The Weyl group
W^{G}(M_{0})
is the relative Weyl group of
G
, and we abbreviate it by
W_{0}^{G}
. We have

W^{G}(M)=\{\widetilde{w}\in N_{G(F)}(A_{0}):\widetilde{w}\cdot M=M\}/M(F)

and we have a canonical isomorphism

W^{G}(M)=\{w\in W_{0}^{G}:w\cdot M=M\}/W_{0}^{M}.

Fix a
W_{0}^{G}
-invariant inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. This restricts to a
W^{G}(M)
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
for each semistandard Levi subgroup
M
of
G
. Transporting these inner products by conjugation, we obtain a unique
W^{G}(M)
-invariant inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
for each Levi subgroup
M
of
G
. We denote the associated norm by
\|\cdot\|
. The decomposition
\mathfrak{a}_{M}=\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{G}
is orthogonal with respect to the inner product on
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
.

2.4.
Group norms

We recall the notion of norms on affine varieties over local fields from
[
Kot05
, §18]
, which are used to capture polynomial growth and decay. Let
X
be a set. An abstract norm on
X
is a function
\|\cdot\|:X\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
. Let
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
be abstract norms on
X
. We write
\|\cdot\|_{1}\preceq\|\cdot\|_{2}
if there exists
M>0
such that
\|\cdot\|_{1}\ll\|\cdot\|_{2}^{M}
. The abstract norms
\|\cdot\|_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2}
are said to be equivalent and we write
\|\cdot\|_{1}\approx\|\cdot\|_{2}
if
\|\cdot\|_{1}\preceq\|\cdot\|_{2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}\preceq\|\cdot\|_{1}
.

Let
X
be an affine scheme of finite type over
F
. There is a canonical equivalence class of abstract norms on
X(F)
, defined as follows. For any set of generators
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
of the
F
-algebra
\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)
, of regular functions on
X
, we have an abstract norm
\|\cdot\|
on
X(F)
defined by

\|x\|\coloneqq\sup\{1,|f_{1}(x)|_{F},\dots,|f_{m}(x)|_{F}\}.

The equivalence class of
\|\cdot\|
does not depend on the choice of
f_{1},\dots,f_{m}
. We call any abstract norm in the equivalence class of
\|\cdot\|
a norm on
X(F)
. For the definition of norms on
X(F)
when
X
is a non-affine scheme of finite type over
F
, see
[
Kot05
, §18.5]
.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
F
. By
[
Kot05
, Proposition 18.1 (7)]
, if
\Omega
is a bounded (relatively compact) subspace of
G(F)
, for every norm
\|\cdot\|
on
G(F)
there exist
C,M>0
such that
\|\omega_{1}g\omega_{2}\|\leq C\|g\|^{M}
for all
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega
and
g\in G(F)
.

We may construct a norm on
G(F)
as follows. Let
\iota:G\to\GL(V)
be a faithful algebraic representation of
G
on a finite-dimensional vector space
V
over
F
. Choose a vector space norm
\|\cdot\|
on
V
that is compatible with the canonical absolute value
|\cdot|_{F}
on
F
. For example, if
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
is a basis of
V
and
v=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}e_{i}
, we can take
\|v\|=\max_{i=1,\dots,n}\{|a_{i}|_{F}\}
. We equip
\End(V)
with the corresponding operator norm, which we also denote by
\|\cdot\|
. Define
\|g\|=\max(\|\iota(g)\|,\|\iota(g^{-1})\|)
for all
g\in G(F)
. Then
\|\cdot\|:G(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 1}
is a norm on
G(F)
that satisfies the following properties:

(1)

\|\cdot\|
is continuous;

(2)

\|g_{1}g_{2}\|\leq\|g_{1}\|\|g_{2}\|
for all
g_{1},g_{2}\in G(F)
;

(3)

\|g^{-1}\|=\|g\|\geq 1
for all
g\in G(F)
;

(4)

for all
R\geq 0
, the subspace
B_{R}=\{g\in G(F):\|g\|\leq R\}
is compact.

Since
\|\cdot\|
is a norm on
G(F)
, changing the choice of
\iota
or of the vector space norm on
V
results in an equivalent norm on
G(F)
. We call the norms
\|\cdot\|
on
G(F)
that are obtained from the above construction of group norms.

If
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
are bounded subsets of
G(F)
, then
\|\omega_{1}x\omega_{2}\|\asymp\|x\|
for
x\in G(F)
and
\omega_{i}\in\Omega_{i}
. This follows from

\|x\|=\|y_{1}^{-1}y_{1}xy_{2}y_{2}^{-1}\|\leq\|y_{1}^{-1}\|\|y_{1}xy_{2}\|\|y_{2}^{-1}\|

and
\|y_{1}xy_{2}\|\leq\|y_{1}\|\|x\|\|y_{2}\|
for all
x,y_{1},y_{2}\in G(F)
.

Now suppose that
G
is our connected reductive group. We fix a group norm
\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{G}
on
G(F)
and define the non-negative function
\sigma=\sigma_{G}\coloneqq\log\|\cdot\|
. For a closed central subgroup
\mathcal{Z}
of
G(F)
, we define
\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}=\sigma_{G}^{\mathcal{Z}}:G(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by
\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}(g)=\inf_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\sigma(zg)
. We will use this to define growth properties modulo
\mathcal{Z}
. If
\mathcal{Z}
is of the form
\mathcal{Z}=Z(F)
for a subgroup
Z\subseteq Z_{G}
, we will write
\sigma^{Z}=\sigma_{G}^{Z}
instead of
\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}=\sigma_{G}^{\mathcal{Z}}
to simplify the notation.

Recall that we have fixed a
W_{0}^{G}
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

C_{1}(1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|)\leq 1+\sigma(m_{0})\leq C_{2}(1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|)

for all
m_{0}\in M_{0}(F)
. That is,
1+\sigma(m_{0})\asymp 1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|
for
m_{0}\in M_{0}(F)
.

2.5.
Universal enveloping algebras

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a complex reductive Lie algebra. We denote the centre of the universal enveloping algebra
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g})
of
\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})
. We recall that

\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g})^{\ad(\mathfrak{g})}=\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g})^{e^{\ad(\mathfrak{g})}}=\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g})^{\Int(\mathfrak{g})},

where
\Int(\mathfrak{g})
is the group of inner automorphisms of
\mathfrak{g}
, that is, the connected subgroup of
\Aut(\mathfrak{g})
with Lie algebra
\ad(\mathfrak{g})
.

For each Levi subalgebra
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathfrak{g}
there is a canonical Harish-Chandra homomorphism
\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})
. (See Definition 6.3 in
[
Vog87
]
.) The construction of
\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
uses a choice of a parabolic subalgebra
\mathfrak{p}
with Levi factor
\mathfrak{m}
, but
\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
does not depend on this choice. The Harish-Chandra homomorphism is injective and has image
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{m})}
. The isomorphism
\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{m})}
is often called a Harish-Chandra isomorphism. The Harish-Chandra homomorphisms are functorial in the sense that if
\mathfrak{l}\subseteq\mathfrak{m}
are two Levi subalgebras of
\mathfrak{g}
, then
\xi_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathfrak{g}}=\xi_{\mathfrak{l}}^{\mathfrak{m}}\circ\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}
. When
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{t}
is a maximal toral subalgebra, then
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{t})=\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{t})=\Sym(\mathfrak{t})
and the Harish-Chandra isomorphism
\xi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\to\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})}
is the classical Harish-Chandra isomorphism most commonly found in the literature. If
\mathfrak{t}
is contained in
\mathfrak{m}
, then it follows from functoriality of the Harish-Chandra homomorphisms that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{t})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mathfrak{m}}}

That is, if we identify
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})=\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})}
and
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})=\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{t})}
using the Harish-Chandra isomorphisms, then the Harish-Chandra homomorphism
\xi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{m})
is simply the inclusion
\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{t})}\hookrightarrow\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{t})}
.

If
G
is a real Lie group, then
\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\Ad(G)}\subseteq\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
. If
G
belongs to the Harish-Chandra class, in particular, if
G
is the group of
\mathbb{R}
-points of a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{R}
, then
\Ad(G)\subseteq\Int(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
and it follows that
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})=\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})^{\Ad(G)}
.

We will need a “norm” on the set
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{-alg}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}),\mathbb{C})
of infinitesimal characters of
G
. Choose a maximal torus
T
of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
. By the Harish-Chandra isomorphism, we may identify

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{-alg}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}),\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T).

We fix a
W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
-invariant inner product on
\mathfrak{t}^{*}
. Then, for
\mu\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
the resulting norm
\|\mu\|=\|\mu\|_{G}
is well-defined. Different choices of inner products or maximal tori result in a function
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}
on the set of infinitesimal characters of
G
with
\|\cdot\|^{\prime}\asymp\|\cdot\|
. Since we will only use
\|\cdot\|
in estimates, definitions made using
\|\cdot\|
will not depend on the choices made to define it. Note that if
M
is a Levi subgroup of
G
, then
\|\cdot\|_{M}\asymp\|\cdot\|_{G}
.

2.6.
Measures

We normalise the Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d^{+}x
on
F
so that
\vol(\mathcal{O}_{F})=1
if
F
is non-archimedean,
\vol(|x|_{F}\leq 1)=2
if
F\cong\mathbb{R}
, and
\vol(|x|_{F}\leq 1)=\pi
if
F\cong\mathbb{C}
. We use the Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d^{\times}x
on
F^{\times}
defined by
\!\mathop{}d^{\times}x=|x|_{F}^{-1}\!\mathop{}d^{+}x
if
F
is archimedean, and
\!\mathop{}d^{\times}x=(1-q_{F})^{-1}|x|_{F}^{-1}\!\mathop{}d^{+}x
if
F
is non-archimedean, so that
\vol(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})=1
.

Let
T
be any torus over
F
. We define canonical normalisations of the Haar measures on
A_{T}(F)
,
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
,
A_{T}(F)^{1}
, and
T(F)
as follows. Let
l
be the split rank of
T
and choose an isomorphism
A_{T}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{l}
, that is, a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
X^{*}(A_{T})
. This determines isomorphisms
A_{T}(F)\cong(F^{\times})^{l}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{T}\cong\mathbb{R}^{l}
. Transport the product Haar measure on
(F^{\times})^{l}
to
A_{T}(F)
and the usual Haar measure on
\mathbb{R}^{l}
to
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
. The resulting Haar measures on
A_{T}(F)
and
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
do not depend on the choice of isomorphism
A_{T}\cong\mathbb{G}_{m}^{l}
. We use the counting measure for lattices in
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{T}^{*}
. If
F
is archimedean, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F}=\mathfrak{a}_{T}
. If
F
is non-archimedean, then
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F}
is a lattice in
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
; in fact, under our chosen isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{T}\cong\mathbb{R}^{l}
, we have
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F}\cong(\log q_{F}\mathbb{Z})^{l}
and thus
\vol(\mathfrak{a}_{T}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F})=(\log q_{F})^{l}
. In any case, we have fixed a Haar measure on
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F}
. We normalise the Haar measure of the maximal compact subgroup
A_{T}(F)^{1}
of
A_{T}(F)
so that with respect to the short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow A_{T}(F)^{1}\longrightarrow A_{T}(F)\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{T,F}\longrightarrow 0

it is compatible with the normalisations of the Haar measures on
A_{T}(F)
and
\mathfrak{a}_{T}
,
If
F\cong\mathbb{R}
, then
A_{T}(F)^{1}\cong\{\pm 1\}^{l}
and the Haar measure on
A_{T}(F)^{1}
is the counting measure. If
F\cong\mathbb{C}
, then
A_{T}(F)^{1}\cong(S^{1})^{l}
and the Haar measure on
A_{T}(F)^{1}
is normalised so that
\vol(A_{T}(F)^{1})=(2\pi)^{l}
. If
F
is non-archimedean, then
A_{T}(F)^{1}\cong(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})^{l}
and the Haar measure on
A_{T}(F)^{1}
is normalised so that
\vol(A_{T}(F)^{1})=1
, and thus it coincides with the restriction of our Haar measure on
A_{T}(F)
. We normalise the Haar measure on
T(F)
so that
\vol(T(F)/A_{T}(F))=1
.

Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of
G
. Recall that we have fixed an inner product on
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
. We identify
i\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}
with the Pontryagin dual of
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
via
\lambda\mapsto e^{\langle\lambda,\cdot\rangle}
and equip
i\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}
with the Haar measure dual to the fixed Haar measure on
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
.

Let
P
be a semistandard parabolic of
G
. The group
N_{P}(F)
has a canonical Haar measure, which we now describe, following
[
Art91
]
. Let
\delta_{P}
be the modular function of
P
. We have

\delta_{P}(mn)=e^{\langle H_{M}(m),2\rho_{P}\rangle},

for all
m\in M_{P}(F)
and
n\in N_{P}(F)
, where
\rho_{P}
is half the sum of the roots (counted with multiplicity) of
(P,A_{M_{P}})
. Let
\overline{P}
be the parabolic subgroup opposite to
P
with respect to
M_{P}
. For any Haar measure
\nu
on
N_{P}(F)
, the integral

\gamma(P,\nu)=\int_{N_{P}(F)}e^{\langle H_{\overline{P}}(n),2\rho_{\overline{P}}\rangle}\!\mathop{}d{\nu(n)}>0

converges and we have
\gamma(P,c\nu)=c\gamma(P,\nu)
for all
c>0
. Therefore
\gamma(P,\nu)^{-1}\nu
is a canonical Haar measure on
N_{P}(F)
. From now on, we will use the canonical Haar measure on
N_{P}(F)
.

Let
M
be a semistandard Levi subgroup of
G
. The Haar measures on
M(F)
and
G(F)
can be normalised so that for every parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
with Levi factor
M
, we have

\int_{G(F)}f(g)\!\mathop{}d{g}=\int_{N_{P}(F)}\int_{M(F)}\int_{N_{\overline{P}}(F)}f(nm\overline{n})\delta_{P}(m)^{-1}\!\mathop{}d{\overline{n}}\!\mathop{}d{m}\!\mathop{}d{n}.

Following
[
Art91
]
, we say that the Haar measures
\!\mathop{}d{x}
and
\!\mathop{}d{m}
are compatible in this case. Let
L\subseteq M
be a semistandard Levi subgroup of
G
. Then
L
is a semistandard Levi subgroup of
M
. Let
\!\mathop{}d{g}
,
\!\mathop{}d{m}
, and
\!\mathop{}d{l}
be Haar measures on
G(F)
,
M(F)
, and
L(F)
respectively. If any two of the pairs
(\!\mathop{}d{g},\!\mathop{}d{m})
,
(\!\mathop{}d{m},\!\mathop{}d{l})
, and
(\!\mathop{}d{g},\!\mathop{}d{l})
are compatible, then so is the third pair. We say that a collection of Haar measures
\{\mu_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}
on the semistandard Levi subgroups
M(F)
of
G(F)
(including
G(F)
itself) is compatible if each Haar measure
\mu_{M}
is compatible with the Haar measure
\mu_{G}
on
G(F)
, or equivalently
\!\mathop{}d{m}
is compatible with
\!\mathop{}d{l}
whenever
L\subseteq M
. If
\{\mu_{M}\}_{M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}
and
\{\mu_{M}^{\prime}\}_{M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}
are two collections of compatible Haar measures on the semistandard Levi subgroups of
G(F)
, there exists
c>0
such that
\mu_{M}^{\prime}=c\mu_{M}
for all
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
. We choose a collection of compatible Haar measures
\!\mathop{}d{m}
on the semistandard Levi subgroups
M(F)
of
G(F)
. If the minimal Levi
M_{0}
is a torus (necessarily maximal in
G
), then its Haar measure is already fixed and we must choose the Haar measures of the other semistandard Levis compatibly.

For a semistandard parabolic subgroup
P
of
G
, we normalise the left Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d_{l}p
on
P(F)
so that

\int_{P(F)}f(p)\!\mathop{}d_{l}{p}=\int_{M_{P}(F)}\int_{N_{P}(F)}f(mn)\!\mathop{}d{n}\!\mathop{}d{m}.

The corresponding right Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d_{r}p
on
P(F)
is

\displaystyle\int_{P(F)}f(p)\!\mathop{}d_{r}{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{P}(F)}\delta_{P}(m)^{1/2}\int_{N_{P}(F)}f(mn)\!\mathop{}d{n}\!\mathop{}d{m}

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{P}(F)}\int_{N_{P}(F)}f(nm)\!\mathop{}d{n}\!\mathop{}d{m}.

For each semistandard Levi subgroup
M
of
G
, we normalise the Haar measure on the maximal compact subgroup
K_{M}\subseteq M(F)
so that
\vol(K_{M})=1
. In particular,
\vol(K)=1
. We have the integral formulas

\int_{K}f(k)\!\mathop{}d{k}=\int_{N_{\overline{P}}}f(k_{P}(\overline{n}))e^{2\rho_{P}(H_{P}(\overline{n}))}\!\mathop{}d{\overline{n}}

and

\int_{G(F)}f(g)\!\mathop{}d{g}=\int_{K}\int_{P(F)}f(pk)\!\mathop{}d{n}\!\mathop{}d_{l}p,

as in
[
Art91
]
. By conjugation, the Haar measures on semistandard Levi subgroups, parabolic subgroups, and their unipotent radicals can be transported canonically to arbitrary Levi subgroups, parabolic subgroups, and their unipotent radicals.

2.7.
Spaces of functions and distributions

Let
\mathcal{Z}
denote a closed central subgroup of
G(F)
equipped with a choice of Haar measure and let
\zeta:\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character of
\mathcal{Z}
. The pair
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
is called a central character datum or simply a central datum of
G
. We say that a central datum
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
is unitary if
\zeta
is unitary. The trivial central datum is the one with
\mathcal{Z}=1
. Later, we will only use unitary central data and only consider the cases
\mathcal{Z}=1
or
\mathcal{Z}=A_{G}(F)
. We may omit
\mathcal{Z}
and
\zeta
from notation if the central datum
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
is trivial in the sense that
\mathcal{Z}=1
. For a function
f:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
and
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, we define
zf:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
by
zf(g)=f(z^{-1}g)
.

We write
C_{c}(G,\zeta)
for the space of continuous functions on
G(F)
that are
\zeta^{-1}
-equivariant (i.e.
f(zg)=\zeta(z)^{-1}f(g)
, or equivalently
zf=\zeta(z)f
) and whose support is compact modulo
\mathcal{Z}
. The space
C_{c}(G,\zeta)
has a natural topology. For each closed subspace
B
of
G(F)
that is
\mathcal{Z}
-stable and compact modulo
\mathcal{Z}
, we define
C_{B}(G,\zeta)
to be the subspace of functions in
C_{c}(G,\zeta)
whose support is contained in
B
. It is a Banach space with respect to the sup norm. We have
C_{c}(G,\zeta)=\bigcup_{B}C_{B}(G,\zeta)
and give
C_{c}(G,\zeta)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space. The space of
\zeta
-equivariant Radon measures on
G(F)
is
C_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, the continuous dual with the (standard) strong dual topology.

Similarly, we denote by
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
the space of all functions
f:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
that are smooth (i.e. locally constant if
F
is non-archimedean),
\zeta^{-1}
-equivariant, and whose support is compact modulo
\mathcal{Z}
. The space
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
also has a natural topology. Suppose that
F
is archimedean. Let
B
be a closed subspace of
G(F)
that is
\mathcal{Z}
-stable and compact modulo
\mathcal{Z}
. Define
C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
to be the subspace of functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
whose support is contained in
B
. We give
C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
the Fréchet space topology defined by the family of seminorms

\|f\|_{D}\coloneqq\sup_{g\in G(F)}|Df(g)|

for
D
a
\mathcal{Z}
-invariant differential operator on
D
. We have
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)=\bigcup_{B}C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
and give
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space. Now, suppose that
F
is non-archimedean. Then
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
is naturally a countable increasing union of finite-dimensional subspaces, and we equip it with the locally convex limit topology, making it a strict LF-space. The topology on
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
is simply the finest locally convex topology. The space of
\zeta
-equivariant distributions on
G(F)
is
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
. The inclusion
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)\to C_{c}(G,\zeta)
is continuous with dense image, and thus
\zeta
-equivariant Radon measures may be identified with
\zeta
-equivariant distributions.

We will also make use of the subspace
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
of left and right
K
-finite functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
. If
F
is non-archimedean, then
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)=C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
. For
F
archimedean, the space
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
is naturally an inductive limit
[
Art89
]
. Indeed, let
\Gamma\subseteq\widehat{K}
be a finite set of irreducible representations of
K
. We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}
to be the subspace of functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
that transform on each side under
K
according to a finite direct sum of representations in
\Gamma
. For each compact subspace
B
of
G
, we define
C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}=C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)\cap C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}
, which is a closed subspace of
C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
and thus a Fréchet space. We have
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}=\colim_{B}C_{B}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}
and
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)=\colim_{\Gamma}C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)_{\Gamma}
, and we give each the locally convex inductive limit topology, which makes them strict LF-spaces. The inclusion
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)\to C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
is continuous.

The most natural space for harmonic analysis is the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space. Recall that we have fixed a minimal parabolic
P_{0}
of
G
, a Levi factor
M_{0}
of
P_{0}
, and maximal compact subgroup
K
of
G(F)
in good position relative to
M_{0}
. Thus, the Iwasawa decomposition
G(F)=P_{0}(F)K
holds. Let
e:G(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be the unique function satisfying
e(K)=1
and
e(p_{0}g)=\delta_{P_{0}}^{1/2}(p_{0})e(g)
for all
p_{0}\in P_{0}(F)
and
g\in G(F)
. That is,
e
is the unique smooth vector of the parabolically induced representation
I_{M_{0},P_{0}}^{G}(1_{M_{0}})
satisfying
e(K)=1
. Note that
e
is
Z_{G}(F)
-invariant and right
K
-invariant. Although
e
depends on the choice of
P_{0}
and
K
, it does not depend on the choice of
M_{0}
. We define
\Xi=\Xi^{G}:G(F)\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
by

\Xi(g)=\int_{K}e(kg)\!\mathop{}d{k}

for all
g\in G(F)
. Note that
\Xi
is
Z_{G}(F)
-invariant and bi-
K
-invariant. Recall that for
x\in G(F)
, we choose elements
p_{0}(x)=p_{P_{0}}(x)\in P_{0}(F)
and
k_{0}(x)=k_{P_{0}}(x)\in K
such that
x=p_{0}(x)k_{0}(x)
. We have
\Xi(g)=\int_{K}\delta_{P_{0}}^{1/2}(p_{0}(kg))\!\mathop{}d{k}
. Although
\Xi
depends on the choices of
P_{0}
and
K
, different choices result in a Xi-function
\Xi^{\prime}
with
\Xi\asymp\Xi^{\prime}
. Since we will only use
\Xi
in estimates, the choices made in the definition of
\Xi
will have no impact on definitions made in terms of it.

A continuous function
f:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
is said to be rapidly decreasing modulo
\mathcal{Z}
if
|f|
is
\mathcal{Z}
-invariant and it satisfies one of the following equivalent conditions
[
Vig94
, §5]
.

(1)

For all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have

\|f\|_{N}\coloneqq\sup_{g\in G(F)}|f(g)|(1+\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}(g))^{N}\Xi(g)^{-1}<\infty.

(2)

For all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have
f\in L^{2}(G(F)/\mathcal{Z},(1+\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}(g))^{N}\!\mathop{}d{g})
.

We now define the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
for a unitary central datum
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
of
G(F)
.

Suppose that
F
is non-archimedean. The Harish-Chandra Schwartz space
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is the space of uniformly smooth complex-valued functions on
G(F)
that are
\zeta^{-1}
-equivariant and rapidly decreasing modulo
\mathcal{Z}
(cf.
[
Sil79
, §4.4]
and
[
Vig94
, §5]
). Let
K_{0}
be a compact open subgroup of
G(F)
that is sufficiently small so that
\zeta(\mathcal{Z}\cap K_{0})=1
. For each compact open subgroup
K
of
K_{0}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{K}(G,\zeta)
denote the subspace of
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
that are left and right
K
-invariant. Then
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)=\bigcup_{K}\mathcal{C}_{K}(G,\zeta)
. The spaces
\mathcal{C}_{K}(G,\zeta)
are Fréchet spaces when given the topology determined by the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{N}
. The space
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is given the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces and is an LF-space.

Suppose that
F
is archimedean. The Harish-Chandra Schwartz space
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is defined to be the space of all smooth functions
f
on
G(F)
such that all derivatives
ufv
for
u,v\in\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
are rapidly decreasing modulo
\mathcal{Z}
. Given
u,v\in\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
and
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, for each
f\in C^{\infty}(G(F))
, we define

\|f\|_{u,v,N}=\|ufv\|_{N}=\sup_{g\in G(F)}|ufv(g)|(1+\sigma^{Z}(g))^{N}\Xi(g)^{-1}.

Then
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is a Fréchet space when given the topology determined by the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{u,v,N}
. There are other Schwartz spaces of
G(F)
in the literature, but since we will only use the Harish-Chandra Schwartz space we may refer to its elements as Schwartz functions. We will write
\mathcal{C}_{c}(G,\zeta)=C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
and we will write
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
as a shorthand for
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{c}(G,\zeta)
) so that we can make statements about both spaces at the same time.

The other characterisation above of a function being rapidly decreasing modulo
\mathcal{Z}
leads to a different way of defining the topology on the Schwartz space
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. Denote the
L^{2}
-norm on
L^{2}(G(F)/\mathcal{Z},(1+\sigma^{\mathcal{Z}}(g))^{N}\!\mathop{}d{g})
by
\|\cdot\|_{2,N}
. Replacing
\|\cdot\|_{N}
with
\|\cdot\|_{2,N}
in the definition of the topology on
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
given above yields in the same topology.

We define the space of tempered
\zeta
-equivariant distributions to be
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
. Since the inclusion
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is continuous with dense image, we have a continuous injection
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}\to C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
. Thus, we may identify tempered distributions with distributions, and a distribution
\Theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
is tempered if and only if it extends to a continuous linear functional
\Theta:\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
.

For
f:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
, we define the function
f^{\zeta}:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
by

f^{\zeta}(g)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\zeta(z)f(zg)\!\mathop{}d{z},

provided this integral converges absolutely. This defines open continuous surjections
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)\to C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
,
C_{c}(G)\to C_{c}(G,\zeta)
, and
\mathcal{C}(G)\to\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. Given a
\zeta
-equivariant distribution
D\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
on
G(F)
, we may thus define a distribution
D^{\zeta}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)^{\prime}
by pullback:
D^{\zeta}(f)=D(f^{\zeta})
. The distribution
D^{\zeta}
is a Radon measure (resp. tempered distribution) on
G(F)
if
D
is. Moreover, the distribution
D^{\zeta}
is
\zeta
-equivariant in the sense that
D^{\zeta}(zf)=\zeta(z)D^{\zeta}(f)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
. Conversely, a distribution
\widetilde{D}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)^{\prime}
that is
\zeta
-equivariant descends to a distribution
D\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, that is, a
\zeta
-equivariant distribution. In this way, one can identify
\zeta
-equivariant distributions on
G(F)
with distributions on
G(F)
that are
\zeta
-equivariant, and likewise for Radon measures and tempered distributions.

3.
Invariant Harmonic Analysis

3.1.
Invariant distributions

Let
G_{\mathrm{sr}}\subseteq G_{\mathrm{rs}}
denote the open subvarieties of
G
consisting of strongly regular (semisimple) elements and regular semisimple elements, respectively. Let

\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)\subseteq\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\subseteq\Gamma(G)

denote the spaces of conjugacy classes of elements in
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
,
G_{\mathrm{rs}}(F)
, and
G(F)
, respectively. Then
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
are open dense locally compact Hausdorff subspaces of
\Gamma(G)
, and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
is naturally an
F
-analytic manifold.

3.1.1.
Orbital integrals.

Fix a central datum
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
of
G
and let
f\in C_{c}(G,\zeta)
. Let
\gamma\in G_{\mathrm{rs}}(F)
and let
G_{\gamma}=C_{G}(\gamma)^{\circ}
, a maximal torus of
G
. The orbital integral of
f
at
\gamma
is defined by

O_{\gamma}(f)=\int_{G_{\gamma}(F)\backslash G(F)}f(g^{-1}\gamma g)\!\mathop{}d{g}.

The resulting linear functional
O_{\gamma}:C_{c}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
is a
\zeta
-equivariant Radon measure on
G(F)
, it only depends on the conjugacy class
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
, and the map
\gamma\mapsto O_{\gamma}
on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
is
\zeta^{-1}
-equivariant. Moreover, the
\zeta
-equivariant distribution
O_{\gamma}
is tempered.

We write
D^{G}
for the Weyl discriminant of
G
. For
\gamma\in G_{\mathrm{rs}}(F)
, if we write
T=G_{\gamma}
we have

D^{G}(\gamma)=\det(1-\Ad(\gamma)|_{\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{t}}).

We recall the Weyl integration formula

\int_{G(F)}f(g)\!\mathop{}d{g}=\sum_{\{T\}}|W_{F}(G,T)|^{-1}\int_{T(F)}|D^{G}(t)|O_{t}(f)\!\mathop{}d{t},

where
f
is an integrable function on
G(F)
and
\{T\}
runs over the set of
G(F)
-conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
G
. The Weyl integration formula makes it natural to define a Radon measure
\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}
on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
by

\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}\alpha(\gamma)\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}=\sum_{\{T\}}|W_{F}(G,T)|^{-1}\int_{T(F)}\alpha(t)\!\mathop{}d{t}

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G))
. The Weyl integration formula then becomes

\int_{G(F)}f(g)\!\mathop{}d{g}=\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}|D^{G}(\gamma)|O_{\gamma}(f)\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}.

Note that
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
has full measure in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
, we write

f_{G}(\gamma)=|D^{G}(\gamma)|^{1/2}O_{\gamma}(f)

for the normalised orbital integral of
f
on
\gamma
. The function
\gamma\mapsto O_{\gamma}(f)
is continuous on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
and smooth on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
. Thus, the same is true of the function
f_{G}
. Moreover,
f_{G}
is locally bounded. We remark that since
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
is an open dense subset of full measure in
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
, many definitions can be made either using
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
or
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
.

We define the spaces of orbital integrals
\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)
and
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\{f_{G}:\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\to\mathbb{C}:f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)/\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{O_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\})

with their natural quotient topologies. We define the space
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta,K)
of orbital integrals of elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
in an analogous way. This space does not depend on the choice of
K
. For non-archimedean
F
, this is because
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)=C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
. For archimedean
F
, it follows from the fact that all maximal compact subgroups of
G(F)
are
G(F)
-conjugate. Thus, we will write
\mathcal{I}_{f}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta,K)
.

We recall that a maximal torus is said to be elliptic if
T/A_{G}
is anisotropic, or equivalently if
A_{T}=A_{G}
. A semisimple element
\gamma\in G(F)
is said to be elliptic if
\gamma\in T(F)
for some elliptic maximal torus
T
of
G
. We have open subvarieties
G_{\mathrm{sr},\mathrm{ell}}\subseteq G_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}\subseteq G
and we write
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr},\mathrm{ell}}(G)\subseteq\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
for the conjugacy classes of elements in
G_{\mathrm{sr},\mathrm{ell}}(F)
and
G_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(F)
, respectively. A function
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
is said to be cuspidal if
f_{G}(\gamma)=0
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\setminus\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. We denote the subspace of cuspidal functions in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
by
\mathcal{C}_{(c),\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
, and we denote its image in
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
by
\mathcal{I}_{(c),\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
. We denote the subspace of cuspidal functions in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
by
C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
, and its image in
\mathcal{I}_{f}(G,\zeta)
by
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
.

3.1.2.
Invariance.

We refer to elements of
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
) as invariant (
\zeta
-equivariant) distributions (resp. invariant tempered (
\zeta
-equivariant) distributions). Note that we have a continuous linear injection
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)
with dense image. Its transpose is a continuous linear injection
\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}\to\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, which enables us to identify each invariant tempered distribution with an invariant distribution.

We may identify
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
with a subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
via the transpose of the quotient map
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. As vector spaces, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}}(\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{O_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}))

\displaystyle=\cl_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime},\textrm{weak-}*}(\{O_{\gamma}:\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}).

That is, a distribution in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
if and only if it lies in the weak-
*
closure in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
of the linear span of the set of regular semisimple orbital integrals of
G
. A locally integrable function
\Theta
on
G(F)
that is continuous on
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
defines an invariant distribution if and only if
\Theta
is conjugation invariant on
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
.

One can also define the notion of conjugation invariant distributions. For
y\in G(F)
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
, we define
\prescript{y}{}{f}\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
by
\prescript{y}{}{f}(x)=f(y^{-1}xy)
. This defines a left action of
G(F)
on
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. The left action of
G(F)
on
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
in turn leads to a right action of
G(F)
on the associated space of distributions: for all
y\in G(F)
and
u\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, we define
u^{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
by
u^{y}(f)=u(\prescript{y}{}{f})
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)_{G}
denote the quotient of
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
by the smallest closed subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
containing all functions of the form
\prescript{y}{}{f}-f
for
y\in G(F)
and
f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. We define
(\mathcal{C}_{c}(G,\zeta)_{G})^{\prime}
(resp.
(\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)_{G})^{\prime}
) to be the space of conjugation invariant distributions (resp. tempered distributions). We have a natural injection
(\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)_{G})^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, and its image is the subspace of distributions in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
such that
u^{y}=u
.

Every orbital integral is conjugation invariant. Thus, if
f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
is annihilated by all conjugation invariant functions, then it is annihilated by all orbital integrals. It follows that the quotient
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
descends to a quotient
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)_{G}\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. It is known that for
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(G)
, if
f
is annihilated by all regular semisimple orbital integrals, then
f
is annihilated by all conjugation invariant distributions. This was proved for real groups by Bouaziz
[
Bou94
, Theorem 3.2.1]
and for
p
-adic groups by Harish-Chandra
[
HC99
, Theorem 10]
. Thus,
\mathcal{C}_{c}(G)_{G}=\mathcal{I}_{c}(G)
, and conjugation invariant distributions are the same as invariant distributions.

3.1.3.
Representations and characters.

Let
\Pi(G)
denote the set of equivalence classes of irreducible admissible representations of
G(F)
. We denote the central character of
\pi\in\Pi(G)
by
\zeta_{\pi}
. Let
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
be a central datum of
G(F)
. We denote by
\Pi(G,\zeta)
the set of equivalence classes of irreducible admissible representations
\pi
of
G(F)
with
\mathcal{Z}
-character
\zeta_{\pi}|_{\mathcal{Z}}=\zeta
. We have the subsets

\Pi_{u}(G,\zeta)\supseteq\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\supseteq\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)

of equivalence classes of irreducible unitary, tempered, and (relatively) square-integrable (or discrete series) representations, respectively. These are only non-empty if
\zeta
is unitary. We denote the space of virtual representations of
G(F)
with
\mathcal{Z}
-character
\zeta
by
D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Pi(G,\zeta)
, and we denote the subspace of virtual tempered representations by
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
.

Let
\pi
be a finite-length admissible representation of
G(F)
with
\mathcal{Z}
-character
\zeta
. For
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
, one can form the operator

\pi(f)=\int_{G(F)/\mathcal{Z}}f(g)\pi(g)\!\mathop{}d{g},

which has a well-defined trace. The resulting
\zeta
-equivariant distribution
\Theta_{\pi}=\tr\pi:C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
is the character of
\pi
. If
\pi
is tempered (and
\zeta
is unitary), then
\pi(f)
is well-defined for
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
and
\Theta_{\pi}
is a tempered
\zeta
-equivariant distribution.

We identify a virtual representation
\pi=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}\pi_{i}\in D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G,\zeta)
with its (virtual) character
\Theta_{\pi}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}\Theta_{\pi_{i}}
. Thus, the space
D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G,\zeta)
is identified with a subspace of the space of
\zeta
-equivariant distributions. Harish-Chandra’s regularity theorem tells us that
\Theta_{\pi}
is a smooth—even analytic if
F
is archimedean—conjugation invariant
\zeta
-equivariant function on
G_{\mathrm{rs}}(F)
; that
\Theta_{\pi}\in L_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}(G(F))
; and that the normalised character
|D^{G}(x)|^{1/2}\Theta_{\pi}(x)
is locally bounded on
G(F)
.

A simple consequence of the Harish-Chandra regularity theorem and the Weyl integration formula is the following. Let
\pi\in\Pi(G,\zeta)
(resp.
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
). If
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
), with
f_{G}(\gamma)=0
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
, then
f_{G}(\pi)=0
. Therefore
\Theta_{\pi}\in\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
(resp.
\Theta_{\pi}\in\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
). Consequently, we have
D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G,\zeta)\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
and
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\subseteq\mathcal{I}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
.

3.1.4.
Parabolic descent.

Let
(P,M)
be a semi-standard parabolic pair. For
f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
(assuming
\zeta
is unitary in the case of Schwartz functions), one defines
f^{(P)}:M(F)\to\mathbb{C}
by

f^{(P)}(m)=\delta_{P}(m)^{1/2}\int_{N_{P}(F)}\int_{K}f(k^{-1}mnk)\!\mathop{}d{n}\!\mathop{}d{k}.

Then
f^{(P)}\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)
. Note that
f^{(P)}
depends on the choice of
K
. This results in a continuous linear operator
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)
called parabolic descent. If
\sigma
is a finite-length admissible representation of
M(F)
with
\mathcal{Z}
-character
\zeta
, then

\langle\Theta_{\sigma},f^{(P)}\rangle=\langle\Theta_{I_{M,P}^{G}\sigma},f\rangle

for all
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
. If
\sigma
is tempered, then this holds for all
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. The parabolic descent map
f\mapsto f^{(P)}
descends to a continuous map

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{W^{G}(M)}

\displaystyle f_{G}

\displaystyle\longmapsto f_{M}

which we also call parabolic descent. Let
\Gamma_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{rs}}(M)
denote the set of
G
-regular semisimple conjugacy classes in
M(F)
. For all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{rs}}(M)
we have
f_{M}(\gamma)=f_{G}(\gamma)
. Consequently, although
f^{(P)}
depends on the choice of
P
and
K
, the function
f_{M}
does not. For
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
, we have that
f
is cuspidal if and only if
f_{M}=0
for all
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
with
M\neq G
.

The transpose of parabolic descent
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{W^{G}(M)}
is a continuous linear map
I_{M}^{G}:\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{\prime}/W^{G}(M)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, which we call parabolic induction since it extends the parabolic induction of characters. If
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
, then every invariant distribution that is parabolically induced from a proper Levi of
G
annihilates
f
.

3.1.5.
The invariant Fourier transform.

Let
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
be a unitary central datum of
G
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. The operator-valued Fourier transform of
f
is the section of
\coprod_{\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)}\End(V_{\pi})\to\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
defined by
\pi\mapsto\pi(f)
. It is the subject of harmonic analysis on
G(F)
, upon which invariant harmonic analysis is built. Invariant harmonic analysis is the study of the invariant (or scalar-valued) Fourier transform of
f
, which is the function
f_{G}:\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
f_{G}(\pi)=\Theta_{\pi}(f)
.

We have the space of invariant Fourier transforms

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\{f_{G}:f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)/\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{\Theta_{\pi}:\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\})

with its natural quotient topology. The space
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c,f}}(G,\zeta)=\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c}}(G,\zeta,K)
of invariant Fourier transforms of elements of
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
is defined in an analogous way, and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c,f}}(G,\zeta)
does not depend on the choice of
K
. If
F
is non-archimedean, we have
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c,f}}(G,\zeta)=\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c}}(G,\zeta)
.

The invariant Fourier transform

\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)

is a surjective linear map by definition of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. Since tempered characters are invariant, it descends to a continuous surjective linear map

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta).

This restricts to a continuous surjective linear map

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{I}_{c,f}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c,f}}(G,\zeta).

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. The invariant Fourier transform
f_{G}:\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
of
f
has a unique extension to a
\mathbb{C}
-linear form
f_{G}:D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
on the space of virtual tempered representations
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
. The invariant Fourier transform is just the representation of this linear form
f_{G}
with respect to the basis
\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
of
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
. In
[
Art93
]
, Arthur defines a set of virtual tempered representations
\{\pi_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)}
parametrised by a set
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
of
W_{0}^{G}
-orbits of equivalence classes of certain triples. These virtual representations arise naturally in the theory of the
R
-group. There is a natural action of
\mathbb{C}^{1}
on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
, and the assignment
\tau\mapsto\pi_{\tau}
is equivariant with respect to the action. That is,
\pi_{z\tau}=z\pi_{\tau}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
and
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
. A choice of representative
\tau
for each
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-orbit
\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau
in
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
gives a basis
\{\pi_{\tau}\}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}}
of
\mathbb{C}\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
. For some purposes, the virtual tempered representations
\{\pi_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)}
are more natural than
\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
and can be regarded as the spectral objects that play the role dual to conjugacy classes in invariant harmonic analysis (cf.
[
Art94a
]
). It is useful to view the Fourier transform of
f
as the
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-equivariant function
f_{G}:T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
f_{G}(\tau)=f_{G}(\pi_{\tau})
. This representation of the Fourier transform allows for more natural formulations of Paley–Wiener theorems. In the following subsections, we recall the theory of the
R
-group, Arthur’s virtual tempered representations, and the invariant Paley–Wiener theorems that we will stabilise in the next section.

3.2.
R
-groups

Let
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
and let
\sigma
be a Hilbert space representation of
M(F)
. One can form the normalised parabolically induced representation Hilbert space representation
I_{P}(\sigma)=I_{P}^{G}(\sigma)=I_{M,P}^{G}(\sigma)
, defined to be the space of equivalence classes of measurable functions
f:G(F)\to V_{\sigma}
such that
f(pg)=\sigma(p)\delta_{P}(p)^{1/2}f(g)
for all
p\in P(F)
and
g\in G(F)
and

\int_{K}\|f(k)\|^{2}\!\mathop{}d{k}<\infty.

By restricting the functions in
I_{P}^{G}(\sigma)
to
K
, we obtain the so-called compact model of
I_{P}(\sigma)
, which is used in the construction of the standard intertwining operators.

Let
\sigma\in\Pi_{2}(M)
. Then
I_{P}(\sigma)
decomposes as a finite direct sum of irreducible tempered representations of
G(F)
and the theory of the
R
-group introduced by Knapp and Stein gives a parametrisation of the irreducible summands of
I_{P}(\sigma)
. We will follow the presentation in
[
MW18
, §1.9–1.11]
with some minor modifications.

For any
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(M)
, we have a normalised standard intertwining operator

R_{Q|P}(\sigma):I_{P}(\sigma)\longrightarrow I_{Q}(\sigma),

which is defined by meromorphically continuing the standard intertwining operator defined by a certain integral, and then renormalising it. The renormalisation involves a choice of a scalar normalising factor, which is a meromorphic function

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{M,\mathbb{C}}^{*}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\longmapsto r_{Q|P}(\sigma_{\lambda})

They can be chosen to satisfy several conditions, including the conditions (R1)–(R8) of
[
Art94a
, Theorem 2.1]
.

We write
\widetilde{w}\in N_{G(F)}(M)
for an element with image
w\in W^{G}(M)
. We have a unitary intertwining operator

\widetilde{w}:I_{P}(\sigma)\longrightarrow I_{w\cdot P}(\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma)

defined by
\phi\mapsto\phi(\widetilde{w}^{-1}\cdot)
. Let
N_{G(F)}(M)_{\sigma}=\{\widetilde{w}\in N_{G(F)}(M):\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma\cong\sigma\}
. Suppose that
\widetilde{w}\in N_{{G(F)}}(M)_{\sigma}
. For each unitary intertwining operator
A:\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma\to\sigma
, we have a unitary intertwining operator

I_{w\cdot P}(A):I_{w\cdot P}(\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma)\longrightarrow I_{w\cdot P}(\sigma)

since
I_{w\cdot P}
is a functor.

We define the unitary intertwining operator
R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w}):I_{P}(\sigma)\to I_{P}(\sigma)
to be the composition
R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w})=R_{P|w\cdot P}(\sigma)\circ I_{w\cdot P}(A)\circ\widetilde{w}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w}):I_{P}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{w\cdot P}(\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{w\cdot P}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P}(\sigma).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{w\cdot P}(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P|w\cdot P}(\sigma)}

Define
\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
to be the set of pairs
(A,\widetilde{w})
where
\widetilde{w}\in N_{G(F)}(M)_{\sigma}
and
A:\widetilde{w}\cdot\sigma\to\sigma
is a unitary intertwining operator. The set
\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
is naturally a group with multiplication defined by
(A_{1},\widetilde{w_{1}})(A_{2},\widetilde{w_{2}})=(A_{1}A_{2},\widetilde{w_{1}}\widetilde{w_{2}})
. The map
M(F)\to\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma),m\mapsto(\sigma(m),m)
is an injective homomorphism with normal image, and we denote the resulting quotient group by
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)=\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)/M(F)
. Note that for each
(A,\widetilde{w})\in\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
, the intertwining operator
I_{w\cdot P}(A)\circ\widetilde{w}:I_{P}(\sigma)\to I_{w\cdot P}(\sigma)
, and thus also
R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w})
, only depends on the image of
(A,\widetilde{w})
in
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
. The map
R_{P}
from
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
to the group of unitary intertwining operators on
I_{P}(\sigma)
is a homomorphism.

For each
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
, we have that
(z,1)
lies in the centre of
\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
. Note that the maps
\mathbb{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
and
\mathbb{C}^{1}\to\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
are injective homomorphisms. Let

\displaystyle W^{G}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{w\in W^{G}(M):w\cdot\sigma\cong\sigma\}

\displaystyle\cong\{w\in W_{0}^{G}:w\cdot M=M,w\cdot\sigma\cong\sigma\}/W_{0}^{M}.

We have a canonical surjective homomorphism
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)\to W^{G}(\sigma)
with kernel
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. That is, we have the central extension

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)\longrightarrow W^{G}(\sigma)\longrightarrow 1.

Consequently,
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
is naturally a compact group. Moreover,
R_{P}
is a (continuous) unitary representation of
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
on
I_{P}(V_{\sigma})
through which
\mathbb{C}^{1}
acts by multiplication.

The definition of
R_{P}
depends in a simple manner on the choice of normalising factors and the parabolic subgroup
P\in\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
. Consider a different choice of normalising factors and the resulting representation
\underline{R}_{P}
of
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
. There exists a unitary character
\chi
of
W^{G}(\sigma)
such that for all
(A,\widetilde{w})\in\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
we have
\underline{R}_{P}(A,\widetilde{w})=\chi(A,\widetilde{w})R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w})
. We can define an automorphism
\alpha_{\chi}
of
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
by
\alpha_{\chi}(A,\widetilde{w})=(\chi(A,\widetilde{w})A,\widetilde{w})
. Then
\underline{R}_{P}=R_{P}\circ\alpha_{\chi}
. If
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
is another choice of parabolic, then
R_{P|P^{\prime}}(\sigma)\circ R_{P^{\prime}}(A,\widetilde{w})=R_{P}(A,\widetilde{w})\circ R_{P|P^{\prime}}(\sigma)
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
, so
R_{P^{\prime}}
is unitarily equivalent to
R_{P}
. Consequently, the kernel
W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}
of
R_{P}
is independent of the choice of normalising factors and parabolic
P\in\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
.

The subgroup
W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}
of
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
injects into
W^{G}(\sigma)
, and we also denote its image by
W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}
. We define
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)=\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)/W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}
. The Knapp–Stein
R
-group of
\sigma
is defined to be
R^{G}(\sigma)=W^{G}(\sigma)/W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}
. We thus have a central extension

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\longrightarrow R^{G}(\sigma)\longrightarrow 1.

Since
\mathbb{C}^{1}
acts by multiplication through
R_{P}
, the representation
R_{P}
of
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)
descends to a representation
R_{P}
of
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)
through which
\mathbb{C}^{1}
acts by multiplication.

We denote by
\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})
the set of irreducible representations of
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)
through which
\mathbb{C}^{1}
acts by multiplication. Let
\mathcal{R}_{P}
denote the representation of
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\times G(F)
on
I_{P}(V_{\sigma})
defined by
\mathcal{R}_{P}(\widetilde{r},x)=R_{P}(\widetilde{r})I_{P}(\sigma,x)
.

Harish-Chandra’s commuting algebra theorem (
[
HC76
, Theorem 38.1]
for real groups and
[
Sil79
, Theorem 5.5.3.2]
for
p
-adic groups) states that the operators
\{R_{P}(\widetilde{r}):\widetilde{r}\in\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\}
span
\End(I_{P}(\sigma))
. The dimension theorem (due to Knapp–Stein for real groups
[
KS75
, Theorem 2]
,
[
KS80
, Theorem 13.4]
and Silberger for
p
-adic groups
[
Sil78
]
) states that the dimension of
\End(I_{P}(\sigma))
is the cardinality of
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)/\mathbb{C}^{1}=R^{G}(\sigma)
, and thus

\End(I_{P}(\sigma))=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{C}^{1}\widetilde{r}\in\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)/\mathbb{C}^{1}}\mathbb{C}R_{P}(\widetilde{r}).

As a corollary, one obtains the main theorem of the theory of
R
-groups, namely that that there is a bijection
\rho\mapsto\pi_{\rho}
between
\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})
and the set
\Pi_{\sigma}(G)
of irreducible summands of
I_{P}^{G}(\sigma)
characterised by the decomposition

\mathcal{R}\cong\bigoplus_{\rho\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})}\rho\boxtimes\pi_{\rho}.

If
P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
, then
R_{P|P^{\prime}}(\sigma)
intertwines the representation
\mathcal{R}_{P^{\prime}}
with
\mathcal{R}_{P}
, so the bijection
\rho\mapsto\pi_{\rho}
is independent of the choice of
P\in\mathcal{P}^{G}(M)
. Consider a different choice of normalising factors and the resulting representation
\underline{R}_{P}
, which is of the form
\underline{R}_{P}=\chi R_{P}
for a unitary character
\chi
of
W^{G}(\sigma)
as above. The resulting representation
\underline{\mathcal{R}}_{P}\coloneqq\underline{R}_{P}I_{P}(\sigma)=\chi\mathcal{R}_{P}
of
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\times G(F)
decomposes as

\bigoplus_{\rho\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})}\chi\rho\boxtimes\pi_{\rho},

and therefore determines the bijection
\rho\mapsto\pi_{\chi^{-1}\rho}
.

The group
\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
and the related objects depend on the representation
\sigma
itself, not just its isomorphism class. This point is often glossed over in the literature. However, suppose that
T:\sigma\to\sigma^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of unitary representations. Then we obtain an isomorphism

\displaystyle T:\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)
