\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})

\displaystyle(A,\widetilde{w})

\displaystyle\longmapsto(T\circ A\circ T^{-1},\widetilde{w})

that induces isomorphisms
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})
,
W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}\to W^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})_{0}
,
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})
, and
R^{G}(\sigma)\to R^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})
, which we also denote by
T
. Moreover, we have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut(I_{P}(V_{\sigma}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Aut(I_{P}(V_{\sigma^{\prime}}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{P}(T)}

3.3.
Arthur’s virtual tempered representations

Consider the set of triples
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
, where
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
,
\sigma
is a discrete series representation of
M(F)
, and
\widetilde{r}\in\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)
. We define two triples
(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
and
(M,\sigma^{\prime},\widetilde{r}^{\prime})
to be equivalent if there exists a unitary isomorphism
T:\sigma\to\sigma^{\prime}
such that
T(\widetilde{r})=\widetilde{r}^{\prime}
.

Let
g\in N_{G(F)}(M_{0})
and consider
g\cdot M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
and
g\cdot\sigma
. We have an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{G}(g\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle(A,\widetilde{w})

\displaystyle\longmapsto g\cdot(A,\widetilde{w})=(A,g\widetilde{w}g^{-1})

and this determines isomorphisms
\mathcal{W}^{G}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{W}^{G}(g\cdot\sigma)
,
W^{G}(\sigma)\to W^{G}(g\cdot\sigma)
,
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}^{G}(g\cdot\sigma)
, and
R^{G}(\sigma)\to R^{G}(g\cdot\sigma)
. Thus, the group
N_{G(F)}(M_{0})
acts on the set of triples
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
by
g\cdot\tau=(g\cdot M,g\cdot\sigma,g\cdot\widetilde{r})
. This descends to an action on the set of equivalence classes of triples, and
M_{0}(F)
acts trivially. Thus, we obtain an action of
W_{0}^{G}
on the set of equivalence classes of triples.

Let
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
be a triple and recall the representation
\mathcal{R}
from the previous subsection, with its decomposition

\mathcal{R}\cong\bigoplus_{\rho\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})}\rho\boxtimes\pi_{\rho}.

Define the virtual tempered representation

\pi_{\tau}=\sum_{\rho\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})}\tr(\rho(\widetilde{r}))\pi_{\rho}.

The invariant tempered distribution character of
\pi_{\tau}
is given by

\Theta_{\tau}(f)=\tr(R_{P}(\widetilde{r})I_{P}(f))=\sum_{\rho\in\Pi(\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}^{1}})}\tr(\rho(\widetilde{r}))\Theta_{\pi_{\rho}}(f),\quad f\in\mathcal{C}(G).

The irreducible tempered representations
\pi_{\rho}
all have the central character
\zeta_{\sigma}
. We define
\zeta_{\tau}=\zeta_{\sigma}
and call it the central character of
\tau
. In particular,
\tau
has a well-defined
\mathcal{Z}
-character, namely the restriction
\zeta_{\tau}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
, and
\pi_{\tau}\in D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta_{\tau}|_{\mathcal{Z}})
.

In the archimedean case, the representation
I_{P}(\sigma)
has an infinitesimal character obtained from
\mu_{\sigma}
in the manner described in our review of parabolic induction. Thus, the irreducible tempered representations
\pi_{\rho}
all have the same infinitesimal character, which we call the infinitesimal character of
\tau
and denote by
\mu_{\tau}
.

The virtual tempered representation
\pi_{\tau}
only depends on the
W_{0}^{G}
-orbit of the equivalence class of
\tau
. Moreover,
\pi_{\tau}
does not depend on the choice of parabolic
P\in\mathcal{P}(M)
, but it does depend on the choice of normalising factors. A different choice of normalising factors results in the virtual tempered representation
\chi(\widetilde{r})\pi_{\tau}
for some unitary character
\chi
of
W^{G}(\sigma)
. Thus,
\pi_{\tau}
is uniquely determined up to multiplication by an element of
\mathbb{C}^{1}
.

The group
\mathbb{C}^{1}
acts on the set of triples by
z(M,\sigma,z\widetilde{r})=(M,\sigma,z\widetilde{r})
, and this descends to an action on equivalence classes that commutes with the action of
W_{0}^{G}
. The map
\tau\mapsto\pi_{\tau}
is equivariant with respect to the action of
\mathbb{C}^{1}
action:
\pi_{z\tau}=z\pi_{\tau}
.

Consider an equivalence class of triples
\tau
. It can happen that there exists a non-trivial
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
such that
z\tau=w\cdot\tau
for some
w\in W_{0}^{G}
. When this happens, we have
z\pi_{\tau}=\pi_{z\tau}=\pi_{\tau}
, and thus
\pi_{\tau}=0
. One says that
\tau
is essential if this does not happen. The set of essential equivalence classes of triples is stable under the actions of
W_{0}^{G}
and
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. We define
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
to be the set of essential equivalence classes of triples
(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
with
\mathcal{Z}
-character
\zeta
, and we define
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)/W_{0}^{G}
. When
\mathcal{Z}
is trivial, we omit
\zeta
from the notation. Arthur proved the following (cf.
[
MW18
, Proposition 2.9]
).

Proposition 3.1
.

For each
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
, we have
\pi_{\tau}\neq 0
, and the space of virtual tempered representations of
G(F)
is

D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}}\mathbb{C}\pi_{\tau}.

In particular, the
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-equivariant map
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta),\tau\mapsto\pi_{\tau}
is injective. We will identify
\tau
with
\pi_{\tau}
, and thus also
\Theta_{\tau}
.

3.3.1.
Elliptic virtual tempered representations.

Suppose that
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
. We can relate
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)
and
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
for
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
in the following way. If
M\in\mathcal{L}^{L}(M_{0})
and
\sigma
is a discrete series representation of
M(F)
, then there is a natural injective homomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{L}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)
. This gives rise to an embedding

\iota_{L}^{G}:\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)\longrightarrow\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)

that is equivariant with respect to the action of
\mathbb{C}^{1}
and the action of
W_{0}^{L}\subseteq W_{0}^{G}
.
This map descends to a
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-equivariant map

\iota_{L}^{G}:T_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)\longrightarrow T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta).

The group
W^{G}(L)
acts on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)
and this map is the quotient for this action. This map is compatible with parabolic induction in the following sense. If
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)
, then

I_{L}^{G}(\pi_{\tau})=\pi_{\iota_{L}^{G}(\tau)}.

We say that
\tau\in\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
is elliptic if it does not lie in the image of
\iota_{L}^{G}:\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(L,\zeta)\to\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
for any
L\neq G
. Let
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
denote the set of elliptic elements in
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
and let
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)/W_{0}^{G}
. We can also define elliptic triples as follows. Let
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})\in\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
and let
r\in R^{G}(\sigma)
be the image of
\widetilde{r}
in
R^{G}(\sigma)
. We define
W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{G}(\sigma)
to be the set of
w\in W^{G}(\sigma)
such that the space
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{w}
of
w
-invariants in
\mathfrak{a}_{M}
is equal to
\mathfrak{a}_{G}
. Then
\tau
is elliptic if and only if
W^{G}(\sigma)_{0}=1
(in which case
R^{G}(\sigma)=W^{G}(\sigma)
, so
r\in W^{G}(\sigma)
) and
r\in W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{G}(\sigma)
(i.e.
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{r}=\mathfrak{a}_{G}
). We have an injection
\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)\to T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
defined by
\pi\mapsto(G,\pi,1)
, and we identify
\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)
with its image in
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
.

We have

\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\coprod_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L,\zeta)

and

T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\Bigg(\coprod_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L,\zeta)\Bigg)^{W_{0}^{G}}=\coprod_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L,\zeta)/W^{G}(L).

We define
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
to be the subspace of
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
generated by
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, that is,

D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}}\mathbb{C}\tau.

3.3.2.
The space of Arthur’s virtual tempered representations.

For
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
, the map

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}^{G}(\sigma_{\lambda})

\displaystyle(A,n)

\displaystyle\longmapsto(Ae^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(n)\rangle},n)

is an isomorphism. It descends to an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}^{G}(\sigma_{\lambda}),\widetilde{r}\mapsto\widetilde{r}_{\lambda}
compatible with
R(\sigma)=R(\sigma_{\lambda})
. We obtain an action of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
on the set of triples
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})
defined by

(\lambda,\tau)\longmapsto\tau_{\lambda}=(M,\sigma_{\lambda},\widetilde{r}_{\lambda}),

for all
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
. The set
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
of equivalence classes of triples and
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
are stable under the action of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
. Moreover, for
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
and
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
, we have
\pi_{\tau_{\lambda}}=(\pi_{\tau})_{\lambda}
.

We have encountered three actions on
\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, namely the actions of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
,
W_{0}^{G}
, and
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Let us summarise these here. If
\tau=[(M,\sigma,(A,n))]\in\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
,
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
,
w\in W_{0}^{G}
, and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
, then

\displaystyle\tau_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=[(M,|\cdot|_{M}^{\lambda}\sigma,(|n|_{G}^{\lambda}A,n))]

\displaystyle w\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=[(wMw^{-1},\sigma\circ\Int(w^{-1}),(A,wnw^{-1}))]

\displaystyle z\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=[(M,\sigma,(zA,n))].

Any two of the above three actions commute with each other. We thus have commuting actions of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
and
\mathbb{C}^{1}
on
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. The action of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
on
\Pi(G)
extends to a linear action on
D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G)
, and
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
preserves
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
and
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. The injection
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\to D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
is equivariant with respect to the actions of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
and
\mathbb{C}^{1}
.

We write
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
for the contracted product

T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}=T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\times^{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}}\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*},

the quotient of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\times\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
by the relation
(\tau_{\lambda_{0}},\lambda)\sim(\tau,\lambda+\lambda_{0})
, where
\lambda_{0}\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
. The injection
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\to D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
extends to an injection
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}\to D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G)
that is equivariant with respect to the actions of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and
\mathbb{C}^{1}
.

Let
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}
. We denote the isotropy subgroup of
\tau
in
\mathfrak{a}_{L,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
by
\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}
. We have

\mathfrak{a}_{L,F}^{\vee}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{L,F}^{\vee}\,.

Thus, if
F
is archimedean we have
\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}=0
, and if
F
is non-archimedean we have that
\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}
is a full lattice in
i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}
. The set
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
is naturally a smooth manifold with uncountably many connected components
i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}\cdot\tau=i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}
, which are Euclidean spaces if
F
is archimedean and compact tori if
F
is non-archimedean. The manifold
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
has uncountably many components because of the action of
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Moreover,
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
with connected components
\mathfrak{a}_{L,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\tau=\mathfrak{a}_{L,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}
. Since

T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)=\coprod_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/W^{G}(L),

we have that
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
is naturally a topological space. We say that a function on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
is smooth if its pullback to each
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
is smooth.

3.3.3.
The elliptic inner product.

We recall the elliptic inner product from
[
MW18
, 7.3]
. Consider the restriction of our Radon measure
\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}
on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
to the open subspace
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. This Radon measure and our Haar measure on
A_{G}(F)
determine a Radon measure on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)
. Identifying
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G/A_{G})
, this Radon measure is the restriction of the Radon measure on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G/A_{G})
determined by our choices of measures.

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, define
m(\gamma)=\vol(G_{\gamma}(F)/A_{G}(F))
. We have a canonical Radon measure
\!\mathop{}d_{\mathrm{ell}}{\gamma}
on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)
defined as follows. For
\alpha\in C_{c}(\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F))
, we define

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)}\alpha(\gamma)\!\mathop{}d_{\mathrm{ell}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)}\alpha(\gamma)m(\gamma)^{-1}\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{T\}}|W_{F}(G,T)|^{-1}\vol(T(F)/A_{G}(F))^{-1}\int_{T(F)/A_{G}(F)}\alpha(t)\!\mathop{}d{t},

where
\{T\}
runs over the conjugacy classes of elliptic maximal tori of
G
. This integral does not depend on any choice of measure.

Let
\zeta
be a unitary character of
A_{G}(F)
. For
\zeta
-equivariant functions
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}:\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)\to\mathbb{C}
, we define

\langle\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)/A_{G}(F)}\alpha(\gamma)\overline{\alpha^{\prime}(\gamma)}\!\mathop{}d_{\mathrm{ell}}{\gamma},

provided the integral converges absolutely. For
\pi,\pi^{\prime}\in D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, we define

\langle\pi,\pi^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\langle|D^{G}|^{1/2}\Theta_{\pi},|D^{G}|^{1/2}\Theta_{\pi^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}.

For
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, one defines
\iota(\tau)=\big|\det(1-\widetilde{r})|_{\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{G}}\big|^{-1}
. We have the following orthogonality relations due to Arthur
[
MW18
, Theorem 7.3]
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\tau,\tau^{\prime}\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. We have

\langle\tau,\tau^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=|W^{G}(\tau)|\iota(\tau)^{-1}\delta_{\tau,\tau^{\prime}}.

Thus,
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}
is an inner product on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
and the decomposition

D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}}\mathbb{C}\tau

is an orthogonal direct sum. It follows from the above theorem that
\{\|\tau\|_{\mathrm{ell}}:\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\}
is bounded. Note that when
\tau=\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
, that is,
\tau=(G,\pi,1)
, we have
\|\pi\|_{\mathrm{ell}}=1
.

We have
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\bigoplus_{\zeta}D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Thus, we obtain an inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}
on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
as the orthogonal direct sum of the inner products on the summands. This inner product is called the elliptic inner product.

3.4.
Paley–Wiener and Schwartz spaces

In this subsection we define abstract Paley–Wiener spaces and Schwartz spaces of the sort needed for the invariant and stable harmonic analysis. See
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV]
for a similar presentation of abstract Paley–Wiener spaces suitable for invariant and stable harmonic analysis on a real group.

Let
V
be a Euclidean space and let
\Lambda=\{\Lambda_{e}\}_{e\in E}
be a countable family of one of the following types:

(1)

archimedean type: for each
e\in E
,
\Lambda_{e}=iV^{*}
equipped with a non-negative real number
\|e\|
.

(2)

non-archimedean type: for each
e\in E
,
\Lambda_{e}=iV^{*}/\Gamma_{e}^{\vee}
, where
\Gamma_{e}
is a lattice in
V
and
\Gamma_{e}^{\vee}=\Hom(\Gamma_{e},2\pi i\mathbb{Z})
. Thus,
\Lambda_{e}
is a compact torus of dimension
\dim V
.

The terminology comes from the types of spaces that appear in Paley–Wiener theorems for
G
when
F
is archimedean or non-archimedean. We will also use
\Lambda
to denote the disjoint union
\Lambda=\coprod_{e\in E}\Lambda_{e}
. The function spaces we define will be spaces of certain smooth functions on
\Lambda
.

Note that for each
e\in E
, the space
\Lambda_{e}
has a natural complexification
\Lambda_{e,\mathbb{C}}
, which is
V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
in the archimedean case and
V_{\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\Gamma_{e}^{\vee}
in the non-archimedean case. Thus, the space
\Lambda
has a natural complexification
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}=\coprod_{e\in E}\Lambda_{e,\mathbb{C}}
. For each
e\in E
, we extend the inner product on
V
to a Hermitian inner product on
V_{\mathbb{C}}
. Note that a function
\varphi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
can be identified with a family of functions
\{\varphi_{e}\}_{e\in E}
.

We define the Paley–Wiener space
PW(\Lambda)
on
\Lambda
to be the vector space of smooth functions
\varphi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
such that the following hold.

(1)

In the non-archimedean case we require that
\varphi
is supported on finitely many connected components
\Lambda_{e}
.

(2)

\varphi
extends to an entire function on
\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}
.

(3)

\varphi
satisfies a growth condition:

(a)

in the archimedean case we require that there exists
r>0
such that for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we have

\|\varphi\|_{r,N}\coloneqq\sup_{e\in E,\lambda\in\Lambda_{e,\mathbb{C}}}|\varphi(\lambda)|(1+\|e\|+\|\lambda\|)^{N}e^{-r\|Re(\lambda)\|}

is finite;

(b)

In the non-archimedean case we require that there exists
r>0
such that

\|\varphi\|_{r}\coloneqq\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{C}}}|\varphi(\lambda)|e^{-r\|Re(\lambda)\|}

is finite.

We now define a locally convex topology on
PW(\Lambda)
. Suppose first that we are in the archimedean case.
For each
r>0
, we define
PW^{r}(\Lambda)
to be the subspace of all
\varphi\in PW(\Lambda)
such that
\|\varphi\|_{r,N}<\infty
for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and we give
PW^{r}(\Lambda)
the topology defined by the family of norms
\|\cdot\|_{r,N}
with
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. It is a Fréchet space. We give
PW(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{r}PW^{r}(\Lambda)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space.

In general, we define
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
to be the linear subspace of
PW(\Lambda)
consisting of all
\varphi\in PW(\Lambda)
that are supported on finitely many components
\Lambda_{e}
. Note that in the non-archimedean case we have
PW_{f}(\Lambda)=PW(\Lambda)
. We topologise
PW_{f}
as follows. First, for each finite set
E_{0}\subseteq E
, define
PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
to be the subspace of all
\varphi\in PW(\Lambda)
that are supported on
\coprod_{e\in E_{0}}\Lambda_{e}
. For each
r>0
, define
PW_{E_{0}}^{r}(\Lambda)
to be the subspace of all
\varphi\in PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
such that
\|\varphi\|_{r}<\infty
in the non-archimedean case and
\|\varphi\|_{r,N}<\infty
for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
in the archimedean case. We give
PW_{E_{0}}^{r}(\Lambda)
the Banach space topology defined by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{r}
in the non-archimedean case and the Fréchet space topology defined by the family of norms
\|\cdot\|_{r,N}
in the archimedean case. We give
PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{r}PW_{E_{0}}^{r}(\Lambda)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space. Finally, we give
PW_{f}(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{E_{0}}PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it also a strict LF-space. In the archimedean case, the topology on
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
is at least as fine as the subspace topology inherited from
PW(\Lambda)
, that is, the injection
PW_{f}(\Lambda)\to PW(\Lambda)
is continuous.

We have defined the topology on
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
in an analogous way to how the topology is defined on
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,K)
. A simpler way of describing the topology on
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
is as follows. We write
PW_{e}(\Lambda)=PW_{\{e\}}(\Lambda)
. The subspace
PW_{e}(\Lambda)
is the classical Paley–Wiener space
PW(\Lambda_{e})
on
\Lambda_{e}
. Observe that we have the locally convex direct sum decomposition
PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)=\bigoplus_{e\in E_{0}}PW_{e}(\Lambda)
, where
PW_{e}(\Lambda)=PW_{\{e\}}(\Lambda)
. This can be shown by checking that
PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
satisfies the correct universal property. Alternatively, this follows from the fact that
\bigoplus_{e\in E_{0}}PW_{e}(\Lambda)
is a Fréchet space (even a Banach space in the non-archimedean case) and applying a suitable version of the open mapping theorem to the continuous bijection
\bigoplus_{e\in E_{0}}PW_{e}(\Lambda)\to PW_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
. Choosing an enumeration
e_{1},e_{2},\dots
of
E_{0}
, we have

PW_{f}(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}PW_{\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}}(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}PW_{e_{i}}(\Lambda)

with the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, and consequently we have the locally convex direct sum decomposition

PW_{f}(\Lambda)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}PW_{e}(\Lambda).

To define the Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
on
\Lambda
, in the archimedean case we need to make use of certain constant coefficient differential operators on
\Lambda
. Recall in the archimedean case we have an identification of each
\Lambda_{e}
with a fixed vector space
iV^{*}
. Thus, we have a notion of a differential operator on
\Lambda
that is the same on almost all (all but finitely many) components
\Lambda_{e}
.

We define the Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
on
\Lambda
to be the space of smooth functions
\varphi:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
such that the following holds.

(1)

In the non-archimedean case we require that
\varphi
is supported on finitely many connected components
\Lambda_{e}
.

(2)

\varphi
satisfies a decay condition:

(a)

In the archimedean case, we require that for each differential operator
D
on
\Lambda
that is the same on almost all components and for each
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have

\|\varphi\|_{D,N}\coloneqq\sup_{e\in E,\lambda\in\Lambda_{e}}|D\varphi(\lambda)|(1+\|e\|+\|\lambda\|)^{N}

is finite.

(b)

In the non-archimedean case, we require that for each differential operator
D
on
\Lambda
, we have

\|\varphi\|_{D}\coloneqq\sup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}|D\varphi(\lambda)|

is finite.

In the archimedean case,
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
is a Fréchet space with the topology defined by the family of seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{D,N}
. Note that in the definition of
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
in the archimedean case, we could have restricted attention to differential operators
D
that are the same on all components of
\Lambda
; this is how such Schwartz spaces are often defined in the literature.

For each finite set
E_{0}\subseteq E
we define
\mathscr{S}_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
to be the subspace of all
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
that are supported on
\coprod_{e\in E_{0}}\Lambda_{e}
. In the archimedean case,
\mathscr{S}_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
is a closed subspace of
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
and is thus a Fréchet space with respect to the topology defined by the family of seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{D,N}
. In the non-archimedean case, we give
\mathscr{S}_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
the topology defined by the family of seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{D}
. It is a Fréchet space. We define
\mathscr{S}_{e}(\Lambda)=\mathscr{S}_{\{e\}}(\Lambda)
. Note that
\mathscr{S}_{e}(\Lambda)
is the classical Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{e})
on
\Lambda_{e}
.

In the non-archimedean case, we give
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)=\bigcup_{E_{0}}\mathscr{S}_{E_{0}}(\Lambda)
the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space, and we have
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)=C_{c}^{\infty}(\Lambda)
.

We could also define a space
\mathscr{S}_{f}(\Lambda)
in the archimedean case in a manner similar to how we defined
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
, although we will not make use of this space. Such Schwartz spaces would presumably be used in an invariant Paley–Wiener theorem for
K
-finite Schwartz functions, but to our knowledge such a theorem has not yet been established.

3.4.1.
The Fourier transform.

Fix a space
\Lambda
as in
Section

3.4
. It is naturally dual to the space
X=\coprod_{e\in E}X_{e}
, where
X_{e}=V
in the archimedean case and
X_{e}=\Gamma_{e}
in the non-archimedean case.

We will define function spaces
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
and
\mathscr{S}(X)
and a Fourier transform that gives isomorphisms of topological vector spaces
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\to PW(\Lambda)
and
\mathscr{S}(X)\to\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
. We remark that in the archimedean case,
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
will not be the space of compactly supported smooth functions on
X
if
X
has infinitely many components.

First, we define the spaces
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
and
\mathscr{S}(X)
in the non-archimedean case. We define
\mathscr{S}(X)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}\mathscr{S}(\Gamma_{e})
, where
\mathscr{S}(\Gamma_{e})
is the space of functions
\phi:\Gamma_{e}\to\mathbb{C}
that are rapidly decreasing, i.e. for all
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we have

\|\phi\|_{N}\coloneqq\sup_{x\in X}|\phi(x)|(1+\|x\|)^{N}<\infty.

The space
\mathscr{S}(\Gamma_{e})
is a Fréchet space with respect to the topology defined by the seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{N}
, and
\mathscr{S}(X)
is a strict LF-space.

We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
. Here,
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
is the space of compactly supported (i.e. finitely supported) functions on
\Gamma_{e}
equipped with the finest locally convex topology. Thus,
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
also has the finest locally convex topology. For
r>0
, we define
C_{r}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
to be the space of functions on
\Gamma_{e}
with support contained in
\{x\in\Gamma_{e}:\|x\|\leq r\}
. It is a finite-dimensional space, and we give it the locally convex topology. Then
C_{r}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
is a closed subspace of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
. Moreover, we have an increasing union
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})=\bigcup_{r>0}C_{r}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
and
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
is the inductive limit of the
C_{r}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
in the category of locally convex spaces. It is a strict LF-space. We define
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)=\bigoplus_{e\in E}C_{r}^{\infty}(\Gamma_{e})
. Again, we have that
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is the increasing union
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)=\bigcup_{r>0}C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
, it is the inductive limit of the
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
in the category of locally convex spaces, and it is a strict LF-space.

Now, we treat the archimedean case. We may view
V=i(iV^{*})^{*}
, and thus we have a definition of
\mathscr{S}(X)
. It remains for us to define
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
in the archimedean case. For
r>0
, we define
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
to be the space of smooth functions
\phi:X\to\mathbb{C}
such that:

(1)

the support of
\phi_{e}
is contained in the closed ball of radius
r
in
X_{e}
;

(2)

for all
N>0
and all differential operators
D
that are the same on almost all components
X_{e}
, we have

\|\phi\|_{D,N}\coloneqq\sup_{e\in E,x\in X_{e}}|D\phi_{e}(x)|(1+\|e\|)^{N}

is finite.

We give
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
the topology defined by the family of seminorms
\|\cdot\|_{D,N}
, which makes
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
a Fréchet space. (Note that in the definition of
C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
and its topology, it suffices to use differential operators that are the same on all components of
X
.) We define
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
to be the increasing union
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)=\bigcup_{r>0}C_{r}^{\infty}(X)
and we give it the inductive limit topology in the category of locally convex spaces, making it a strict LF-space.

Note that
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
is a dense subspace of
\mathscr{S}(X)
in all cases and the inclusion map
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\to\mathscr{S}(X)
is continuous.

We now fix measures on
X
and
\Lambda
. We fix Pontryagin dual measures on
V
and
iV^{*}
. These determine Pontryagin dual measures on
X_{e}
and
\Lambda_{e}
in the archimedean case. In the non-archimedean case, we give each
X_{e}=\Gamma_{e}
the counting measure and
\Lambda_{e}=iV^{*}/\Gamma_{e}^{\vee}
the Pontryagin dual measure.

For
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(X)
, we define its Fourier transform
\widehat{\phi}=(\widehat{\phi_{e}})_{e\in E}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{C}
by taking the Fourier transform on each component
X_{e}
, that is,
\widehat{\phi_{e}}(\lambda)=\int_{X_{e}}\phi_{e}(x)e^{-2\pi\langle x,\lambda\rangle}\!\mathop{}d{x}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The Fourier transform gives isomorphisms of topological vector spaces
\mathscr{S}(X)\to\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
,
C_{r/2\pi}^{\infty}(X)\to PW^{r}(\Lambda)
, and
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)\to PW(\Lambda)
.

This follows easily from the classical version of this lemma when
E
is a singleton, together with the following basic inequalities:
(1+x+y)\leq(1+x)(1+y)\leq(1+x+y)^{2}
for
x,y\geq 0
; and for
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have
(1+x)^{N}\ll(1+x^{2})^{N}\ll(1+x)^{2N}
for
x\geq 0
.

Corollary 3.4
.

PW(\Lambda)
is a dense subspace of
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
and the inclusion map

PW(\Lambda)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)

is continuous.

3.4.2.
Pullback mappings.

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be Euclidean spaces and let
T:iV_{1}^{*}\to iV_{2}^{*}
be an injective linear map. Then pullback defines continuous linear maps
T^{*}:\mathscr{S}(iV_{2}^{*})\to\mathscr{S}(iV_{1}^{*})
and
T^{*}:PW(iV_{2}^{*})\to PW(iV_{1}^{*})
. Similarly, if
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
are lattices in
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, respectively, and
T:iV_{1}^{*}/\Gamma_{1}^{\vee}\to iV_{2}^{*}/\Gamma_{2}^{\vee}
is a smooth homomorphism, then pullback along
T
defines continuous linear maps
T^{*}:\mathscr{S}(iV_{2}^{*}/\Gamma_{2}^{\vee})\to\mathscr{S}(iV_{1}^{*}/\Gamma_{1}^{\vee})
and
T^{*}:PW(iV_{2}^{*}/\Gamma_{2}^{\vee})\to PW(iV_{1}^{*}/\Gamma_{1}^{\vee})
.

There is a simple generalisation of this to the Schwartz and Paley–Wiener spaces
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
,
PW(\Lambda)
, and
PW_{f}(\Lambda)
defined above.

Lemma 3.5
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\Lambda_{i}=\coprod_{e\in E_{i}}\Lambda_{e}
be a space as defined in
Section

3.4
with
V=V_{i}
.

Let
T_{E}:E_{1}\to E_{2}
be a partially defined map and let
T_{V}:iV_{1}^{*}\to iV_{2}^{*}
be a linear map. In the archimedean case, assume that
\|T_{E}(e_{1})\|\gg\|e_{1}\|
and that
T_{V}
is injective. In the non-archimedean case, assume that
T_{E}
has finite fibres and that
T_{V}(\Gamma_{1}^{\vee})\subseteq\Gamma_{2}^{\vee}
so that
T_{V}
descends to a smooth homomorphism
T_{V}:iV_{1}^{*}/\Gamma_{1}^{\vee}\to iV_{2}^{*}/\Gamma_{2}^{\vee}
.

Let
\varphi=(\varphi_{e_{2}})_{e_{2}\in E_{2}}\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{2})
. Define
T^{*}\varphi=((T^{*}\varphi)_{e_{1}})_{e_{1}\in E_{1}}
by
(T^{*}\varphi)_{e_{1}}=0
if
T_{E}(e_{1})
is undefined and
(T^{*}\varphi)_{e_{1}}=T_{V}^{*}\varphi_{T_{E}(e_{1})}
otherwise. To simplify notation, we write
\varphi_{T_{E}(e_{1})}=0
if
T_{E}(e_{1})
is not defined. Then
(T^{*}\varphi)_{e_{1}}=T_{V}^{*}\varphi_{T_{E}(e_{1})}
for all
e_{1}\in E_{1}
.

For all
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{2})
, we have
T^{*}\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{1})
and

T^{*}:\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{2})\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{1})

is a continuous linear map. Moreover,
T^{*}
restricts to a continuous linear map

T^{*}:PW(\Lambda_{2})\longrightarrow PW(\Lambda_{1}).

In the archimedean case, if
T_{E}
in addition has finite fibres, then
T^{*}
restricts further to a continuous linear map

T^{*}:PW_{f}(\Lambda_{2})\longrightarrow PW_{f}(\Lambda_{1}).

Proof.

The non-archimedean case and the last claim is straightforward. We prove the first claim in the archimedean case, the second being similar. Let
D
be an invariant differential operator on
iV_{1}^{*}
and let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We will also use
D
to denote the extension of
D
along
T_{V}
to an invariant differential operator on
iV_{2}^{*}
. Let
\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{2})
. We have

\|T^{*}\varphi\|_{D,N}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}e_{1}\in E_{1}\\
\lambda\in\Lambda_{e_{1}}\end{subarray}}|D\varphi_{T_{E}(e_{1})}(T_{V}\lambda)|(1+\|e_{1}\|+\|\lambda\|)^{N}.

For all
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we have

|D\varphi_{T_{E}(e_{1})}(T_{V}\lambda)|\leq\|\varphi\|_{D,N^{\prime}}(1+\|T_{E}(e_{1})\|+\|T_{V}\lambda\|)^{-N^{\prime}}.

Since
T_{V}:iV_{1}^{*}\to iV_{2}^{*}
is injective, we have that
\lambda\mapsto\|T_{V}\lambda\|
is a norm on
iV_{1}^{*}
and thus
\|T_{V}\lambda\|\asymp\|\lambda\|
. Since
\|T_{E}(e_{1})\|\gg\|e_{1}\|
, we have

(1+\|T_{E}(e_{1})\|+\|T_{V}\lambda\|)^{-N^{\prime}}\ll(1+\|e_{1}\|+\|\lambda\|)^{N}

for
N^{\prime}
sufficiently large. Thus,

\|T^{*}\varphi\|_{D,N}\ll\|\varphi\|_{D,N^{\prime}}

for
N^{\prime}
sufficiently large. Thus, we have
T^{*}\varphi\in\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{1})
and
T^{*}
defines a continuous linear map
T^{*}:\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{2})\to\mathscr{S}(\Lambda_{1})
. That
T^{*}
restricts to a continuous linear map
T^{*}:PW(\Lambda_{2})\to PW(\Lambda_{1})
follows in a similar way.
∎

The proof of
Theorem

5.1
, from which the existence of stable transfer follows, makes use of this lemma.

3.5.
Invariant Paley–Wiener Theorems

As explained above, we view the invariant Fourier transform of
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
as the
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant function
f_{G}:T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
f_{G}(\tau)=f_{G}(\pi_{\tau})
. We thus view the space of invariant Fourier transforms of elements of
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G)
as

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G)

\displaystyle=\{f_{G}:T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\to\mathbb{C}:f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G)\}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G)/\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G)}(T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)).

The invariant Paley–Wiener theorem for
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G)
asserts that the invariant Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G)\to\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G)
is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces and gives a characterisation of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}}(G)
as a Schwartz (resp. Paley–Wiener) space. In this subsection we review this and other invariant Paley–Wiener theorems that we will stabilise.

Let
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
. Recall that if
F
is archimedean we have defined a “norm” on the set of infinitesimal characters of
L
. We define
\|\tau\|=\|\mu_{\tau}\|
for all
\tau\in\widetilde{T}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
. For any countable set
E\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
, we have a Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\Lambda)
and a Paley–Wiener space
PW(\Lambda)
defined as in
Section

3.4
on the space
\Lambda=\coprod_{\tau\in E}\Lambda_{\tau}
with
\Lambda_{\tau}=i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}
.

We define
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
to be the space of smooth
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-equivariant functions
\varphi:T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)\to\mathbb{C}
such that for some (and hence any) choice of representatives
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
for the connected components of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, we have

\varphi\in\mathscr{S}\Bigg(\coprod_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)}i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}\Bigg).

We define
PW_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
(resp.
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(L)
) in the same way as
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
, except that we replace
\mathscr{S}(\cdot)
by
PW(\cdot)
(resp.
PW_{f}(\cdot)
). We define

\mathscr{S}(G)=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}PW_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)\Bigg)^{W_{0}^{G}}=\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}PW_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)^{W^{G}(L)}

and similarly we define
PW(G)
and
PW_{f}(G)
. It follows from the decomposition of
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
in terms of the
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)
, that
\mathscr{S}(G)
,
PW(G)
, and
PW_{f}(G)
are naturally spaces of smooth
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-equivariant functions on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
. We have the following invariant Paley–Wiener theorems.

Theorem 3.6
.

The invariant Fourier transform is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}(G).

It restricts to isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}_{c}(G)\longrightarrow PW(G).

This further restricts to an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}_{f}(G)\longrightarrow PW_{f}(G)

Thus, we have
\widehat{\mathcal{I}}(G)=\mathscr{S}(G)
,
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{c}}(G)=PW(G)
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{f}}(G)=PW_{f}(G)
.

Injectivity of the first map is equivalent to the assertion that for
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
, if
f_{G}
vanishes on
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, then
f_{G}(\gamma)=0
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
. This is called spectral density. Kazhdan proved spectral density follows for
p
-adic groups
[
Kaz86
, Theorem J.(a)]
. Spectral density for both real and
p
-adic groups can also be seen as a corollary of Arthur’s Fourier inversion theorem for orbital integrals
[
Art94a
, Theorem 4.1 with
M=G
]
, which we will recall in a moment. The first map in the theorem was proved to be an open continuous surjection by Arthur in
[
Art94b
]
. For real groups, the second statement in the theorem is proved in a more general twisted form in
[
MW16a
]
using a twisted invariant Paley–Wiener theorem due Renard, which generalises a theorem due to Bouaziz in the non-twisted case. For
p
-adic groups, the second statement is proved in
[
Art93
]
. The third statement is only different from the second in the case of real groups, and it is also proved in
[
Art93
]
. (See also
[
MW18
, §6.2]
.)

To state Arthur’s Fourier inversion theorem for orbital integrals, we need a measure on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. First, we define a measure on
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for each
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
by

\int_{T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\alpha(\tau)\!\mathop{}d{\tau}=\sum_{\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/(\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*})}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\tau}^{\vee}}\alpha(\tau_{\lambda})\!\mathop{}d{\lambda},

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. Then, we define a measure on
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by

\int_{T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\alpha(\tau)\!\mathop{}d{\tau}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}|W^{G}(L)|^{-1}\int_{T_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}}\alpha(\tau)\!\mathop{}d{\tau}

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. The following is Arthur’s Fourier inversion theorem for orbital integrals.

Theorem 3.7
.

There exists a smooth function
I_{G}(\gamma,\tau)
on
\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)\times T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, which satisfies
I_{G}(\gamma,z\tau)=z^{-1}I_{G}(\gamma,\tau)
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and satisfies

f_{G}(\gamma)=\int_{T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{\times}}I_{G}(\gamma,\tau)f_{G}(\tau)\!\mathop{}d{\tau}

for all
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
and
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
.

This is
[
Art94a
, Theorem 4.1 with
M=G
]
. See the discussion before Lemma 6.3 in
[
Art96
]
, where this specialisation of
[
Art94a
, Theorem 4.1]
is discussed. Writing
I_{G}(\tau,\gamma)
for the normalised character
I_{G}(\tau,\gamma)=|D^{G}(\gamma)|^{1/2}\Theta_{\tau}(\gamma)
, we have the dual formula

f_{G}(\tau)=\int_{\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}I_{G}(\tau,\gamma)f_{G}(\gamma)\!\mathop{}d{\gamma}.

Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
,
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
) denotes the image of the space
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
(resp.
C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G)
,
C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,K)
) in
\mathcal{I}(G)
(resp.
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G)
,
\mathcal{I}_{c}(G,K)
). For
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
, it follows from spectral density that
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
if and only if its invariant Fourier transform is supported on
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. We obtain the following invariant Paley–Wiener theorems for cuspidal functions as a corollary of the above invariant Paley–Wiener theorem.

Corollary 3.8
.

The invariant Fourier transform is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G).

It restricts to isomorphisms of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\longrightarrow PW_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)

and

\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\longrightarrow PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G).

3.5.1.
Pseudocoefficients.

Let
\zeta
be a unitary character of
A_{G}(F)
. The finite group
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
acts on
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
and the
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbits in
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
meet
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
in
(\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee})
-orbits. Thus, it is natural to regard
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
as a discrete space. Define
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)
to be the space of all
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-equivariant functions
\varphi:T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
that are supported on finitely many
\mathbb{C}^{1}
-orbits. Let
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
be a set of representatives for the countable set
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Then we have an identification

PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C},

defined by mapping
\varphi
to
(\varphi(\tau))_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}
. The direct sum
\bigoplus_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C}
can be thought of as the
PW_{f}
-space on the countable 0-dimensional manifold
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Recall that we have identified
\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)
with a subset of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Thus, we may assume that
\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)\subseteq E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
.

There is a Paley–Wiener theorem for the invariant Fourier transform on the space
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
, which we now recall from
[
MW18
, §7.2]
.

Theorem 3.9
.

The invariant Fourier transform gives an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta).

A consequence is that for each
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, there exists a unique function
f[\tau]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
such that
f[\tau]_{G}(z\tau)=z\|\tau\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
, and
f[\tau]_{G}(\tau^{\prime})=0
for
\tau^{\prime}\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
with
\tau^{\prime}\not\in\mathbb{C}^{1}\tau
. The function
f[\tau]_{G}
is called the pseudocoefficient of
\tau
in
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
. Note that for all
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
, we have
f[z\tau]_{G}=z^{-1}f[\tau]_{G}
. Moreover, the set
\{f[\tau]_{G}\}_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}
is a basis of
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
. It follows from the simple form of the local trace formula that for
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs},\mathrm{ell}}(G)
we have

f[\tau]_{G}(\gamma)=m(\gamma)^{-1}|D^{G}(\gamma)|^{1/2}\overline{\Theta_{\tau}(\gamma)}.

Recall that
m(\gamma)=\vol(G_{\gamma}(F)/A_{G}(F))
. (See
[
MW18
, §7.2]
.)

Since
\{f[\tau]_{G}\}_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}
is a basis of
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
, there is a unique conjugate-linear isomorphism

D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)

given on the basis
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
by
\tau\mapsto f[\tau]_{G}
. By conjugate-linearity, for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{1}
we have
z\tau\mapsto z^{-1}f[\tau]_{G}=f[z\tau]_{G}
. Consequently, the isomorphism does not depend on the choice of representatives
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. For all
\pi\in D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
we write
f[\pi]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
for the corresponding element under the above isomorphism. We call
f[\pi]_{G}
the pseudocoefficient of
\pi
in
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
.

For all
f_{G}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
and
\pi\in D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, we have
\langle mf_{G},mf[\pi]_{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=f_{G}(\pi)
. Indeed, it suffices to check this for
\pi=\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, and this in turn follows from the formula for
f[\tau]_{G}
given above. Note that the map
\pi\mapsto mf[\pi]_{G}
is unitary with respect to the elliptic inner product in the sense that for all
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, we have
\langle\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\langle mf[\pi_{1}]_{G},mf[\pi_{2}]_{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}
. If
B
is an orthogonal basis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
(with respect to the elliptic inner product), then for all
b,b^{\prime}\in B
, we have

f[b]_{G}(b^{\prime})=\langle mf[b]_{G},mf[b^{\prime}]_{G}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\langle b,b^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime}).

4.
Stable Harmonic Analysis

4.1.
Stable distributions

Stable harmonic analysis is concerned with stable orbital integrals, which are integrals over stable conjugacy classes. We begin by recalling these notions. Let
x,x^{\prime}\in G(F)
. If there exists
g\in G
such that
x^{\prime}=g^{-1}xg
, then
g\sigma(g)^{-1}\in G^{x_{s}}=C_{G}(x_{s})
, where
x_{s}
is the semisimple component of
x
in its Jordan decomposition. The elements
x,x^{\prime}\in G(F)
are said to be stably conjugate if there exists
g\in G
such that
x^{\prime}=g^{-1}xg
and
g\sigma(g)^{-1}\in G_{x_{s}}=C_{G}(x_{s})^{\circ}
for all
\sigma\in\Gamma_{F}
(see
[
Kot82
, §3]
). Stable conjugacy is an equivalence relation on
G(F)
, which is intermediate in strength between
G(F)
-conjugacy and
G
-conjugacy. Each stable conjugacy class (or stable class) is a finite union of
G(F)
-conjugacy classes. Note that for complex groups, stable conjugacy is the same as conjugacy. Two strongly regular elements of
G(F)
are stably conjugate if and only if they are
G
-conjugate, and thus the stable conjugacy class of a strongly regular element
x\in G(F)
is simply the intersection
x^{G}\cap G(F)
of its
G
-conjugacy class with
G(F)
. The Weyl discriminant
D^{G}
is stably invariant, that is, constant on stable conjugacy classes. If
T
is a maximal torus of
G
, then two
G
-regular elements of
T(F)
are conjugate if and only if they lie in the same orbit of the stable Weyl group
W(G,T)(F)
of
T
. Let

\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)\subseteq\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\subseteq\Delta(G)

denote the spaces of stable conjugacy classes in
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
,
G_{\mathrm{rs}}(F)
, and
G(F)
, respectively. Then
\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
and
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
are open dense locally compact Hausdorff subspaces of
\Delta(G)
, and
\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
is naturally an
F
-analytic manifold.

The stable orbital integral at
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
is the
\zeta
-equivariant Radon measure and tempered distribution on
G(F)
defined by

SO_{\delta}=\sum_{\gamma\subseteq\delta}O_{\gamma},

where the sum is over all conjugacy classes
\gamma
in the stable conjugacy class
\delta
. Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
. The normalised stable orbital integral of
f
is defined by

f^{G}(\delta)=|D^{G}(\delta)|^{1/2}SO_{\delta}(f)=\sum_{\gamma\subseteq\delta}f_{G}(\gamma).

The functions
\delta\mapsto SO_{\delta}(f)
and
f^{G}
are continuous on
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
and smooth on
\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
, and
f^{G}
is locally bounded.

Two maximal tori
T,T^{\prime}
of
G
are said to be stably conjugate if there exists
g\in G
such that
\Int(g):T\to T^{\prime}
is defined over
F
. We have a stable version of the Weyl integration formula,

\int_{G(F)}f(x)\!\mathop{}d{x}=\sum_{\{T\}_{\mathrm{st}}}|W(G,T)(F)|^{-1}\int_{T(F)}|D^{G}(t)|SO_{t}(f)\!\mathop{}d{t},

where
\{T\}_{\mathrm{st}}
runs over the set of stable conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
G
. As in the invariant case, one can define a Radon measure
\!\mathop{}d{\delta}
on
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
by

\int_{\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}\alpha(\delta)\!\mathop{}d{\delta}=\sum_{\{T\}_{\mathrm{st}}}|W(G,T)(F)|^{-1}\int_{T(F)}\alpha(t)\!\mathop{}d{t},

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G))
, where the sum runs over a set of representatives of the stable conjugacy classes of maximal tori of
G
. The stable Weyl integration formula then becomes

\int_{G(F)}f(x)\!\mathop{}d{x}=\int_{\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}|D^{G}(\delta)|SO_{\delta}(f)\!\mathop{}d{\delta}.

Note that
\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
has full measure in
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
.

We define the subspace of unstable functions in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{(c)}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{SO_{\delta}:\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\})

\displaystyle=\{f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta):f^{G}(\delta)=0,\,\forall\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}

and its image in
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
,

\mathcal{I}_{(c)}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=\Ann_{\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{SO_{\delta}:\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}).

We define the space of stable orbital integrals

\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)=\{f^{G}:f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\}.

We have

\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)/\mathcal{C}_{(c)}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)/\mathcal{I}_{(c)}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)

and we give it the natural quotient topology. We also define the spaces
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,K)
,
\mathcal{I}_{f}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{I}_{c}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,K)
, and
\mathcal{S}_{f}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{S}_{c}(G,\zeta,K)
in a similar way.

The space of
\zeta
-equivariant stable distributions is defined to be
\mathcal{S}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
and the space of tempered
\zeta
-equivariant stable distributions is defined to be
\mathcal{S}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
. Note that we have a continuous linear injection
\mathcal{S}_{c}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{S}(G,\zeta)
with dense image. Its transpose is a continuous linear injection
\mathcal{S}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}\to\mathcal{S}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, which enables us to identify each tempered
\zeta
-equivariant stable distribution with a
\zeta
-equivariant stable distribution.

We may identify
\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
with a subspace of
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
via the transpose of the quotient map
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. Thus, we have a further identification of
\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
as a subspace of
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
. As vector spaces, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}}(\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{SO_{\delta}:\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}))

\displaystyle=\cl_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime},\textrm{weak-}*}(\{SO_{\delta}:\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)\}).

That is, an distribution in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
if and only if it lies in the weak-
*
closure in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
of the linear span of the set of regular semisimple stable orbital integrals of
G
. A locally integrable function
\Theta
on
G(F)
that is continuous on
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
defines a stable distribution of
G
if and only if
\Theta
is stably invariant on
G_{\mathrm{sr}}(F)
.

We say that a virtual representation is stable if its character, which we identify it with, is stable. Let
D_{\mathrm{spec}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
D_{\mathrm{temp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
,
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
) denote the subspace of stable elements in
D_{\mathrm{spec}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
D_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
,
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
).

The parabolic descent map
\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{W^{G}(M)}
descends to a continuous map

\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{W^{G}(M)},

which we write as
f^{G}\mapsto f^{M}
and also call parabolic descent. Thus, the parabolic induction map
I_{M}^{G}:\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{\prime}/W^{G}(M)\to\mathcal{I}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
restricts to a continuous map
I_{M}^{G}:\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{\prime}/W^{G}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
, namely the transpose of the parabolic descent map

\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(M,\zeta)^{W^{G}(M)}.

Consequently, parabolic induction
I_{M}^{G}
maps stable distributions on
M(F)
to stable distributions on
G(F)
. Let
\Delta_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{rs}}(M)
denote the set of
G
-regular semisimple stable conjugacy classes in
M(F)
. For all
\delta\in\Delta_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{rs}}(M)
we have
f^{M}(\delta)=f^{G}(\delta)
.

4.2.
L
-groups and
L
-parameters

We refer to a homomorphism
\eta:G\to G^{\prime}
with a normal image as a normal homomorphism. Let
\mathsf{Red}_{F,\Out}
be the category whose objects are connected reductive groups
G
over
F
, and whose morphisms are normal homomorphisms
\eta:G\to H
, taken up to
H_{\ad}(F)
-conjugacy, which we call equivalence. We write
\Psi(G)
for the based root datum
\Psi(G_{\overline{F}})
of
G_{\overline{F}}
equipped with the locally constant homomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(\Psi(G_{\overline{F}}))
. We write
\Psi(G)^{\vee}\!
for the based root datum
\Psi(G_{\overline{F}})^{\vee}\!
dual to
\Psi(G_{\overline{F}})
equipped with the locally constant homomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(\Psi(G_{\overline{F}})^{\vee}\!)
.

Let
\mathsf{Red}_{\mathbb{C},L,\Out}
be the category whose objects are connected reductive groups
G^{\vee}\!
over
\mathbb{C}
equipped with an
L
-action (a locally constant homomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(G^{\vee}\!)
that preserves a pinning), and whose morphisms are
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant normal homomorphisms
\eta^{\vee}\!:H^{\vee}\!\to G^{\vee}\!
taken up to
G^{\vee}\!{}^{\Gamma_{F}}
-conjugacy, which we call equivalence. We write
\Psi(G^{\vee}\!)
for the based root datum of
G^{\vee}\!
equipped with the locally constant homomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(\Psi(G^{\vee}\!))
.

There is an exact contravariant Langlands dual group functor

\mathsf{Red}_{F,\Out}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Red}_{\mathbb{C},L,\Out}

such that we have an identification of based root data
\Psi(G)^{\vee}\!=\Psi(G^{\vee}\!)
equipped with
\Gamma_{F}
-actions (cf.
[
Ngô20
]
). This functor is unique up to natural isomorphism. We denote this functor on objects by
G\mapsto G^{\vee}\!
and on morphisms by
\eta\mapsto\eta^{\vee}\!
. Moreover, the Langlands dual group functor has a section to the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Red}_{F,\Out}
consisting of quasisplit groups. The Langlands dual group
G^{\vee}\!
of
G
comes equipped with an
L
-action
\rho_{G}:\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(G^{\vee}\!)
.

We have a canonical
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant isomorphism

X^{*}(G_{\overline{F}})\longrightarrow X_{*}(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\circ})

and

X^{*}(G)\longrightarrow X_{*}(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}),

which we write as
\theta\mapsto\theta^{\vee}\!
.

The identification
\Psi(G)^{\vee}\!=\Psi(G^{\vee}\!)
gives rise to a canonical
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant bijection between the set of conjugacy classes of parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroups of
G_{\overline{F}}
and those of
G^{\vee}\!
. This restricts to a bijection between the set of
\Gamma_{F}
-stable conjugacy classes of parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroups of
G_{\overline{F}}
and those of
G^{\vee}\!
. A parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroup of
G^{\vee}\!
is said to be
G
-relevant if its conjugacy class corresponds to the conjugacy class of a parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroup of
G_{\overline{F}}
that is defined over
F
. If a parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroup of
G^{\vee}\!
is
G
-relevant, then so is every parabolic (resp. Levi) subgroup of
G
containing it.

As mentioned in the introduction, we use the Weil form of the
L
-group:
\prescript{L}{}{G}=G^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}
. The
L
-groups lie in a natural category of what we refer to as
\lambda
-groups based on the notation in
[
Lan04
]
.

Given a surjective homomorphism
\mathcal{G}\to W_{F}
, we will write
\mathcal{G}^{0}=\ker(\mathcal{G}\to W_{F})
. Note that we have used a superscript “0” instead of a superscript “
\circ
”, which we use to denote identity components. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a second countable locally compact group together with a continuous surjective homomorphism
\mathcal{G}\to W_{F}
. By the open mapping theorem, the homomorphism
\mathcal{G}\to W_{F}
is open. Suppose that the kernel
\mathcal{G}^{0}=\ker(\mathcal{G}\to W_{F})
is the group of
\mathbb{C}
-points of a connected reductive group
\mathcal{G}^{0}
over
\mathbb{C}
and that for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
the automorphism
\Int(g)|_{\mathcal{G}^{0}}:\mathcal{G}^{0}\to\mathcal{G}^{0}
is algebraic. The resulting homomorphism
\mathcal{G}\to\Aut(\Psi(\mathcal{G}^{0}))
then factors through
W_{F}
. Suppose that it further factors through
W_{F}/W_{K}=\Gamma_{K/F}
for some finite Galois subextension
K/F
of
F_{s}/F
, and thus extends to a continuous homomorphism
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(\Psi(\mathcal{G}^{0}))
along
W_{F}\to\Gamma_{F}
. We then say that
\mathcal{G}\to W_{F}
, or just
\mathcal{G}
, is a
\lambda
-group.

An element
g\in\mathcal{G}
is said to be semisimple if
\Int(g)|_{\mathcal{G}^{0}}
is a semisimple automorphism of
\mathcal{G}^{0}
, that is, after embedding
\mathcal{G}^{0}
in a general linear group it can be realised as conjugation by a semisimple element. If
g\in\mathcal{G}^{0}
, then
g
is semisimple as an element of
\mathcal{G}
if and only if it is semisimple as an element of
\mathcal{G}^{0}
. If
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
are
\lambda
-groups, an isomorphism of topological groups
\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
over
W_{F}
maps semisimple elements to semisimple elements.

Note that an
L
-group
\prescript{L}{}{G}=G^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}
of a connected reductive group
G
over
F
together with the projection
\prescript{L}{}{G}\to W_{F}
is a
\lambda
-group. The semisimple elements of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
are those of the form
(g,w)
, where
g
is a semisimple element of
G^{\vee}\!
.

Let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}
be two
\lambda
-groups. A continuous homomorphism
\xi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
over
W_{F}
is said to be an
L
-homomorphism if its restriction
\xi_{0}:\mathcal{H}^{0}\to\mathcal{G}^{0}
is algebraic and semisimple in the sense that it maps semisimple elements to semisimple elements. Two
L
-homomorphisms
\xi,\xi^{\prime}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
are said to be equivalent if there exists
g\in\mathcal{G}^{0}
such that
\xi^{\prime}=\Int(g)\circ\xi
. We define a morphism of
\lambda
-groups to be an equivalence class of
L
-homomorphisms and denote the category of
\lambda
-groups by
\lambda\mathsf{Gp}
. We denote the full subcategory of
L
-groups by
L\mathsf{Gp}
. We call an
L
-homomorphism
\xi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
an
L
-embedding if it is a topological embedding, in which case
\xi(\mathcal{H})
is a closed subgroup of
\mathcal{G}
and
\xi_{0}:\mathcal{H}^{0}\to\mathcal{G}^{0}
is a closed embedding of algebraic groups. We will often switch between thinking of
\xi
as an equivalence class and a single
L
-homomorphism.

For a normal homomorphism
\eta:G\to H
, we have an equivalence class of
L
-homomorphisms
\prescript{L}{}{\eta}=\eta^{\vee}\!\rtimes\mathrm{id}_{W_{F}}:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
and this gives an exact contravariant functor
\mathsf{Red}_{F,\Out}\to\lambda\mathsf{Gp}
. Its essential image is
L\mathsf{Gp}
, and a
\lambda
-group
\mathcal{G}
lies in
L\mathsf{Gp}
if and only if there is a splitting
c:W_{F}\to\mathcal{G}
(i.e. continuous homomorphic section of
\mathcal{G}\to W_{F}
) such that the action
\rho_{c}:W_{F}\to\Aut(\mathcal{G}^{0})
defined by
\rho_{c}(w)=\Int(c(w))|_{\mathcal{G}^{0}}
preserves a pinning, in which case
\rho_{c}
factors through
\Gamma_{K/F}
if
W_{F}\to\Aut(\Psi(\mathcal{G}^{0}))
does.

We recall that a parabolic subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
is defined to be a closed subgroup
\mathcal{P}
that maps onto
W_{F}
such that
\mathcal{P}^{0}
is a parabolic subgroup of
G^{\vee}\!
. Similarly, a Levi subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
is defined to be a closed subgroup
\mathcal{M}
that maps onto
W_{F}
such that
\mathcal{M}^{0}
is a Levi subgroup of
G^{\vee}\!
. We say that
\mathcal{P}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}
) is
G
relevant if
\mathcal{P}^{0}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}^{0}
) is
G
-relevant.

For each Levi subgroup of
G
, we have a canonical equivalence class of
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant embeddings
\iota_{M}^{G}:M^{\vee}\!\to G^{\vee}\!
, and these extend to give a canonical equivalence class of tempered
L
-embeddings
\iota_{M}^{G}:\prescript{L}{}{M}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
. The
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy classes of groups
\iota_{M}^{G}(M^{\vee}\!)
(resp.
\iota_{M}^{G}(\prescript{L}{}{M})
) are precisely the
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy classes of
G
-relevant Levi subgroups of
G^{\vee}\!
(resp.
\prescript{L}{}{G}
). If
L\subseteq M
are Levi subgroups of
G
, then
\iota_{M}^{G}\circ\iota_{L}^{M}=\iota_{L}^{G}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
that maps onto
W_{F}
. By
[
Bor79
, Proposition 3.6]
the Levi subgroups
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{H}}
of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
that contain
\mathcal{H}
minimally are all conjugate by
C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\mathcal{H})
. We say that
\mathcal{H}
is
G
-relevant if
\mathcal{H}
is only contained in
G
-relevant Levi subgroups of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
, or equivalently if
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{H}}
is
G
-relevant. We say that
\mathcal{H}
is elliptic if it is not contained in a proper Levi subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
. If
\mathcal{H}
is a
\lambda
-group, an
L
-homomorphism
\xi:\mathcal{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
is said to be
G
-relevant (resp. elliptic) if its image is
G
-relevant (resp. elliptic), and we write
\mathcal{M}_{\xi}=\mathcal{M}_{\xi(\mathcal{H})}
.

Every
L
-homomorphism
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
can be written in the form
\xi(h,w)=(\xi_{0}(h)a_{\xi}(w),w)
, where
\xi_{0}:H^{\vee}\!\to G^{\vee}\!
is a morphism of algebraic groups and
a_{\xi}\in Z_{c}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)
. The cohomology class
a_{\xi}\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)
only depends on the equivalence class of
\xi
. One says that
\xi
is tempered (or bounded, or of unitary type) if the image of
a_{\xi}
in
G^{\vee}\!
is bounded, that is, has compact closure. We will use the notation
a_{\xi}
and
\xi_{0}
without comment.

Recall that
L_{F}
denotes the local Langlands group of
F
, which defined by

L_{F}=\begin{cases}\prescript{L}{}{1}=W_{F}&\textrm{if $F$ is archimedean},\\
\prescript{L}{}{\PGL_{2}}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times W_{F}&\textrm{if $F$ is non-archimedean}.\end{cases}

We write the homomorphism
L_{F}\to W_{F}
as
l\mapsto w(l)
. For an
L
-homomorphism
\phi:L_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
, we write
\phi(l)=(a_{\phi}(l),w(l))
.

The set
\Phi(G)
of
L
-parameters of
G
is defined to be the set of equivalence classes of
G
-relevant
L
-homomorphisms
\phi:L_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
. We have the subset
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\subseteq\Phi(G)
of tempered
L
-parameters of
G
the further subset
\Phi_{2}(G)\subseteq\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
of discrete
L
-parameters of
G
, which are those that are tempered and elliptic.

Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an
L
-homomorphism. For each
\phi\in\Phi(H)
we obtain an equivalence class of
L
-homomorphisms
\xi_{*}\phi=\xi\circ\phi:L_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
, which only depends on the equivalence class of
\xi
. We thus obtain a partially defined map
\xi_{*}:\Phi(H)\to\Phi(G)
, whose domain is the set
\phi\in\Phi(H)
such that
\xi_{*}\phi
is
G
-relevant. If
G
is quasisplit or
\xi
is an isomorphism, then
\xi_{*}
is defined on all of
\Phi(H)
. If
\xi=\prescript{L}{}{\eta}
for a normal homomorphism
\eta:G\to H
with abelian kernel and cokernel, then
\xi_{*}
is also defined on all of
\Phi(H)
.

4.2.1.
Central and cocentral characters and the Langlands pairing.

In order to formulate various properties of the local Langlands correspondence we need two constructions originally due to Langlands
[
Lan89
]
. A more intrinsic approach is given by Kaletha in
[
Kal15
]
using the cohomology of crossed modules. The first is the central character map

\zeta:H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)\longrightarrow\Pi(Z_{G}(F)).

The classes
H_{c,\mathrm{bdd}}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)
represented by bounded 1-cocycles map into the group
\Pi_{u}(Z_{G}(F))
of unitary central characters. We have a map
\Phi(G)\to H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)
defined by
\phi\mapsto a_{\phi}
, and this restricts to a map
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\to H_{c,\mathrm{bdd}}^{1}(W_{F},G^{\vee}\!)
. Thus, we have a central character map

\displaystyle\zeta:\Phi_{(\mathrm{temp})}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Pi_{(u)}(Z_{G}(F)).

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\zeta_{\phi}

For a central datum
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
of
G(F)
, we can thus define
\Phi(G,\zeta)=\{\phi\in\Phi(G):\zeta_{\phi}|_{\mathcal{Z}}=\zeta\}
. We define
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)=\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\cap\Phi(G,\zeta)
and
\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)=\Phi_{2}(G)\cap\Phi(G,\zeta)
, which are empty unless
\zeta
is unitary.

The second construction is the cocentral character homomorphism

\displaystyle\chi:H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Hom_{c}(G(F),\mathbb{C}^{\times})

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\longmapsto\chi_{a}

The corresponding pairing
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))\times G(F)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is called the Langlands pairing. (See also
[
KS99
, §5.1]
and Appendix A by Labesse and Lapid in
[
LM15
]
.) Let
Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{1}
denote the maximal compact subgroup of
Z(G^{\vee}\!)
. The cocentral character homomorphism restricts to a homomorphism

\chi:H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{1})\longrightarrow\Hom_{c}(G(F),\mathbb{C}^{1}),

and thus we have the corresponding pairing
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{1})\times G(F)\to\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Following
[
KP23
]
, we write
G(F)^{\natural}=\im(G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F)\to G(F))
, where
G_{\mathrm{sc}}\to G_{\mathrm{der}}
is the simply connected cover of the derived group of
G
. The construction in
[
Kal15
]
makes it clear that the cocentral character homomorphism has image in the group
\Hom_{c}(G(F)/G(F)^{\natural},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
of cocentral characters of
G
.

The cocentral character homomorphism and its unitary restriction are isomorphisms if
G
is quasisplit, in particular if
G
is a torus. The homomorphism is injective if
F
is non-archimedean. It is surjective if
G_{\mathrm{sc}}(F)
is perfect, or equivalently if
F
is archimedean or
G_{\mathrm{sc}}
does not contain a simple factor of the form
\Res_{E/F}(\SL_{1}(D))
for a finite-dimensional non-commutative division algebra
D
over a finite separable (this works over positive characteristic) extension
E
of
F
. (See Appendix A by Labesse and Lapid in
[
LM15
]
.)

4.2.2.
The local Langlands correspondence for tori.

Suppose that
G=T
is a torus. Then we have a bijection
\Phi(T)\cong H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},T^{\vee}\!)
defined by
\phi\mapsto a_{\phi}
, and we transport the group structure from
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},T^{\vee}\!)
to
\Phi(T)
so that this bijection becomes a group isomorphism. It restricts to an isomorphism
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(T)=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},(T^{\vee}\!)^{1})
, where
(T^{\vee}\!)^{1}
is the maximal compact subgroup of
T^{\vee}\!
. Furthermore, we have
\Pi(T)=\Hom_{c}(T(F),\mathbb{C}^{\times})
and
\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(T)=\Hom_{c}(T(F),\mathbb{C}^{1})
. The cocentral character homomorphism in this case thus gives us an isomorphism
\rec_{T}:\Phi(T)\to\Pi(T)
, which restricts to an isomorphism
\rec_{T}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(T)\to\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(T)
. It is called the local Langlands correspondence or local reciprocity map for tori and was first constructed in
[
Lan97
]
. (See
[
Bor79
, §9]
for an overview and
[
Lab85
]
for a slightly different approach.)

4.2.3.
Unramified characters.

Let
W_{F}^{1}=\ker(|\cdot|:W_{F}\to\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and consider

H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})=\Hom_{c}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}).

We call the homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(G(F),\mathbb{C}^{\times})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

the unramified cocentral character homomorphism. Its image is the group
X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
of unramified characters of
G
, and we recall why this is the case by relating it to the isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G),\lambda\mapsto|\cdot|_{G}^{\lambda}
.

Recall the canonical isomorphism

\displaystyle X^{*}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X_{*}(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\longmapsto\theta^{\vee}

which comes from Langlands duality. This induces an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}=X^{*}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

which we write as
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{\vee}\!
. Here, we have used that for any complex torus
T
we have identifications

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(T)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}\otimes\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we define
\|\cdot\|^{\lambda^{\vee}\!}\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})
by

\|w\|^{\lambda^{\vee}\!}=\exp((\log\|w\|)\lambda^{\vee}\!)

for all
w\in W_{F}
. This defines a homomorphism

\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}).

For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, let
a_{\lambda}
be the image of
\|\cdot\|^{\lambda^{\vee}\!}
in
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
, yielding a homomorphism

\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\longrightarrow H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)).

We have

\chi_{a_{\lambda}}=|\cdot|_{G}^{\lambda}.

(See the discussion below 4.7 in
[
SZ18
]
.) Thus, the unramified cocentral character homomorphism maps onto
X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
and the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)}

We will now proceed to describe the unramified cocentral character homomorphism and the homomorphism
\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
in more detail. In particular, we will see that the kernel of
\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
is trivial when
F
is archimedean, and is equal to
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
when
F
is non-archimedean. We will make use of these facts in the following subsection.

Suppose that
F
is archimedean. We have
W_{F}/W_{F}^{1}=\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and thus

H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}}).

We may identify this with
\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}})=\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})
. The homomorphism
\lambda\mapsto\|\cdot\|^{\lambda^{\vee}}
is then identified with the isomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}}),\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{\vee}
. The homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
is also an isomorphism. Consequently, the unramified cocentral character homomorphism is identified with an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The homomorphism
W_{F}\to\Gamma_{F}
maps
W_{F}^{1}
onto
\Gamma_{F}
. Therefore
Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}}=Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{W_{F}^{1}}
. The unramified cocentral character homomorphism is the restriction of the cocentral character homomorphism along the inflation homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}})=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{W_{F}^{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)).}

In summary, we have the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(G(F)/G(F)^{\natural},\mathbb{C}^{\times})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

The composition on the left is the homomorphism
\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
, which is thus injective.

Suppose that
F
is non-archimedean. We have
W_{F}^{1}=I_{F}
and
W_{F}/W_{F}^{1}=\langle\mathrm{Fr}\rangle
and the norm
\|\cdot\|:W_{F}/W_{F}^{1}\to q_{F}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is defined by
\|\mathrm{Fr}\|=q_{F}
. We may identify

H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/W_{F}^{1},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})=Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}.

The homomorphism
\lambda\mapsto\|\cdot\|^{\lambda^{\vee}}
is then identified with the surjective homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\twoheadrightarrow Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ},\lambda\mapsto q_{F}^{\lambda^{\vee}}
, which descends to an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\frac{2\pi i}{\log q_{F}}X^{*}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
has kernel
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
, and the kernel of the unramified cocentral character homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/I_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)}

is the finite subgroup of
Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}
corresponding to the subgroup

\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\frac{2\pi i}{\log q_{F}}X^{*}(G)

of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\frac{2\pi i}{\log q_{F}}X^{*}(G)
.

As in
[
Hai14
, §3.3]
, we recall how the unramified cocentral character homomorphism can be described using Kottwitz homomorphism for
G
, which was introduced in
[
Kot97
]
. (See
[
KP23
, Ch. 11]
for a detailed exposition.) The Kottwitz homomorphism for
G
is a surjective homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}:G(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{*}(Z(G^{\vee}\!))_{I_{F}})^{\mathrm{Fr}}=X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}).}

Its kernel
G(F)_{1}=\ker\kappa_{G}
is an open subgroup of
G(F)^{1}
, and
G(F)_{1}
is the subgroup of
G(F)
generated by its parahoric subgroups. A (continuous) character of
G(F)
is said to be weakly unramified if it is trivial on
G(F)_{1}
. We denote the group of weakly unramified characters of
G(F)
by

X^{\mathrm{wnr}}(G)=\Hom(G(F)/G(F)_{1},\mathbb{C}^{\times}).

Since Kottwitz homomorphism is functorial and trivial on simply connected groups
[
KP23
, Proof of Prop. 11.5.4]
, it follows that
G(F)^{\natural}\subseteq G(F)_{1}
. Therefore weakly unramified characters are cocentral. By definition we have an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}:G(F)/G(F)_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Consequently, we obtain an isomorphism defined by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}),\mathbb{C}^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{wnr}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}^{*}}

As explained in
[
Hai14
, §3.3.1]
, it follows from
[
Kal15
, Prop. 4.5.2]
that this isomorphism is the restriction of the cocentral character homomorphism
a\mapsto\chi_{a}
along the inflation homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/I_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)).}

If we further restrict the cocentral character homomorphism to

(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}\coloneqq((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}})^{\circ},

then we obtain an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

This follows since the Kottwitz homomorphism descends to an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}:G(F)/G(F)^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}})/{\Tor}=X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
\Tor
denotes the torsion subgroup, and therefore
\chi
restricts to the isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(X^{*}((Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}),\mathbb{C}^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{G}^{*}}

The unramified cocentral character homomorphism factors as the composition of the surjective homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}}

with the restriction of the cocentral character homomorphism to
(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}
. Surjectivity of the above homomorphism can be seen as a consequence of the following commutative diagram, which summarises the above discussion.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\mathrm{wnr}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(G(F)/G(F)^{\natural},\mathbb{C}^{\times})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

The homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
in this diagram is the the homomorphism
\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
. It follows that its image is
(Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{I_{F}})_{\mathrm{Fr}}^{\circ}
and its kernel is
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
.

4.2.4.
Twists.

Let
a\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
and
\phi\in\Phi(G)
. Recall that we write
\phi(l)=(a_{\phi}(l),w(l))
. We define
a\cdot\phi\in\Phi(G)
by
(a\cdot\phi)(l)=(a(w(l))a_{\phi}(l),w(l))
, and this gives a well-defined action of the group
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
on the pointed set
\Phi(G)
. The action of
H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{1})
preserves
\Phi_{2}(G)
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
.

Pulling back along the homomorphism
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\to H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!)),\lambda\mapsto a_{\lambda}
, we obtain an action of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
on
\Phi(G)
, and the action of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
preserves
\Phi_{2}(G)
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
. We define

\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\quad,\quad\Phi_{2}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\Phi_{2}(G).

The set
\Phi_{2}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
is precisely the set of elements of
\Phi(G)
that are elliptic but not necessarily tempered. We call the elements of
\Phi_{2}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
essentially discrete
L
-parameters and the elements of
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
essentially tempered
L
-parameters. We caution the reader that sometimes in the literature
\Phi_{2}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\Phi_{2}(G)
are denoted by
\Phi_{2}(G)
and
\Phi_{2,\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, respectively.
