4.2.5.
Infinitesimal characters and
L
-parameters of real groups.

Assume that
G
is a real group. Let
\phi\in\Phi(G)
. As explained in
[
Lan89
]
, one can attach an infinitesimal character
\mu_{\phi}\in\Hom_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{-alg}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}),\mathbb{C})
of
G
to
\phi
as follows. Choose a Borel pair
(B,T)
of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
and a
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
-stable Borel pair
(\mathcal{B}^{0},\mathcal{T}^{0})
of
G^{\vee}\!
. We choose a representative of
\phi
such that
\phi(\mathbb{C}^{\times})\subseteq\mathcal{T}^{0}
. There exists
\mu,\nu\in X_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\Lie(\mathcal{T}^{0})
with
\mu-\nu\in X_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0})
such that
\phi(z)=z^{\mu}\overline{z}^{\nu}
for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. The
W(G^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}^{0})
-orbit of
\mu
does not depend on the choice of representative of
\phi
. The Borel pairs and the identification
\Psi(G)^{\vee}=\Psi(G^{\vee}\!)
give us an isomorphism
X_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0})\cong X^{*}(T)
and a compatible isomorphism
W(G^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}^{0})\cong W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
. Using the identification
\mathfrak{t}^{*}=\Lie(T)^{*}
with
X^{*}(T)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
, we obtain an isomorphism
\Lie(\mathcal{T}^{0})/W(G^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}^{0})\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
. Thus, we obtain an element
\mu_{\phi}\in\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
. Recall that the Harish-Chandra isomorphism
\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})\cong\Sym(\mathfrak{t})^{W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)}
gives an isomorphism
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}\mathrm{-alg}}(\mathfrak{Z}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}),\mathbb{C})\cong\mathfrak{t}^{*}/W(G_{\mathbb{C}},T)
. Thus, we have an infinitesimal character
\mu_{\phi}
of
G
. It does not depend on any of the choices made.

4.2.6.
Classification of
L
-parameters.

For each
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
, recall that we have a canonical equivalence class of tempered
L
-homomorphisms
\iota_{M}^{G}:\prescript{L}{}{M}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
. This gives rise to a pushforward map
\iota_{M}^{G}=(\iota_{M}^{G})_{*}
on
L
-parameters preserving temperedness. The images of
\Phi_{2}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
in
\Phi(G)
and
\Phi_{2}(M)
in
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
are both quotients for the natural actions of
W^{G}(M)
. We have a decomposition

\Phi(G)=\coprod_{M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\Phi_{2}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}/W^{G}(M)

and this restricts to a decomposition

\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)=\coprod_{M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\Phi_{2}(M)/W^{G}(M).

This latter decomposition is an analogue for tempered
L
-parameters of the Harish-Chandra classification of irreducible tempered representations. The first decomposition is analogous to the classification of irreducible admissible representations obtained by combining the Langlands classification with the Harish-Chandra classification.

Implicit in
[
Lan89
]
is a classification of
\Phi(G)
that is analogous to the Langlands classification of
\Pi(G)
. This was elaborated on and given an explicit formulation and detailed proof in
[
SZ18
]
. We define a Langlands datum for
L
-parameters to be a triple
((P,M),\phi,\lambda)
, where
(P,M)
is a parabolic pair,
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(M)
, and
\lambda\in(\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*})^{P,>0}
. To each Langlands datum
((P,M),\phi,\lambda)
, we may assign an
L
-parameter
\iota_{M}^{G}(\phi_{\lambda})\in\Phi(G)
and the Langlands classification for
L
-parameters asserts that this gives a bijection between the set of
G(F)
-conjugacy classes of Langlands data and
\Phi(G)
. See
[
SZ18
]
for more details and a description of the inverse of this bijection.

4.3.
The local Langlands correspondence

The local Langlands correspondence for
G
, which is still hypothetical for
p
-adic groups in general, is a natural surjective “local reciprocity” map

\rec_{G}:\Pi(G)\longrightarrow\Phi(G)

with finite fibres. The fibre of
\rec_{G}
above an
L
-parameter
\phi\in\Phi(G)
is called the
L
-packet of
\phi
and denoted by
\Pi_{\phi}
. The local reciprocity map
\rec_{G}
is natural in the sense that it satisfies several desiderata, which say that
\rec_{G}
is compatible with various structures (cf.
[
Bor79
,
KT
]
).

Desiderata 4.1
.

(1)

Compatibility with central characters: for each
\pi\in\Pi(G)
, we have
\zeta_{\pi}=\zeta_{\phi_{\pi}}
.

(2)

Compatibility with cocentral twists: for each representation
\pi\in\Pi(G)
and cohomology class
a\in H^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
, we have
\phi_{\pi\otimes\chi_{a}}=a\cdot\phi_{\pi}
. In particular, we have compatibility with unramified twists: for each
\pi\in\Pi(G)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have
\phi_{\pi_{\lambda}}=(\phi_{\pi})_{\lambda}
.

(3)

Compatibility with temperedness and discreteness: for
\pi\in\Pi(G)
, we have
\pi\in\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
(resp.
\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
) if and only if
\phi_{\pi}\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
(resp.
\phi_{\pi}\in\Phi_{2}(G)
).

(4)

Naturality: if
\eta:G\to H
is a normal homomorphism with abelian kernel and cokernel and if
\pi\in\Pi(H)
, then the irreducible constituents of the restriction
\pi\circ\eta
, which is a direct sum of finitely many irreducible admissible representations of
G(F)
, all belong to
\Pi_{\prescript{L}{}{\eta}\circ\phi}
. In particular, if
\eta:G\to H
is an isomorphism, then the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(\eta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rec_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rec_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\eta)}

where
\Pi(\eta)=\eta^{*}
and
\Phi(\eta)=(\prescript{L}{}{\eta})_{*}
.

(5)

Compatibility with Weil restriction: If
K/F
is a finite subextension of
\overline{F}/F
,
G^{\prime}
is a connected reductive group over
K
, and
G=\Res_{K/F}G^{\prime}
, then we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(G^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(G^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rec_{G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rec_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the upper horizontal arrow is the isomorphism coming from the identification
G(F)=G^{\prime}(K)
and the lower horizontal arrow is a canonical bijection coming from Shapiro’s lemma. See
[
Bor79
, §4, §5, §8.4]
for more details.

(6)

Compatibility with the Langlands classification of
\Pi(G)
: if
((P,M),\phi,\lambda)
is a Langlands triple, then
\Pi_{\iota_{M}^{G}(\phi)}
is the set of Langlands quotients
J((P,M),\sigma,\lambda)\in\Pi(G)
, for
\sigma\in\Pi_{\phi}
.

(7)

Compatibility with the Harish-Chandra classification of
\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
: if
M
is a Levi subgroup of
G
and
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(M)
, then
\Pi_{\iota_{M}^{G}(\phi)}
is the set of irreducible subrepresentations of the various parabolically induced representations
I_{M}^{G}(\sigma)
for
\sigma\in\Pi_{\phi}
.

(8)

Stable tempered characters: for each
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, there exists a stable virtual tempered representation

\pi_{\phi}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}c_{\phi,\pi}\pi

with
c_{\phi,\pi}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
for all
\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}
, such that the following properties hold.

(a)

Compatibility with parabolic induction: If
M
is a Levi subgroup of
G
and
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(M)
, then
I_{M}^{G}(\pi_{\phi})=\pi_{\iota_{M}^{G}(\phi)}
.

(b)

The set
\{\pi_{\phi}:\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\}
is a basis of the space
D_{\mathrm{temp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
of stable tempered virtual representations of
G
. Consequently, the set
\{\pi_{\phi}:\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G)\}
is a basis of the space
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
.

The character
\Theta_{\phi}=\Theta_{\pi_{\phi}}
of
\pi_{\phi}
is a stable tempered distribution called the stable tempered character of
\phi
, and called a stable discrete series character if
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G)
. Note that the stable tempered virtual representations are linearly independent. We will often identify
\phi=\pi_{\phi}=\Theta_{\phi}
.

(9)

Compatibility with infinitesimal characters in the case
F=\mathbb{R}
: for each
\pi\in\Pi(G)
, we have
\mu_{\pi}=\mu_{\phi_{\pi}}
.

It follows from
Item

5
that the local Langlands correspondence for groups over arbitrary local fields of characteristic zero is determined by and can be constructed from the local Langlands correspondence for groups over
F=\mathbb{R}
or
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
for a prime
p
. It follows from the Langlands classification of admissible representations and the parallel Langlands classification of
L
-parameters that a local Langlands correspondence
\rec_{G}:\Pi(G)\to\Phi(G)
satisfying
Desiderata

4.1
is determined by its restricted tempered local Langlands correspondence
\rec_{G}:\Pi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)\to\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
. Furthermore, it follows from what we have called the Harish-Chandra classification of tempered representations and the parallel Harish-Chandra classification of tempered
L
-parameters that a local Langlands correspondence satisfying
Desiderata

4.1
is determined by its restricted discrete local Langlands correspondence
\rec_{G}:\Pi_{2}(G)\to\Phi_{2}(G)
. Conversely, given a discrete local reciprocity map
\rec_{G}:\Pi_{2}(G)\to\Phi_{2}(G)
satisfying the parts of
Desiderata

4.1
that make sense, one can construct a local reciprocity map
\rec_{G}:\Pi(G)\to\Phi(G)
satisfying
Desiderata

4.1
. (See
[
Bor79
, §11.7]
, and
[
KT
, §6.1.2]
for more on this.)

Note that
\Pi_{\phi}
can be recovered from
\Theta_{\phi}
since the characters of irreducible admissible representations are linearly dependent. Thus the local Langlands correspondence is determined by the parametrisation
\phi\mapsto\Theta_{\phi}
of stable discrete series characters and can be constructed by defining stable discrete series characters in such a way that the associated discrete reciprocity map satisfies
Desiderata

4.1
.

For real groups, and thus for complex groups by restriction of scalars, Langlands constructed the local Langlands correspondence in the above manner. Suppose that
G
is a connected reductive group over
\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G)
. Langlands showed that
G
has an elliptic maximal torus
T
. Moreover, a choice of a Borel subgroup
B
of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
containing
T
determines an
L
-embedding
\prescript{L}{}{T}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
through which
\phi
factors as a tempered
L
-parameter of
\phi_{T}\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(T)
. The unitary character of
\theta_{\phi}
of
T(\mathbb{R})
corresponding to
\phi_{T}
under the local Langlands correspondence for tori is dominant with respect to
B
and its orbit under the absolute Weyl group
W(G,T)
does not depend on
B
. In Harish-Chandra’s papers on discrete series, he defined a stable tempered distribution
\Theta_{\theta_{\phi}}
that is uniquely determined by its formula on the set
T(\mathbb{R})^{\prime}
of regular elements in
T(\mathbb{R})
:

\Theta_{\theta_{\phi}}(t)=(-1)^{q(G)}\sum_{w\in W(G,T)}\frac{\theta_{\phi}(w^{-1}\cdot t)}{\prod_{\alpha>_{B}0}(1-\alpha(w^{-1}\cdot t)^{-1})},

where
q(G)=1/2\dim(G(\mathbb{R})/K)
for any maximal compact subgroup
K
of
G(\mathbb{R})
and
\alpha>_{B}0
indicates that
\alpha
is a positive root with respect to the positive system determined by
B
. Harish-Chandra showed that
\Theta_{\theta_{\phi}}
is the sum of
\Theta_{\pi}
for all
\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
with
\zeta_{\pi}=\theta_{\phi}|_{Z_{G}(\mathbb{R})}
and
\mu_{\pi}=d\theta_{\phi}
. Note that we have
\zeta_{\phi}=\theta_{\phi}|_{Z_{G}(\mathbb{R})}
and
\mu_{\phi}=d\theta_{\phi}
. Langlands constructed the local Langlands correspondence for
G
by requiring that
\Theta_{\phi}=\Theta_{\theta_{\phi}}
. Consequently, we have that
\Pi_{\phi}
consists of all
\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
with infinitesimal character
\mu_{\pi}=\mu_{\phi}
and central character
\zeta_{\pi}=\zeta_{\phi}
, and we have
\Theta_{\phi}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}\Theta_{\pi}
. In fact, in
[
AV16
]
Adams and Vogan prove that
\Pi_{\phi}
consists of all
\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
with infinitesimal character
\mu_{\pi}=\mu_{\phi}
and what they call “split radical character”
\zeta_{\pi}|_{A_{G}(\mathbb{R})}=\zeta_{\phi}|_{A_{G}(\mathbb{R})}
. For
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G)
, the cardinality of
\Pi_{\phi}
is bounded by
|W(G,T)/W_{\mathbb{R}}(G,T)|
, where
T
is an elliptic torus of
G
. For
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, we also have
\pi_{\phi}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}\pi
. This follows as in the proof of
[
She79
, Lemma 3.1]
using
[
Kna76
, Theorem]
and
[
SV80
, Theorem 2.9]
.
Item

8b
is a corollary of
[
ABV92
, Lemma 18.11]
, which follows from the proof of
[
She79
, Lemma 5.2]
. The
L
-packets for complex groups are singletons, which agrees with the fact that conjugacy and stable conjugacy coincide for complex groups.

For non-archimedean groups, it is expected that stable discrete series characters
\Theta_{\phi}
can be constructed directly in terms of
\phi
, as was the case for real groups. This would then give rise to construction of the local Langlands correspondence and a characterisation of it. (See
[
Kal21
,
Kal23
]
for further discussion.)

We will make use of the following hypothesis on the local Langlands correspondence for non-archimedean groups.

Hypothesis 4.2
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over a non-archimedean local field
F
. The group
G
and its Levi subgroups have local reciprocity maps satisfying the following subset of
Desiderata

4.1
:
Item

1
,
Item

2
in the special case of unramified twists,
Item

3
,
Item

4
in the special case when
\eta
is of the form
\Int(g):M\to g\cdot M
for some
g\in G(F)
and
M
a Levi subgroup of
G
,
Item

6
,
Item

7
, and
Item

8
.

Hypothesis

4.2
is known for tori. The local reciprocity map is the cocentral character isomorphism given by the Langlands pairing and the stable tempered character of a tempered
L
-parameter can be taken to simply be the corresponding character.

In the case
G=\GL_{n}
,
Hypothesis

4.2
was proved by Harris and Taylor, Henniart, and Scholze
[
HT01
,
Hen00
,
Sch13
]
. The
L
-packets are singletons and the stable tempered characters are simply the irreducible tempered characters.

If
G
is an inner form of a quasisplit symplectic, odd special orthogonal, unitary, or odd general spin group, then
Hypothesis

4.2
is satisfied. This follows from the works of Arthur, Mok, and Moeglin
[
Art13
,
Mok15
,
Mg14
]
, which use the theory of twisted endoscopy and the local Langlands correspondence for
\GL_{n}
. See
[
Var24
]
, where this is established carefully.

From now on, if
F
is non-archimedean we assume
Hypothesis

4.2
for all connected reductive groups over
F
.

4.3.1.
Infinitesimal characters for
p
-adic groups.

Suppose that
F
is archimedean. An infinitesimal character (also called an infinitesimal parameter) of
G
is defined to be a
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy class of
L
-homomorphisms
\mu:W_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
. This notion originates in
[
Vog93
]
. Another useful reference is
[
CFM+22
]
. Every
L
-parameter
\phi:L_{F}=\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})\times W_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
of
G
has an associated infinitesimal character
\mu_{\phi}:W_{F}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
defined by
\mu_{\phi}(w)=\phi(d_{w},w)
, where
d_{w}=\diag(\|w\|^{1/2},\|w\|^{-1/2})
. Each
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
can be recovered from its infinitesimal character
\mu_{\phi}

[
Var24
, §6.6]
. In general, there are at most finitely many elements of
\Phi(G)
with a given infinitesimal character
[
Vog93
, Corollary 4.6]
.

For each
\pi\in\Pi(G)
, we define its infinitesimal character
\mu_{\pi}
by
\mu_{\pi}=\mu_{\phi_{\pi}}
. It follows from the finiteness of
L
-packets that for each infinitesimal character
\mu
of
G
, there are finitely many
\pi\in\Pi(G)
with
\mu_{\pi}=\mu
.

Let
M
be a Levi subgroup of
G
. If
\mu
is an infinitesimal character of
M
, then
\iota_{M}^{G}\circ\mu
is an infinitesimal character of
G
. Moreover, if
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are infinitesimal characters of
M
, then
\iota_{M}^{G}\circ\mu_{1}=\iota_{M}^{G}\circ\mu_{2}
if and only if
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are in the same
W^{G}(M)
-orbit.

For each
\tau=(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})\in T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, we define its infinitesimal character to be
\mu_{\tau}=\iota_{M}^{G}\circ\mu_{\sigma}
. Note that
\mu_{\tau}
only depends on the image of
\tau
in
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
. There are at most finitely many elements in
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
with a given infinitesimal character.

We have an action of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
on the set of infinitesimal characters by
(\lambda,\mu)\mapsto a_{\lambda}\cdot\mu
. The assignment of infinitesimal characters to elements of
\Pi(G)
(resp.
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
) is equivariant with respect to the actions of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
(resp.
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
).

4.4.
Stable Paley–Wiener Theorems

4.4.1.
The stable Fourier transform.

For
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
, we define its stable Fourier transform to be the function
f^{G}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
f^{G}(\phi)=\Theta_{\phi}(f)
.

We define the space of stable Fourier transforms

\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)=\{f^{G}:f\in\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\}.

We have

\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)/\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{\Theta_{\phi}:\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\})

and we give it the natural quotient topology. The stable Fourier transform is a continuous surjective linear map
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)
.

A simple consequence of the Harish-Chandra regularity theorem and the stable Weyl integration formula is the following. Let
\phi\in\Phi(G,\zeta)
(resp.
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
). If
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
), then
f^{G}(\phi)=0
. Consequently, we have
\Theta_{\phi}\in\mathcal{S}_{c}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
(resp.
\Theta_{\phi}\in\mathcal{S}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
).

It follows that the stable Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\to\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta)
descends to a continuous surjective linear map

\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}_{(c)}}(G,\zeta).

We call the property of injectivity of this map stable spectral density for
\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
. Stable spectral density for
\mathcal{S}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
is equivalent to

\mathcal{C}_{(c)}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=\Ann_{\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)}(\{\Theta_{\phi}:\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)\}).

It follows from stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)
that a distribution in
\mathcal{C}_{(c)}(G,\zeta)^{\prime}
is stable if and only if it lies in the weak-
*
closure of the linear span of
\{\Theta_{\phi}\}_{\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)}
.

For archimedean
F
, stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
was proved by Shelstad in
[
She79
, Lemma 5.3]
for
\mathcal{Z}=1
and in
[
She08
, Theorem 4.1]
for
\mathcal{Z}=Z(F)
, where
Z
is a central torus of
G
. For non-archimedean
F
, stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}_{c}(G,\zeta)
is an immediate consequence of
[
Art96
, Theorem 6.1, Theorem 6.2]
when
G
is quasi-split and
\mathcal{Z}=Z(F)
for
Z
a central induced torus of
G
. The assumption that
G
is quasisplit can be removed using endoscopic transfer between a non-quasisplit group
G
and its quasisplit inner form
G^{*}
, as explained in
[
Var24
, §3.2]
. Arthur’s proof uses a global argument involving the simple trace formula, and it is generalised to the twisted setting in
[
MW16b
, XI.5.2]
in the case of a trivial central character. The reader may find this a helpful reference since the spectral density result and proof are stated explicitly there. Thus, we have the following result.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{Z},\zeta)
be a unitary central datum with
\mathcal{Z}=Z(F)
for
Z
a central induced torus of
G
. Stable spectral density for
C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
holds.

In particular, we have stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}_{c}(G)
and
C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
when
Z=A_{G}(F)
, and we will use these to prove the stable Paley–Wiener theorems below. Stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}(G)
in the non-archimedean case does not appear to be in the literature. It is established below (
Theorem

4.17
) in the course of the proof of the stable Paley–Wiener theorem for
\mathcal{C}(G)
.

4.4.2.
The space of
L
-parameters.

In order to define Paley–Wiener and Schwartz spaces on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, we must recall how it is naturally a topological space. Recall that we have an action of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
on
\Phi(G)
, and that the action of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
preserves
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
and
\Phi_{2}(G)
. We denote the isotropy subgroup of
\phi\in\Phi(G,\zeta)
in
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
by
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}
. Just as was the case for
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\tau}^{\vee}
, we have

\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}

Thus, if
F
is archimedean we have
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}=0
, and if
F
is non-archimedean we have that
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}
is a full lattice in
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
. Consequently,
\Phi_{2}(G)
is naturally a smooth manifold with countably many components
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot\phi=i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}
, which are Euclidean spaces if
F
is archimedean and compact tori if
F
is non-archimedean. Moreover,
\Phi_{2}(G)_{\mathbb{C}}
is the complexification of
\Phi_{2}(G)
with connected components
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cdot\phi=\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,\phi}^{\vee}
. Since

\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)=\coprod_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\Phi_{2}(L)/W^{G}(L),

we have that
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, and similarly
\Phi(G)
is naturally a topological space. We say that a function on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
is smooth if it pulls back to a smooth function on each
\Phi_{2}(L)
.

4.4.3.
The stable Paley–Wiener Theorems.

We define
\|\phi\|=\|\mu_{\phi}\|
in the archimedean case. Note that for any countable set
E\subseteq\Phi_{2}(L)
, we have Paley–Wiener and Schwartz spaces defined on the space
\Lambda=\bigcup_{\phi\in E}\Lambda_{\phi}
with
\Lambda_{\phi}=i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\phi}^{\vee}
.

We define
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)
to be the space of smooth functions
\varphi:\Phi_{2}(L)
such that for some (and hence any) set of representatives
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)\subseteq\Phi_{2}(L)
for the connected components of
\Phi_{2}(L)
, we have

\varphi\in\mathscr{S}\Bigg(\coprod_{\phi\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)}i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\phi}^{\vee}\Bigg).

We define
PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)
(resp.
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)
) in the same way as
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)
, except that we replace
\mathscr{S}(\cdot)
by
PW(\cdot)
(resp.
PW_{f}(\cdot)
).

We define

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)\Bigg)^{W_{0}^{G}}=\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)^{W^{G}(L)}

and similarly we define
PW^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
and
PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. These are naturally spaces of smooth functions on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
.

Example 4.1
.

We describe the above spaces explicitly in the case when
G=\SL_{2}
and
F=\mathbb{R}
. Let
T=\SO_{2}
, an anisotropic maximal torus of
\SL_{2}
. We write the elements of
T(\mathbb{R})
as

t(\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}.

Let
S
be the subgroup of diagonal elements of
\SL_{2}
, a split maximal torus. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
, we write
s(x)=\diag(x,x^{-1})\in S(\mathbb{R})
. The set
\{S,\SL_{2}\}
is a set of representatives for the conjugacy classes of Levi subgroups of
G
. The Weyl group
W_{0}^{G}=W^{G}(S)
is of order two, with the non-trivial element acting by
s(x)\mapsto s(x^{-1})
.

The space
\Phi_{2}(G)=\{\phi_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is countable and discrete, with
\phi_{n}
denoting the
L
-parameter whose
L
-packet consists of the discrete series representations
\pi_{\pm n}
, whose characters are given on the regular elements of
T(\mathbb{R})
by the formula

\Theta_{\pi_{\pm n}}(t(\theta))=-\frac{\pm e^{\pm in\theta}}{e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}}.

The stable discrete series character of
\phi_{n}
is

\Theta_{\phi_{n}}(t(\theta))=-\frac{e^{in\theta}-e^{-in\theta}}{e^{i\theta}-e^{-i\theta}}.

We identify
\Phi_{2}(G)=\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
via
\phi_{n}\mapsto n
. We have

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

the space of rapidly decreasing functions
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore,

PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=C_{c}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

the space of finitely supported functions
\varphi:\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\to\mathbb{C}
.

By the local Langlands correspondence for
S
, we may identify
\Phi_{2}(S)
with
\Pi_{u}(S)
, the set of unitary characters of
S(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}^{\times}
. The unitary unramified characters of
S(\mathbb{R})
are those of the form
s(x)\mapsto|x|^{i\lambda}
for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. An arbitrary unitary character of
S(\mathbb{R})
can be written as

\chi_{m,\lambda}(s(x))=\sgn(x)^{m}|x|^{i\lambda},

for unique
m\in\{0,1\}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. The non-trivial element of
W^{G}(S)
acts
\Pi_{u}(S)
by
\chi_{m,\lambda}\mapsto\chi_{m,-\lambda}
. We identify
\Phi_{2}(S)=\{0,1\}\times\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}\coprod\mathbb{R}
via
\chi_{m,\lambda}\mapsto(m,\lambda)
. Then
\Phi_{2}(S)/W_{0}^{G}=\mathbb{R}/\{\pm 1\}\coprod\mathbb{R}/\{\pm 1\}=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\coprod\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. We have

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)=\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})\oplus\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})

and

\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)^{W^{G}(S)}=\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}\oplus\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}.

Similarly, we have

PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)=PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)=PW(\mathbb{R})\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})

and

PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)^{W^{G}(S)}=PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(S)^{W^{G}(S)}=PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0})\oplus\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}\oplus\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}},

\displaystyle PW^{\mathrm{st}}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0})\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}},

\displaystyle PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)

\displaystyle=C_{c}(\mathbb{Z}_{>0})\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}\oplus PW(\mathbb{R})^{\{\pm 1\}}.

The aim of this section is to prove the following result, which contains three stable Paley–Wiener theorems. This was stated as
Theorem

1.2
in
Section

1
.

Theorem 4.2
.

If
F
is non-archimedean, we assume
Hypothesis

4.2
for
G
. The stable Fourier transform is an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{S}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)

and restricts to isomorphisms of topological vector spaces

\mathcal{S}_{c}(G)\longrightarrow PW^{\mathrm{st}}(G)

and

\mathcal{S}_{f}(G)\longrightarrow PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G).

Arthur proved the stable Paley–Wiener theorem for test functions on a quasisplit
p
-adic group as a consequence of his more general result
[
Art96
, Theorem 6.1]
on collective endoscopic transfer (see the discussion below the statement of Theorem 6.2 in
[
Art96
]
). Moeglin–Waldspurger proved the stable Paley–Wiener theorem for test functions and
K
-finite test functions on a quasisplit real group (see the theorem in
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV, §4.2.3]
and the corollary in
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV, §4.2.9]
). In fact, they work in the more general setting of twisted spaces (also called twisted groups). What is new in our theorem is the stable Paley–Wiener theorem for Schwartz functions, i.e. the first isomorphism in our theorem, and the treatment of groups that are not quasisplit. Generalising Arthur’s proof to Schwartz functions would require generalising several results that we do not need. Instead, we follow the strategy in the proof by Moeglin–Waldspurger, which is simplified because we work with groups (not general twisted groups) and make use of stable spectral density for test functions. This necessitates revisiting the cases of test functions and
K
-finite test functions.

Before turning to the proofs of the stable Paley–Wiener theorems, we remark that there is a stable Fourier inversion theorem for stable orbital integrals. Define a measure on
\Phi_{2}(L)
for each
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
by

\int_{\Phi_{2}(L)}\alpha(\phi)\!\mathop{}d{\phi}=\sum_{\phi\in\Phi_{2}(L)/i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}}\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{L}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{L,\phi}^{\vee}}\alpha(\phi_{\lambda})\!\mathop{}d{\lambda},

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(\Phi_{2}(L))
. Then, define a measure on
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
by

\int_{\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)}\alpha(\phi)\!\mathop{}d{\phi}=\sum_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}|W^{G}(L)|^{-1}\int_{\Phi_{2}(L)}\alpha(\phi)\!\mathop{}d{\phi}

for all
\alpha\in C_{c}(\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G))
. As in the proof of
[
Art96
, Lemma 6.3]
, one can use
Theorem

3.7
to prove the following.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exists a smooth function
S^{G}(\delta,\phi)
on
\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)\times\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
such that

f^{G}(\delta)=\int_{\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)}S^{G}(\delta,\phi)f^{G}(\phi)\!\mathop{}d{\phi}

for all
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
and
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)
.

Writing
S^{G}(\phi,\delta)
for the normalised stable tempered character
S^{G}(\phi,\delta)=|D^{G}(\delta)|^{1/2}\Theta_{\phi}(\delta)
, we have the dual formula

f^{G}(\phi)=\int_{\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}S^{G}(\phi,\delta)f^{G}(\delta)\!\mathop{}d{\delta}.

4.5.
Proofs of the stable Paley–Wiener theorems

4.5.1.
Stable and unstable elliptic tempered characters.

Recall that the stable tempered characters are linearly independent and that we identify tempered
L
-parameters
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
with their associated virtual tempered representations
\pi_{\phi}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}c_{\phi}(\pi)\pi
and with their stable tempered distribution characters
\Theta_{\phi}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}c_{\phi}(\pi)\Theta_{\pi}
.

By
Hypothesis

4.2
, we have
D_{\mathrm{temp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G,\zeta)
and
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
. We define the space
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
to be the subspace of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
consisting of all elements orthogonal to
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
with respect to the elliptic inner product.

Lemma 4.4
.

We have
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\oplus D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
.

Let us set some notation and terminology. Let
V
be an inner product space (not necessarily complete) and let
W
be a subspace of
V
. We say that
W
has an orthogonal complement in
V
if there exists a subspace
X
of
V
such that
X
is orthogonal to
W
and
V=W\oplus X
, in which case
X
is

W^{\perp}=\Ann_{V}(W)=\{v\in V:\langle W,v\rangle=0\},

the largest subspace of
V
orthogonal to
W
. If
V
is complete and
W
is closed (in particular, if
V
is finite-dimensional), then
W
has an orthogonal complement in
V
.

Proof.

We have
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C}\phi
. For each
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
, the space
\mathbb{C}\phi
has an orthogonal complement in the finite-dimensional space
\mathbb{C}\Pi_{\phi}
. Since

\mathbb{C}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C}\Pi_{\phi},

it follows that
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
has an orthogonal complement in
\mathbb{C}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)
, namely

\Ann_{\mathbb{C}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)}(D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta))=\bigoplus_{\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)}\Ann_{\mathbb{C}\Pi_{\phi}}(\mathbb{C}\phi).

Since

D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=\mathbb{C}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)\oplus\mathbb{C}(T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\setminus\mathbb{C}^{1}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta))

it follows that

D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=\Ann_{\mathbb{C}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)}(D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta))\oplus\mathbb{C}(T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\setminus\mathbb{C}^{1}\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta))

and that
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\oplus D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
.
∎

4.5.2.
Stable pseudocoefficients.

Let
\zeta
be a unitary character of
A_{G}(F)
. Recall the Paley–Wiener space
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)
. Fix a set of representatives
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
for
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
such that
\Pi_{2}(G,\zeta)\subseteq E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Then
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
is a basis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. As explained above, we have an identification
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C}
defined by
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi(\tau))_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}
.

Let
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)=\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
, which is an orthogonal basis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
(with respect to the elliptic inner product). Let
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
be an orthogonal basis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
and define
B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\cup B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
. Then
B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
is an orthogonal basis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. For
?=\mathrm{st},\mathrm{unst}
, we define

PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G,\zeta)=\bigoplus_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta)}\mathbb{C}.

We identify an element of
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G,\zeta)
) with the linear functional on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta)
) it defines by linear extension. Thus, for each
\varphi\in PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)
the value
\varphi(b)
is defined for all
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Explicitly, for each
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
, let us write
b=\sum_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}c_{b,\tau}\tau
for
c_{b,\tau}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
\varphi(b)=\sum_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}c_{b,\tau}\tau
. We have an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\oplus PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

defined by

(\varphi(\tau))_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}\mapsto(\varphi(b))_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}=((\varphi(b))_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)},(\varphi(b))_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)}),

that is, by change-of-basis. We identify
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)=PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\oplus PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
via the above isomorphism. Note that
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
is the subspace of functions in
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)
that vanish on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
and thus does not depend on the choice of
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
be a closed subspace of
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
consisting of all functions that are constant on regular semisimple stable classes. Let
\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
denote the image of
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
in
\mathcal{S}_{f}(G,\zeta)
and let
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
denote the kernel of the quotient map

\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta).

Note that
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\cap\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)=0
.

Proposition 4.5
.

The invariant Fourier transform

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

restricts to isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G,\zeta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G,\zeta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Consequently, we have

\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)=\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\oplus\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta).

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the subspace of
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
corresponding to the summand
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G,\zeta)
lies in
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G,\zeta)
.

Let
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta)
. The element
\delta_{b}=(\delta_{b,b^{\prime}})_{b^{\prime}\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)}\in PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G,\zeta)
has its inverse invariant Fourier transform equal to
\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{-2}f[b]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
. Thus, it suffices to prove that the pseudocoefficient
f[b]_{G}
lies in
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G,\zeta)

If
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
, then
f[b]_{G}(\phi)=0
for all
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)=B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
, and thus
f[b]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
by stable spectral density for
C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
.

Suppose that
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)=\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
and let us write
b=\phi
and
f[b]_{G}=f[\phi]_{G}
. Let
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
. If
\gamma
is non-elliptic, then
f[\phi]_{G}(\gamma)=0
. If
\gamma
is elliptic, then

f[\phi]_{G}(\gamma)=m(\gamma)^{-1}|D^{G}(\gamma)|^{1/2}\overline{\Theta_{\phi}(\gamma)}.

We have that
D^{G}
and
\Theta_{\phi}
are constant on regular semisimple stable classes. Thus, it suffices to show that
m(\gamma)
is constant on regular semisimple stable classes. By definition, we have
m(\gamma)=\vol(G_{\gamma}(F)/A_{G}(F))
. Suppose that
\gamma^{\prime}
is stably conjugate to
\gamma
. Then there exists
g\in G
such that
\Int(g):G_{\gamma}\to G_{\gamma^{\prime}}
is defined over
F
. The Haar measures on
G_{\gamma}(F)
and
G_{\gamma^{\prime}}(F)
are normalised so that they correspond under
\Int(g)
, and thus
m(\gamma)=m(\gamma^{\prime})
.
∎

We obtain the following stable Paley–Wiener theorem as a corollary

Corollary 4.6
.

The stable Fourier transform gives an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Proof.

The composition of the invariant Fourier transform

\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)\longrightarrow PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)

with the natural projection
PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G,\zeta)\to PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
is the stable Fourier transform. It is surjective and its kernel is
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
by
Proposition

4.5
, so it descends to an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)\to PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
.
∎

4.5.3.
The cuspidal case.

Let
\zeta
be a unitary character of
A_{G}(F)
, and let
\mu
be an infinitesimal character of
G
. We denote by
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
the set of
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
with
\mu_{\tau}=\mu
. Define
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\mathbb{C}T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
.

Lemma 4.7
.

The quotient
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
is finite. If
F
is archimedean, then its cardinality is bounded independently of
(\zeta,\mu)
.

Proof.

If
F
is non-archimedean, there are finitely many elements of
T_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
with a given infinitesimal character. Suppose that
F
is archimedean. We may assume that
F=\mathbb{R}
. Since
L^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}
is finite, it suffices to show that for each
M\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
, the number of possibilities for
\sigma\in\Pi_{2}(M)
such that
(M,\sigma,\widetilde{r})\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
is bounded independently of
(\zeta,\mu)
. There are at most
|W(G,T)/W(M,T)|
possibilities for
\mu_{\sigma}
since it maps to
\mu
. Consider the restriction
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}
of the central character of
\sigma
to
A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
. Since
H_{M}:A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}\to\mathfrak{a}_{M}
is an isomorphism, we have
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}=e^{\langle\lambda,H_{M}(\cdot)\rangle}
for some
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{*}
. Every element of
W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{G}(\sigma)
fixes
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}
. Since
W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{G}(\sigma)
is the subset of
w\in W_{\mathrm{reg}}^{G}(\sigma)
such that
\mathfrak{a}_{M}^{w}=\mathfrak{a}_{G}
, we have
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
. At the same time,
\zeta|_{A_{G}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}=\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{G}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}=e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(\cdot)\rangle}
. It follows that
\lambda
and thus
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}
is determined by
\zeta
. There are
|A_{M}(\mathbb{R})/A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}|
ways that
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})}
can extend
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}
, so there are
|A_{M}(\mathbb{R})/A_{M}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}|
possibilities for
\zeta_{\sigma}|_{A_{M}(\mathbb{R})}
. As remarked above, in
[
AV16
]
Adams and Vogan prove that there are finitely many discrete series representations with a fixed infinitesimal character and split radical character and these representations form a discrete series
L
-packet. (The group
A_{M}(\mathbb{R})
is the split radical of
M(\mathbb{R})
.) Since the cardinality of discrete series
L
-packets of
M
is bounded, the lemma follows.
∎

Fix a set of representatives
\mathscr{X}^{G}
for the set of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbits of unitary characters of
A_{G}(F)
. We denote the
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbit of a pair
(\zeta,\mu)
by
[\zeta,\mu]
. Fix a set of representatives
\mathscr{P}^{G}
for the set of orbits
[\zeta,\mu]
, such that for each
(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}
we have
\zeta\in\mathscr{X}^{G}
.

Let
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
be the subspace of all
\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
such that
(\zeta_{\tau}|_{A_{G}(F)},\mu_{\tau})
belongs to
[\zeta,\mu]
. Let
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}
denote the isotropy subgroup of
\mu
in
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. We have

\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}.

Each connected component of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])/\mathbb{C}^{1}
meets
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
, and two elements of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
lie in the same connected component of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])/\mathbb{C}^{1}
if and only if they are in the same orbit under the finite group
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
. Let
\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
be a set of representatives for the connected components of
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])/\mathbb{C}^{1}
, or equivalently a set of representatives for the
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}
-orbits in
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)/\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Then the set of translates
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}\cdot\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\subseteq T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
contains an orthogonal basis
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
.

We define
\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
to be the set of
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
with
\mu_{\phi}=\mu
, and we define
\Phi_{2}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
to be the set of all
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(G)
with
(\zeta_{\phi}|_{A_{G}(F)},\mu_{\phi})\in[\zeta,\mu]
. Then
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\mathbb{C}\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. Let
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
be the orthogonal complement of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
in
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
with respect to the elliptic inner product. (Recall that
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
is finite-dimensional.) Let
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\subseteq\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
be a set of representatives for the
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbits in
\Phi_{2}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
, or equivalently a set of representatives for the
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}
-orbits in
\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. Then
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}\cdot\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. We write
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. The unitary representation of the finite group
\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mu}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
stabilises
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. Let
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
be an orthonormal eigenbasis of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
for this representation. Define

\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\cup\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)

and

B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)=B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\cup B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu).

Define
\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\coprod_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
, and define
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
,
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G)
, and
B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G)
similarly. Let
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\cup\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
and
B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\cup B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.

In order to be able to treat the case of test functions and Schwartz functions at the same time, we write
\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{C}_{(c)}
,
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{I}_{(c)}
, and
\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{S}_{(c)}
. We also write
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}=\mathscr{S}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}=PW
) if
\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{C}
(resp.
\mathscr{F}=\mathcal{C}_{c}
).

By definition, we have identifications

\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{\tau\in\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot\tau\Bigg)\quad,\quad\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot b\Bigg).

Let
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
(resp.
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
) be the subspace of
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
(resp.
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
) consisting of functions supported on
T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
(resp.
\Phi_{2}(G,[\zeta,\mu])
). The above identifications restrict to identifications

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{\tau\in\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot\tau\Bigg),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot b\Bigg).

We define

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot b\Bigg),

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,[\zeta,\mu])

\displaystyle=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}\Bigg(\coprod_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)}i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}\cdot b\Bigg).

We have two orthogonal bases
E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
and
B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
of
\bigoplus_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
. We define change-of-basis matrices

b=\sum_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}c_{b,\tau}\tau\quad,\quad\tau=\sum_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}c_{\tau,b}b.

It follows from the decompositions

E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\coprod_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\quad,\quad B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\coprod_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)

into orthogonal bases of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
that these change-of-basis matrices are block-diagonal. Let
\varphi=(\varphi_{\tau})_{\tau\in\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\in\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. We identify
\varphi
with its linear extension to
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
. For
\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
and
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, we write
\varphi_{\tau}(\lambda)=\varphi(\tau_{\lambda})
and
\varphi_{b}(\lambda)=\varphi(b_{\lambda})
. Each element of
\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
(resp.
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
) is a translate of an element of
\underline{\tau}\in\underline{E}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
(resp.
\underline{b}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
) by an element of
\lambda_{0}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}
, and thus
\varphi_{\tau}(\lambda)=\varphi_{\underline{\tau}}(\lambda+\lambda_{0})
(resp.
\varphi_{b}(\lambda)=\varphi_{\underline{b}}(\lambda+\lambda_{0})
). For
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
and
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
, we have
b_{\lambda}=\sum_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}c_{b,\tau}\tau_{\lambda}
, and therefore

\varphi_{b}(\lambda)=\sum_{\tau\in E_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}c_{b,\tau}\varphi_{\tau}(\lambda)=\sum_{\underline{\tau},\lambda_{0}}c_{b,\underline{\tau}_{\lambda_{0}}}\varphi_{\underline{\tau}}(\lambda+\lambda_{0}).

Consider the linear map
\varphi\mapsto(\varphi_{b})_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}=(\varphi^{\mathrm{st}},\varphi^{\mathrm{unst}})
, where
\varphi^{?}=(\varphi_{b})_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G)}
. For each
(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}
, this linear map restricts to an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu).

with inverse

\varphi_{\tau}(\lambda)=\sum_{b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}c_{\tau,b}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)=\sum_{\underline{b},\lambda_{0}}c_{\tau,\underline{b}_{\lambda_{0}}}\varphi_{\underline{b}}(\lambda+\lambda_{0}).

This follows easily since each of the spaces
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
,
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
, and
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
is a finite direct sums of classical
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}
-spaces.

Lemma 4.8
.

We have an isomorphism of topological vector spaces

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\longmapsto(\varphi^{\mathrm{st}},\varphi^{\mathrm{unst}})

The argument is similar to that in
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV, §1.5 and §2.2]
.

Proof.

First consider the case where
F
is non-archimedean. In this case, we have locally convex direct sum decompositions

\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\bigoplus_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)\quad,\quad\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G)=\bigoplus_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta,\mu).

Thus, it suffices to show that the columns of the change-of-basis matrices have finitely many non-zero entries. This is true since they are block-diagonal as remarked above and the dimension of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
is finite for each
(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}
.

Now consider the archimedean case. As in
[
MW16a
, Ch. IV, §1.5 and §2.2]
, it suffices to show that: (1) the number of nonzero entries in a column of the change-of-basis matrices is bounded; and (2) the entries of the change-of-basis matrices are bounded. Property (1) follows since the dimension of
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta,\mu)
is bounded independently of
(\zeta,\mu)
by
Lemma

4.7
. For property (2), recall that we have seen that
\{\|\tau\|_{\mathrm{ell}}:\tau\in T_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\}
is bounded. Moreover, by construction
\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}=1
for
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
. Suppose that
b\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\Phi_{2}(G)
and write
b=\phi
. We have
\phi=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}\pi
. Therefore
\|\phi\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}=\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{\phi}}\|\pi\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}
. As mentioned above, for
\pi\in\Pi_{2}(G)
we have
\|\pi\|_{\mathrm{ell}}=1
. (See the discussion after the orthogonality relations
Theorem

3.2
.) Therefore
\|\pi\|_{\mathrm{ell}}=|\Pi_{\phi}|
, which is bounded.
∎

We will identify
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
via the above isomorphism. Note that the subspace
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
of
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
consists of those functions
\varphi
whose linear extension to
D_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
vanishes on
D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. Thus, it does not depend on any of the choices made above.

Define
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
to be the closed subspace of
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
consisting of normalised invariant orbital integrals that are constant on regular semisimple stable classes. Let
\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
denote the image of
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
in
\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}(G)
and let
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
denote the kernel of the quotient map
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)\to\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
. Note that
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\cap\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)=0
.

Proposition 4.9
.

The invariant Fourier transform

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

restricts to isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Consequently, we have

\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)=\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G).

Corollary 4.10
.

The stable Fourier transform gives an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

We start with the following lemma which we will use to construct functions in
\mathscr{F}(G)
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G,\zeta)
and let
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
(resp.
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
). The function
f^{\phi}\coloneqq f\cdot(\phi\circ H_{G})
lies in
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
(resp.
\mathcal{C}(G)
).

Note that if
f
is
K
-finite, then so is
f^{\phi}
.

Proof.

Let
f^{\phi}=f\cdot(\phi\circ H_{G})
. Evidently,
f^{\phi}
is smooth. Let
C\subseteq G(F)
be a compact subspace such that
\supp(f)=CA_{G}(F)
.

First, we suppose that
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
. We have

\supp(f^{\phi})\subseteq\supp(\phi\circ H_{G})\cap\supp(f)\subseteq H_{G}^{-1}(\supp(\phi))\cap CA_{G}(F).

Thus, to show that
\supp(f^{\phi})
is compact, it suffices to show that
H_{G}^{-1}(\supp\phi)\cap CA_{G}(F)
is compact. Let
x\in H_{G}^{-1}(\supp(\phi))\cap CA_{G}(F)
and write
x=ca
, where
c\in C
and
a\in A_{G}(F)
. Then
H_{G}(a)=H_{G}(x)-H_{G}(c)
, which lies in the compact subspace
\supp(\phi)-H_{G}(C)
of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}
. Let
\widetilde{C}=\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}\cap(\supp(\phi)-H_{G}(C))
, a compact subspace of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
. Then
a\in H_{G}|_{A_{G}(F)}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
, and
x\in CH_{G}|_{A_{G}(F)}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
Thus,

H_{G}^{-1}(\supp(\phi))\cap CA_{G}(F)\subseteq CH_{G}|_{A_{G}(F)}^{-1}(\widetilde{C}).

Therefore it suffices to show that
H_{G}|_{A_{G}(F)}^{-1}(\widetilde{C})
is compact. This follows since
H_{G}|_{A_{G}(F)}:A_{G}(F)\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
is proper, as it is a continuous surjective homomorphism with compact kernel
A_{G}(F)^{1}
.

For the rest of the proof, we suppose that
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
. First we show that
f^{\phi}
is rapidly decreasing. Let
r>0
. We must show that
|f^{\phi}(g)|\ll\Xi(g)(1+\sigma(g))^{-r}
. Recall the decomposition
G(F)=KM_{0}(F)K
, that
\Xi
is bi-
K
-invariant, and that
1+\sigma(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})\asymp 1+\sigma(m_{0})
and
1+\sigma(m_{0})\asymp 1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|
for
k_{1},k_{2}\in K
and
m_{0}\in M_{0}(F)
. Therefore, it suffices to show
|f^{\phi}(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})|\ll\Xi(m_{0})(1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|)^{-r}
.

Since
f\in\mathcal{C}(G,\zeta)
, we have
|f(g)|\ll\Xi(g)(1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(g))^{-r}
. We have
1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})\asymp 1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(m_{0})
, and therefore
|f(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})|\ll\Xi(m_{0})(1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(m_{0}))^{-r}
. Since
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
, we have
|\phi(X)|\ll(1+\|X\|)^{-r}
. Therefore

|f^{\phi}(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})|\ll\Xi(m_{0})(1+\|H_{G}(m_{0})\|)^{-r}(1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(m_{0}))^{-r}.

Decompose
H_{0}(m_{0})\in\mathfrak{a}_{M_{0},F}
as
H_{0}(m_{0})=H_{G}(m_{0})+H_{0}(m_{0})^{G}
according to the decomposition
\mathfrak{a}_{M_{0},F}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{M_{0},F}^{G}
. Since
1+\sigma(m_{0})\asymp 1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|
, we have

1+\sigma^{A_{G}}(m_{0})\asymp 1+\inf_{a\in A_{G}(F)}\|H_{0}(a)+H_{0}(m_{0})\|.

Since
\mathfrak{a}_{M_{0},F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{M_{0},F}^{G}
and
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
is finite, we obtain

1+\inf_{a\in A_{G}(F)}\|H_{0}(a)+H_{0}(m_{0})\|\asymp 1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})^{G}\|.

Therefore

1+\sigma^{A_{G}(F)}(m_{0})\asymp 1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})^{G}\|.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle|f^{\phi}(k_{1}m_{0}k_{2})|/\Xi(m_{0})

\displaystyle\ll(1+\|H_{G}(m_{0})\|)^{-r}(1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})^{G}\|)^{-r}

\displaystyle\ll(1+\|H_{0}(m_{0})\|)^{-r}

as required.

Suppose that
F
is non-archimedean. In this case, we must show that there exists a compact open subgroup
K_{0}
of
G(F)
such that
f^{\phi}
is bi-
K_{0}
-invariant. Since
F
is non-archimedean,
\supp(f)
is open and has compact open image in
G(F)/A_{G}(F)
. Thus, we may take
C
to be compact open. Then there exists a compact open subgroup
K_{0}
of
G(F)
such that
K_{0}CK_{0}=C
and
f
is constant on the
K_{0}
double cosets in
C
. Since
f
is zero outside of
CA_{G}(F)
, it suffices to show that
f
is bi-
K_{0}
-invariant on
CA_{G}(F)
. Write
C=\bigcup_{i=1}^{M}K_{0}g_{i}K_{0}
and define
c_{i}=f(K_{0}g_{i}K_{0})
for
i=1,\dots,M
. Then
f(k_{0}g_{i}k_{0}^{\prime}a)=\zeta(a)^{-1}c_{i}
for all
k_{0},k_{0}^{\prime}\in K_{0}
,
i=0,\dots,M
, and
a\in A_{G}(F)
. For any
k_{0}^{\prime\prime},k_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}
we have

f(k_{0}^{\prime}k_{0}g_{i}k_{0}^{\prime}ak_{0}^{\prime\prime})=f(k_{0}^{\prime}k_{0}g_{i}k_{0}^{\prime}k_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}a)=\zeta(a)^{-1}c_{i}=f(k_{0}g_{i}k_{0}^{\prime}a).

Therefore
f
is bi-
K_{0}
-invariant. Since
H_{G}
is zero on all compact subgroups of
G(F)
, we have that
f^{\phi}
is bi-
K_{0}
-invariant.

Now assume that
F
is archimedean. We must show that for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
, the function
L(X)R(Y)f^{\phi}
is rapidly decreasing. For
X\in\mathfrak{g}
, we have

(R(X)f^{\phi})(g)=[D\phi(H_{G}(g))\cdot(R(X)H_{G})(g)\big]f(g)+(R(X)f)^{\phi}(g),

and therefore
R(X)f^{\phi}
is a linear combination of functions of the form
f^{\phi}
. A similar formula hods for
L(X)
. It follows that for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}})
, the function
L(X)R(Y)f^{\phi}
is a linear combination of functions of the form
f^{\phi}
, and is thus rapidly decreasing by what we have already shown.
∎

We now form the union

\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta)=\bigcup_{(\zeta,\mu)\in\mathscr{P}^{G}}\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G,\zeta,\mu),

and define
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)=\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\cup\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
. Let
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. Since the elements of
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
are mutually orthogonal, the pseudocoefficient
f[b]_{G}
satisfies

\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime}),

for all
b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. The domain of integration
G(F)/A_{G}(F)
fibres over the finite set
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
with open fibres. We will need the following formula for the integral of
\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)
on a single fibre in the case when
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
. For
b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
and
X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
, we have

\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|^{-1}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}(X).

Proof.

Let
b^{\prime}\in B_{\mathrm{ell}}(G,\zeta)
. For
X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
, define

f_{b,b^{\prime}}(X)=\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}.

We will calculate the Fourier transform of
f_{b,b^{\prime}}
. The Pontryagin dual of
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
is
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
. For
\lambda\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{f_{b,b^{\prime}}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{-\langle\lambda,X\rangle}f_{b,b^{\prime}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{-\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})/A_{G}(F)}e^{-\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)\rangle}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b_{\lambda}](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=\langle b,b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}

If
b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
, then
b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}\in D_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G,\zeta)
and thus
\langle b,b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=0
. If
b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)\subseteq\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
, then
b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}\in\Phi_{2}(G,\zeta)
and
\langle b,b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathrm{ell}}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\delta_{b}(b_{-\lambda}^{\prime})
. Thus, we have
\widehat{f_{b,b^{\prime}}}(\lambda)=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\delta_{b}(b_{-\lambda}^{\prime})
.

Suppose
b=b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}
. Then
\mu_{b}=(-\lambda)\cdot\mu_{b^{\prime}}
. It follows that
[\zeta,\mu_{b}]=[\zeta,\mu_{b^{\prime}}]
. Since
(\zeta,\mu_{b}),(\zeta,\mu_{b^{\prime}})\in\mathscr{P}^{G}
, it follows that
\mu_{b}=\mu_{b^{\prime}}
. Consequently,
\lambda
lies in the stabiliser
\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}
of
b
in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}^{\vee}
. Moreover, since
b,b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,\mu_{b})
and
b=b_{-\lambda}^{\prime}
, we have
b=b^{\prime}
. It follows that
\delta_{b}(b_{-\lambda}^{\prime})=\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}(\lambda)
.

Thus, we have shown that

\widehat{f_{b,b^{\prime}}}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}.

When
b^{\prime}\neq b
, we obtain
f_{b,b^{\prime}}=0
as claimed. It remains to be shown that

f_{b,b}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}.

We have

\widehat{f_{b,b}}=\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}.

Thus, we must show that the Fourier transform of
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}
on
\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
is
|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}
. This follows from the general formula for the Fourier transform of a characteristic function of a subgroup of a finite abelian group. Let
A
be a finite abelian group and let
B
be a subgroup of
A
. Then for
\chi\in\widehat{B}
, we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbbm{1}_{B}}(\chi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in A}\chi(a)^{-1}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\in B}\chi(b)^{-1}

\displaystyle=|B|\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{A^{\perp}}(\chi),

where
A^{\perp}=\{\chi\in\widehat{A}:\chi|_{B}=1\}
. If we take
A=\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
and
B=\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}
, then
A^{\perp}=\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}
, and we obtain the desired result.
∎

For
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
, define
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)_{b}
to be the subspace of all
\varphi=(\varphi_{b^{\prime}})_{b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}
in
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
with
\varphi_{b^{\prime}}=0
for
b^{\prime}\neq b
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Let
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
and let
\varphi=(\varphi_{b^{\prime}})_{b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)}\in\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)_{b}
. Regarding
\varphi_{b}\in\widehat{\mathscr{F}}(i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee})
as an element of
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}(i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee})
, let
\phi_{b}\in\mathscr{F}(\mathfrak{a}_{G,F})
be its Fourier transform. The inverse invariant Fourier transform of
\varphi
is

\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})^{-1}\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{-2}\frac{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|}{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(\gamma))f[b]_{G}(\gamma)

and this lies in
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
.

Proof.

Let
\zeta=\zeta_{b}
be the
A_{G}(F)
-character of
b
. Choose a function
f[b]\in C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
representing the pseudocoefficient
f[b]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty}(G,\zeta,K)
of
b
. Define
f:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}
by

f(x)=\phi_{b}(H_{G}(x))f[b](x).

By
Lemma

4.11
, we have that
f\in\mathscr{F}(G)
. We have
f_{G}(\gamma)=\phi_{b}(H_{G}(\gamma))f[b]_{G}(\gamma)
, which does not depend on the choice of
f[b]
and lies in
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
. By
Proposition

4.5
, we have
f[b]_{G}\in\mathcal{I}C_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\infty,\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta,K)
. That is,
f[b]_{G}(\gamma)
is constant on regular semisimple stable classes. Since
H_{G}
is constant on elements of
G(F)
that lie in the same
G
-conjugacy class, we have that
f_{G}
is constant on regular semisimple stable classes, i.e.
f_{G}\in\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
.

It remains for us to show that for all
b^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)
and
\lambda\in i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
we have

f_{G}(b_{\lambda}^{\prime})=\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\frac{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|}{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)\delta_{b}(b^{\prime}).

Let
\zeta^{\prime}=\zeta_{b^{\prime}}
be the
A_{G}(F)
-character of
b^{\prime}
. We have

\displaystyle f_{G}(b_{\lambda}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\int_{G(F)}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)\rangle}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)\phi_{b}(H_{G}(x))f[b](x)\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}\int_{A_{G}(F)}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(xa)\rangle}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(xa)\phi_{b}(H_{G}(xa))f[b](xa)\!\mathop{}d{a}\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)\rangle}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{G}(F)}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(a)\rangle}\zeta^{\prime}(a)\zeta(a)^{-1}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(xa))\!\mathop{}d{a}\!\mathop{}d{x}.

The inner integral in the third equality is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{A_{G}(F)/A_{G}(F)^{1}}\int_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(aa^{1})\rangle}\zeta^{\prime}(aa^{1})\zeta(aa^{1})^{-1}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(xaa^{1}))\!\mathop{}d{a^{1}}\!\mathop{}d{a}

\displaystyle=\int_{A_{G}(F)/A_{G}(F)^{1}}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(a)\rangle}\zeta^{\prime}(a)\zeta(a)^{-1}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(xa))\int_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}\zeta^{\prime}(a^{1})\zeta(a^{1})^{-1}\!\mathop{}d{a^{1}}\!\mathop{}d{a}.

Now,

\int_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}\zeta^{\prime}(a^{1})\zeta(a^{1})^{-1}\!\mathop{}d{a^{1}}=\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\delta_{\zeta|_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}}(\zeta^{\prime}|_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}),

which is equal to 1 if
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}
lie in the same
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbit, and 0 otherwise. Since
\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}
lie in our set
\mathscr{X}^{G}
of representatives of
i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}
-orbits, we obtain

\int_{A_{G}(F)^{1}}\zeta^{\prime}(a^{1})\zeta(a^{1})^{-1}\!\mathop{}d{a^{1}}=\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\delta_{\zeta}(\zeta^{\prime})

Therefore we have
f_{G}(b^{\prime}_{\lambda})=0
if
\zeta^{\prime}\neq\zeta
. Thus, we may assume that
\zeta^{\prime}=\zeta
. Then

\displaystyle\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})^{-1}f_{G}(b_{\lambda}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)

\displaystyle\int_{A_{G}(F)/A_{G}(F)^{1}}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)+H_{G}(a)\rangle}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(x)+H_{G}(a))\!\mathop{}d{a}\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G(F)/A_{G}(F)}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)+\widetilde{X}\rangle}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(x)+\widetilde{X})\!\mathop{}d{\widetilde{X}}\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{\langle\lambda,H_{G}(x)+\widetilde{X}\rangle}\phi_{b}(H_{G}(x)+\widetilde{X})\!\mathop{}d{\widetilde{X}}\!\mathop{}d{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}\int_{H_{G}^{-1}(X+\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F})}\Theta_{b^{\prime}}(x)f[b](x)\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{\langle\lambda,X+\widetilde{X}\rangle}\phi_{b}(X+\widetilde{X})\!\mathop{}d{\widetilde{X}}\!\mathop{}d{x}

Consequently, by
Proposition

4.12
, we have

\displaystyle f_{G}(b_{\lambda}^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|^{-1}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})

\displaystyle\sum_{X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}\int_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}}e^{\langle\lambda,X+\widetilde{X}\rangle}\phi_{b}(X+\widetilde{X})\!\mathop{}d{\widetilde{X}}

\displaystyle=\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|^{-1}\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}}e^{\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\phi_{b}(X)\!\mathop{}d{X}.

For
X\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{b}(X)

\displaystyle=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}e^{-\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)\!\mathop{}d{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}}\sum_{\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}e^{-\langle\lambda+\widetilde{\lambda},X\rangle}\varphi_{b}(\lambda+\widetilde{\lambda})\!\mathop{}d{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}}e^{-\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)\sum_{\widetilde{\lambda}\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}}e^{-\langle\widetilde{\lambda},X\rangle}\!\mathop{}d{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\int_{i\mathfrak{a}_{G}^{*}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}}e^{-\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}}(X)\!\mathop{}d{\lambda}

\displaystyle=|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}}(X)\widehat{\varphi_{b}}(X).

Therefore

\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}}e^{\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\phi_{b}(X)\!\mathop{}d{X}=|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|\int_{\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}}e^{\langle\lambda,X\rangle}\widehat{\varphi_{b}}(X)\!\mathop{}d{X}=|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|\varphi_{b}(\lambda).

Thus, we have

f_{G}(b_{\lambda}^{\prime})=\vol(A_{G}(F)^{1})\|b\|_{\mathrm{ell}}^{2}\frac{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}^{\vee}/\mathfrak{a}_{G,F}^{\vee}|}{|\mathfrak{a}_{G,b}/\widetilde{\mathfrak{a}}_{G,F}|}\varphi_{b}(\lambda)\delta_{b}(b^{\prime})

as required.
∎

Recall that we denote the invariant Fourier transform by
\mathcal{F}
. Stable spectral density for
\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
is equivalent to the assertion that
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.

Lemma 4.14
.

We have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
. That is, stable spectral density holds for
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
.

Proof.

By stable spectral density for
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}(G)
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{c,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
. Since
PW_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
is dense in
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
is closed in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
, it follows that we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.
∎

We now prove
Proposition

4.9
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. Write
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
as a countable increasing union of finite sets
\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell},i}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. For each
i
, define
\varphi_{i}=(\varphi_{i,b})_{b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell},i}(G)}\in\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
by
\varphi_{i,b}=\varphi_{b}
if
b\in\underline{B}_{\mathrm{ell},i}(G)
and
\varphi_{i,b}=0
otherwise. Then
\lim_{i}\varphi_{i}=\varphi
. By
Proposition

4.13
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\varphi_{i})\in\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. Since
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
is closed in
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
, it follows that
\mathcal{F}(\varphi)\in\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. Therefore we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
.

Since
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G))\oplus\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G).

Therefore
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{?}(G))=\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G)
as required.
∎

4.5.4.
K
-finite functions.

If we trace through the above, replacing
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}
with
\mathcal{I}_{f}
,
\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}
with
\mathcal{S}_{f}
, and
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}
with
PW_{f}
, we obtain a proof of the following.

Proposition 4.15
.

The invariant Fourier transform

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

restricts to isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{?}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{?}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Consequently, we have

\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)=\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G).

Corollary 4.16
.

The stable Fourier transform gives an isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces PW_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

4.5.5.
Proof of the main stable Paley–Wiener theorems.

We now apply the constructions and results of
Section

4.5.3
to each semistandard Levi
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
. We define

\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(L)\Bigg)^{W_{0}^{G}}=\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(L)^{W^{G}(L)}.

We have decompositions

\widehat{\mathscr{F}}(G)=\Bigg(\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(G)\Bigg)^{W_{0}^{G}}=\bigoplus_{L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})/W_{0}^{G}}\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)^{W^{G}(L)}

and
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}(L)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(L)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(L)
for each
L\in\mathcal{L}^{G}(M_{0})
. Thus, we obtain a decomposition
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}(G)=\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\oplus\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
. By
Corollary

3.4
the inclusion
PW^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)\to\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
is continuous with dense image.

We can now deduce the full stable spectral density theorem for
\mathcal{C}(G)
from the stable spectral density theorem for
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
.

Theorem 4.17

(Stable spectral density)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}(G)
. If
f^{G}(\phi)=0
for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
, then
f^{G}(\delta)=0
for all
\delta\in\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
. Equivalently,
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.

Proof.

By stable spectral density for
C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
, we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(PW^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}C_{c}^{\infty,\mathrm{unst}}(G)
. Since
PW^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
is dense in
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
and
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
is closed in
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}(G)
, it follows that we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\mathcal{I}\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.
∎

Note that
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))\subseteq\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
follows immediately from the definitions. Thus, we have
\mathcal{F}^{-1}(\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G))=\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
.

We now prove the main stable Paley–Wiener theorems.

Proof.

The stable Fourier transform
