\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}(G)\to\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
is the composition of the invariant Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
and the projection onto
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. The kernel is
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{unst}}(G)
, so it descends to a continuous bijection
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}(G)\to\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
. Since its inverse is the composition of continuous maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathscr{F}}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}(G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{-1}}

the stable Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}(G)\to\widehat{\mathscr{F}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
is a topological isomorphism.

In the same way, one can prove that the stable Fourier transform restricts to an isomorphism of topological vector spaces
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}:\mathcal{S}_{f}(G)\to PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
.
∎

Note that the stable Paley–Wiener theorems for
\mathcal{S}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}(G,\zeta)
and
\mathcal{S}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}(G)
can be proved in a similar way. The proof given above gives more information, namely that the subspace
\mathcal{I}_{f,\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
\mathcal{I}\mathscr{F}_{\mathrm{cusp}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
) corresponds to
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell},f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G,\zeta)
(resp.
\widehat{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)
) under the invariant Fourier transform.

Using the stable Paley–Wiener Theorem for
\mathcal{S}(G)
, a Fourier inversion formula for elements of
\mathcal{S}(G)
can be obtained from Arthur’s Fourier inversion formula for elements of
\mathcal{I}(G)
. The argument is the same as the one used by Arthur to prove
[
Art96
, 6.3]
.

5.
Stable Transfer

In this section we prove
Theorem

1.1
. We also describe some examples of stable transfer operators explicitly. We begin by recalling the setup from the introduction. Let
H
and
G
be connected reductive groups over our local field
F
, and let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an
L
-homomorphism (taken up to equivalence). Assume that
G
is quasisplit so that
G
-relevance is automatic. Then we have a map

\xi_{*}:\Phi(H)\longrightarrow\Phi(G)

defined by
\xi_{*}(\phi)=\xi\circ\phi
. This restricts to a map

\xi_{*}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\longrightarrow\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)

if and only if
\xi
is tempered in the sense that
\xi|_{W_{F}}
is a tempered
L
-parameter of
G
. Suppose that
\xi
is injective and tempered. In
[
Lan13
, Questions A & B]
, Langlands asked whether for each
f^{G}\in\mathcal{S}_{c}(G)
there exists
f^{H}\in\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)
such that

f^{H}(\phi)=f^{G}(\xi_{*}(\phi))

for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
. Note that if
f^{H}
exists, it is uniquely determined by stable spectral density for test functions. More generally, one can ask whether for each
f^{G}\in\mathcal{S}(G)
there exists
f^{H}\in\mathcal{S}(H)
with the above property, which would also be uniquely determined by spectral density. The function
f^{H}
is called the stable transfer of
f^{G}
along
\xi
, and we denote it by
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G}
. It has also been called the functorial transfer of
f^{G}
and, to avoid confusion with endoscopic transfer, the stable-stable transfer of
f^{G}
. We will prove the following.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of injective tempered
L
-homomorphisms, and assume that
G
is quasisplit. Then pullback along
\xi_{*}
gives a well-defined continuous linear map

\xi^{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H).

Furthermore, it restricts to continuous linear maps

\xi^{*}:PW^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\longrightarrow PW^{\mathrm{st}}(H)

and

\xi^{*}:PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\longrightarrow PW_{f}^{\mathrm{st}}(H).

It follows that stable transfer along
\xi
can be constructed by taking the stable Fourier transform, pulling back along
\xi_{*}
, and then taking the inverse stable Fourier transform:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}(H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\xi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{st}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi^{*}}

We obtain
Theorem

1.1
as a corollary, which we restate here for convenience. In particular, we obtain an affirmative answer to
[
Lan13
, Questions A & B]
.

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of injective tempered
L
-homomorphisms, and assume that
G
is quasisplit. If
F
is non-archimedean, we assume
Hypothesis

4.2
for
H
and
G
. There exists a continuous linear operator

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}:\mathcal{S}(G)\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(H)

whose transpose

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime}:\mathcal{S}(H)^{\prime}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(G)^{\prime}

satisfies

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}^{\prime}\Theta_{\phi}=\Theta_{\xi_{*}\phi}

for all
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
restricts to continuous linear operators
\mathcal{S}_{c}(G)\to\mathcal{S}_{c}(H)
and
\mathcal{S}_{f}(G)\to\mathcal{S}_{f}(H)
.

5.1.
Reduction to
\pi_{0}
-finiteness of functorial transfer

Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of injective tempered
L
-homomorphisms. To prove the first claim in
Theorem

5.1
and thus that stable transfer along
\xi
exists, we must show that pullback along
\xi_{*}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\to\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
gives a well-defined continuous linear map

\xi^{*}:\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H).

Recall that we have decompositions

\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)=\coprod_{M_{G}}\Phi_{2}(M_{G})/W^{G}(M_{G})\quad,\quad\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)=\coprod_{M_{H}}\Phi_{2}(M_{H})/W^{G}(M_{H})

and corresponding decompositions

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(G)=\bigoplus_{M_{G}}\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{G})^{W^{G}(M_{G})}\quad,\quad\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{st}}(H)=\bigoplus_{M_{H}}\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{H})^{W^{H}(M_{H})}.

Thus, it suffices to show that for each
M_{H}
and
M_{G}
, pullback along the partially defined map

\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})/W^{H}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})/W^{G}(M_{G})

and extension by zero gives a well-defined continuous linear map

\xi_{M_{H},M_{G}}^{*}:\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{G})^{W^{G}(M_{G})}\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{H})^{W^{H}(M_{H})}.

In turn, it suffices to show that pullback along the partially defined map
\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
and extension by zero gives
a well-defined continuous linear map

\xi_{M_{H},M_{G}}^{*}:\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{G})\longrightarrow\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{ell}}^{\mathrm{st}}(M_{H}).

We will establish this by showing that we can apply
Lemma

3.5
. Moreover, applying
Lemma

3.5
also gives the second claim in
Theorem

5.1
, thus establishing
Theorem

5.1
in full. We will now turn to an examination of the partially defined map
\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
in more detail.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
\lambda
-group. The outer action
\Gamma_{F}\to\Out(\mathcal{G}^{0})
determines an action
\Gamma_{F}\to\Aut(Z(\mathcal{G}^{0}))
. We will write
A_{\mathcal{G}}=Z(\mathcal{G}^{0})^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}
for brevity.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of elliptic
L
-homomorphisms. Then
\xi
restricts to a homomorphism

\xi:A_{\prescript{L}{}{H}}\longrightarrow A_{\prescript{L}{}{G}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}=C_{\prescript{L}{}{G}}(\xi(A_{\prescript{L}{}{H}}))=C_{\prescript{L}{}{G}}(\xi(Z(H^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F},\circ}))
. Since
Z(H^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}}=C_{H^{\vee}\!}(\prescript{L}{}{H})
, we have
\xi(\prescript{L}{}{H})\subseteq\mathcal{M}
. Therefore
\mathcal{M}
maps onto
W_{F}
. Since
\xi(A_{\prescript{L}{}{H}})
is a torus in
G^{\vee}\!
, we have that
\mathcal{M}
is Levi subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
. Since
\xi
is elliptic, we have

\mathcal{M}=\prescript{L}{}{G}
, or in other words

\xi(A_{\prescript{L}{}{H}})\subseteq(C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\prescript{L}{}{G}))=Z(G^{\vee}\!)^{\Gamma_{F}}
. The claim follows.
∎

To simplify notation, for a Levi subgroup
M
of
G
we write the canonical equivalence class of
L
-embeddings
\iota_{M}^{G}:\prescript{L}{}{M}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
as an inclusion
\prescript{L}{}{M}\hookrightarrow\prescript{L}{}{G}
. Fix
\phi\in\Phi_{2}(M_{H})
and suppose that
\xi_{*}(\phi)\in\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
. That is,
\phi
is in the domain of the partially defined map
\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
.

Let
M_{H,\xi}
be the Levi subgroup of
G
determined up to
G(F)
-conjugacy by the property that
\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}
belongs to the
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy class Levi subgroups of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
that contains
\xi(\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}})
minimally. Then
\xi
factors through
\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}\hookrightarrow\prescript{L}{}{G}
as an equivalence class of injective elliptic
L
-homomorphisms
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}\to\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}
. The above lemma gives us an injection
\xi:A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}}\to A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}}
.

Recall that we have an identification
\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})=X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
. Under this identification, we have that the injection
\Lie(\xi):\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})\to\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}})
is the map
\xi_{*}\otimes\mathrm{id}
, where
\xi_{*}:X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})\to X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}})
. We thus write
\xi_{*}=\Lie(\xi)
. We have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

Recall that we have a canonical isomorphism
X^{*}(M_{H})\cong X_{*}(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})
and that we write its extension
\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cong\Lie(A_{\prescript{L}{}{M_{H}}})
as
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{\vee}
. Thus, we may view the above commutative diagram as the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H},\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H,\xi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}(M_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}(M_{H,\xi})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

Note that the injection
\xi_{*}:\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H},\mathbb{C}}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H,\xi},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
restricts to injections
\xi_{*}:\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H}}^{*}\to\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H,\xi}}^{*}
and
\xi_{*}:i\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H}}^{*}\to i\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H,\xi}}^{*}
.

Since
\xi_{*}(\phi)\in\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
it follows that
\prescript{L}{}{M_{G}}\hookrightarrow\prescript{L}{}{G}
factors through
\prescript{L}{}{M_{H,\xi}}\hookrightarrow\prescript{L}{}{G}
. Thus, we may assume that
M_{G}\subseteq M_{H,\xi}
, and therefore that
M_{G}
is a Levi subgroup of
M_{H,\xi}
. Then
\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H,\xi}}^{*}\subseteq\mathfrak{a}_{M_{G}}^{*}
and we have
\xi_{*}:i\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H}}^{*}\to i\mathfrak{a}_{M_{G}}^{*}
. Now, for all
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{M_{H},\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, we have

\xi_{*}(\phi_{\lambda})=\xi_{*}(\phi)_{\xi_{*}(\lambda)}.

This explains how
\xi_{*}
behaves on the component of
\phi
.

Suppose that
F
is archimedean. We may assume that
F=\mathbb{R}
by restriction of scalars. We must show that
\|\xi_{*}\phi\|_{M_{G}}\gg\|\phi\|_{M_{H}}
for all
\phi
in the domain of the partially defined map
\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
. Since
\|\cdot\|_{M_{G}}\asymp\|\cdot\|_{G}
and
\|\cdot\|_{M_{H}}\asymp\|\cdot\|_{H}
, it suffices to show that
\|\xi_{*}\phi\|_{G}\gg\|\phi\|_{H}
for
\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0}
and
\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}
be
\Gamma_{\mathbb{R}}
-stable maximal tori of
H^{\vee}\!
and
G^{\vee}\!
, respectively. Choose a representative
L
-homomorphism
\xi
such that
\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}
. Note that
a_{\xi}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})\subseteq C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0}))
. Since
\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}
, we have
\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}\subseteq C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0}))
. Therefore
\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}
is a maximal torus of
C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0}))
. By replacing
\xi
by a
C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\xi_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0}))
-conjugate, we may assume that both
\xi(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})
and
a_{\xi}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
are contained in
\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0}
. Recall how the infinitesimal character attached to an
L
-parameter is defined. We may identify the infinitesimal character
\mu_{\phi}
of
\phi
with an element of
\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})/W(H^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}_{H})
. Let
\mu_{\xi}=\mu_{\xi|_{W_{\mathbb{R}}}}
denote the infinitesimal character attached to the
L
-parameter
\xi|_{W_{\mathbb{R}}}
. We may identify
\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}
and
\mu_{\xi}
with elements of
\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0})/W(G^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}_{G})
. We have
\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}=\Lie(\xi)\mu_{\phi}+\mu_{\xi}
. Fix a
W(H^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}_{H})
-invariant inner product on
\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})
and a
W(G^{\vee}\!,\mathcal{T}_{G})
-invariant inner product on
\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0})
, and denote the associated norms by
\|\cdot\|_{H^{\vee}\!}
and
\|\cdot\|_{G^{\vee}\!}
, respectively. We have
\|\phi\|_{H}\asymp\|\mu_{\phi}\|_{H^{\vee}\!}
and
\|\xi_{*}\phi\|_{G}\asymp\|\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}
, so it suffices to prove that
\|\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}\gg\|\mu_{\phi}\|_{H^{\vee}\!}
. We have

\|\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}=\|\Lie(\xi)\mu_{\phi}+\mu_{\xi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}\asymp\|\Lie(\xi)\mu_{\phi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}.

Since
\Lie(\xi):\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})\to\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{G}^{0})
is injective, we have that
\|\Lie(\xi)(\cdot)\|_{G^{\vee}\!}
is a norm on
\Lie(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{0})
, and is thus equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{H^{\vee}\!}
. Thus,

\|\mu_{\xi_{*}\phi}\|_{G^{\vee}\!}\asymp\|\mu_{\phi}\|_{H^{\vee}\!}

as required.

In order to show that we can apply
Lemma

3.5
, and thus conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
, all that remains is for us to show that the partially defined map
\xi_{*}^{M_{H},M_{G}}:\Phi_{2}(M_{H})\to\Phi_{2}(M_{G})
maps at most finitely many connected components to a given connected component. The next subsection is devoted to this.

5.2.
\pi_{0}
-finiteness of functorial transfer

In this subsection, we prove that functorial transfer is
\pi_{0}
-finite.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
H
and
G
be connected reductive groups over
F
with
G
quasisplit. If
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
is an injective
L
-homomorphism, then the preimage of a connected component of
\Phi(G)
under the map
\xi_{*}:\Phi(H)\to\Phi(G)
is a finite union of connected components of
\Phi(H)
, that is,
\pi_{0}(\xi_{*}):\pi_{0}(\Phi(H))\to\pi_{0}(\Phi(G))
has finite fibres.

Since the restriction of
\xi_{*}
to a connected component of
\Phi(H)
is injective in the archimedean case and a quotient by a finite group action in the non-archimedean case, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.5
.

The map
\xi_{*}:\Phi(H)\to\Phi(G)
has finite fibres.

We also obtain the following corollary in the tempered case, which gives what we need to conclude the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Corollary 5.6
.

If
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
is a tempered injective
L
-homomorphism, then the preimage of a connected component of
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
under the map
\xi_{*}:\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)\to\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G)
is a finite union of connected components of
\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)
, that is,
\pi_{0}(\xi_{*}):\pi_{0}(\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H))\to\pi_{0}(\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(G))
has finite fibres.

We now begin the proof of
Theorem

5.4
. We write write
L_{F}^{1}
for the preimage of
W_{F}^{1}
in
L_{F}
. If
F
is non-archimedean, we have
L_{F}^{1}=\SL_{2}\times W_{F}^{1}=\SL_{2}\times I_{F}
. If
F
is archimedean, we have
L_{F}=W_{F}
, and thus
L_{F}^{1}=W_{F}^{1}=S^{1}\cup S^{1}j\subseteq\mathbb{H}^{\times}
. We also write
\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1}=G^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}^{1}
.

For
\phi\in\Phi(G)
, we refer to
\phi|_{L_{F}^{1}}
as its inertial
L
-parameter. (See
[
Lat21
]
for a discussion of inertial
L
-parameters for
p
-adic groups.) For
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
, its associated cohomology class
a_{\lambda}\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},Z(G^{\vee}\!))
is trivial on
W_{F}^{1}
. Consequently, for
\phi\in\Phi(G)
and
\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}_{G,\mathbb{C}}^{*}
we have
\phi_{\lambda}|_{L_{F}^{1}}=\phi|_{L_{F}^{1}}
, that is,
\phi_{\lambda}
and
\phi
have the same inertial
L
-parameter. Thus, the restriction map

\Phi(G)\longrightarrow\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1})/G^{\vee}\!

is constant on connected components. Here, we have written
\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1})
to denote the set of continuous homomorphisms
L_{F}^{1}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1}
over
W_{F}^{1}
. We will use similar notation below.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
G
be a connected reductive group over
F
. The fibres of the restriction map

\Phi(G)\longrightarrow\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1})/G^{\vee}\!

are finite unions of connected components. When
F
is archimedean, the fibres are the connected components of
\Phi(G)
.

When
F
is non-archimedean, the fibres can indeed be unions of more than one connected component. Suppose that
G
is anisotropic modulo its centre. Then
G(F)^{1}
is the unique maximal compact subgroup of
G(F)
and
G(F)_{1}
is the unique Iwahori subgroup of
G(F)

[
Hai14
, §3.3.1]
. Suppose further that
G
is a torus. Note 1 at the end of
[
Rap05
]
gives a description of
G(F)_{1}
and
G(F)^{1}
. The discussion there initially concerns a complete discretely valued field
L
with algebraically closed residue field, however at the end of Note 1, Rapoport explains the necessary changes when
L
is replaced by a discretely valued field
F
with perfect residue field. The group
G
has an lft Néron model
\mathcal{G}
over
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{1}
be the maximal subgroup scheme of finite type over
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{F}
. Let
\mathcal{G}^{1,\circ}
be the identity component of
\mathcal{G}^{1}
. Then
\mathcal{G}^{1}(\mathcal{O}_{F})=G(F)^{1}
,
\mathcal{G}^{1,\circ}(\mathcal{O}_{F})=G(F)_{1}
, and
G(F)_{1}
has finite index in
G(F)^{1}
. Identify
\Phi(G)
with
\Pi(G)
using the local Langlands correspondence for tori. The fibre in the proposition containing the trivial character
1:G(F)\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
contains the group of weakly unramified characters
X^{\mathrm{wnr}}(G)=\Hom_{c}(G(F)/G(F)_{1},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
, but the connected component of
1
is the group of unramified characters
X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)=\Hom_{c}(G(F)/G(F)^{1},\mathbb{C}^{\times})
. If
G(F)_{1}\subsetneq G(F)^{1}
, then
X^{\mathrm{wnr}}(G)\supseteq X^{\mathrm{nr}}(G)
.

The following proof was inspired by the proof of
[
Var24
, Lemma 7.2.3]
.

Proof.

The fibres are unions of connected components. We must show that the fibres contain only finitely many connected components. Fix
\phi\in\Phi(G)
and let
\phi_{0}:L_{F}^{1}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
denote the restriction of
\phi
. Let
\phi^{\prime}\in\Phi(G)
be in the fibre containing
\phi
. By replacing
\phi^{\prime}
by a
G^{\vee}\!
-conjugate if necessary, we may assume that
\phi^{\prime}
restricts to
\phi_{0}
.

Assume that
F
is non-archimedean. Then
L_{F}^{1}=\SL_{2}\times I_{F}
. Let

C_{\phi_{0}}=C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F}))=C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(I_{F}))\cap C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(\SL_{2})).

Both
C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(I_{F}))
and
C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(\SL_{2}))
are reductive groups by Lemma 10.2.2 and the proof of Lemma 10.1.1 in
[
Kot84
]
. Therefore
C_{\phi_{0}}
is reductive.

Both
\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
and
\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})
normalise
\phi_{0}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F})
, and therefore also normalise
C_{\phi_{0}}
. Let
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Aut(C_{\phi_{0}})
denote the automorphisms defined by
\Int(\phi(\mathrm{Fr}))
and
\Int(\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr}))
. Since
\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
and
\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})
are semisimple elements of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
, the automorphisms
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
are semisimple. Therefore they each preserve a Borel pair of
C_{\phi_{0}}
by
[
Ste68
, Theorem 7.5]
. By replacing
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
by
C_{\phi_{0}}
-conjugates if necessary, we may assume that
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
preserve the same Borel pair
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{T})
of
C_{\phi_{0}}^{\circ}
.

Let
x=\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})^{-1}\phi(\mathrm{Fr})\in G^{\vee}\!
. Since
\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
and
\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})
act on
\phi_{0}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F})
in the same way, we have that
x
acts trivially on
\phi_{0}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F})
, and thus
x\in C_{\phi_{0}}
. Moreover,
x\in N_{C_{\phi_{0}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{T})
. We have
N_{C_{\phi_{0}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{T})/\mathcal{T}\simeq C_{\phi_{0}}/C_{\phi_{0}}^{\circ}
as explained in
[
DM94
, §1]
. Fix representatives
c_{1},\dots,c_{N}
of
N_{C_{\phi_{0}}}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{T})/\mathcal{T}
. For each
i=1,\dots,N
, let
\phi_{i}:\SL_{2}\times W_{F}\times\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be the extension of
\phi_{0}
that satisfies
\phi_{i}(\mathrm{Fr})=c_{i}\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
. We will show that
\phi^{\prime}
lies in the component of
\phi_{i}
for some
i=1,\dots,N
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=C_{\prescript{L}{}{G}}(\mathcal{T}^{\theta^{\prime},\circ})
. Then
\mathcal{M}
is a Levi subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
containing the image of
\phi^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be a minimal Levi of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
containing the image of
\phi^{\prime}
. Then we have
\mathcal{T}^{\theta^{\prime},\circ}\subseteq A_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that there exists
t_{0}\in\mathcal{T}^{\theta^{\prime},\circ}
and
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
t_{0}t_{1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})t_{1}^{-1}=c_{i}\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
for some
i=1,\dots,N
. Indeed, this says that the unramified twist of
\phi^{\prime}
by
t_{1}\in A_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}=H_{c}^{1}(W_{F}/I_{F},A_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}})
is equivalent to
\phi_{i}
, so that
\phi^{\prime}
lies in the connected component of
\phi_{i}
.

We may write
x=tc_{i}
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
and some
i=1,\dots,N
. Then
t^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})=c_{i}\phi(\mathrm{Fr})
. Let
(1-\theta^{\prime})\mathcal{T}=\{t\theta^{\prime}(t)^{-1}:t\in\mathcal{T}\}
. It follows from the Smith normal form that the homomorphism

\mathcal{T}^{\theta^{\prime},\circ}\times(1-\theta^{\prime})\mathcal{T}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}

given by multiplication in
\mathcal{T}
is surjective. Therefore, we may write
t^{-1}=t_{0}t_{1}\theta^{\prime}(t_{1})^{-1}
for some
t_{0}\in\mathcal{T}^{\theta^{\prime},\circ}
and
t_{1}\in\mathcal{T}
. Then

\displaystyle c_{i}\phi(\mathrm{Fr})

\displaystyle=t_{0}t_{1}\theta^{\prime}(t_{1})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})

\displaystyle=t_{0}t_{1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})t_{1}^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})^{-1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})

\displaystyle=t_{0}t_{1}\phi^{\prime}(\mathrm{Fr})t_{1}^{-1}.

This concludes the proof in the non-archimedean case.

Now assume that
F
is archimedean. By restriction of scalars, we may assume that
F=\mathbb{R}
. Recall that
L_{\mathbb{R}}=W_{\mathbb{R}}=W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
\mathbb{R}_{>}0
lies in the centre of
W_{\mathbb{R}}
and acts trivially on
G^{\vee}\!
, we obtain that both
a_{\phi}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and
a_{\phi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
centralise
\phi_{0}(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1})
. Therefore
a_{\phi}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and
a_{\phi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
are contained in
C_{\phi_{0}}\coloneqq C_{G^{\vee}\!}(\phi_{0}(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}))
. Since
a_{\phi}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and
a_{\phi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
are connected abelian subgroups of
C_{\phi_{0}}
consisting of semisimple elements, they are contained in maximal tori of
C_{\phi_{0}}
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be a maximal torus of
C_{\phi_{0}}
. By replacing
\phi,\phi^{\prime}
by
C_{\phi_{0}}
-conjugates if necessary, we may assume that
a_{\phi}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
and
a_{\phi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
are both contained in
\mathcal{T}
. For each
w\in W_{\mathbb{R}}
, define
a(w)=\phi^{\prime}(w)\phi(w)^{-1}
. For
w\in W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
we have
a(w)=1
, and for
r\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
we have
a(r)=a_{\phi^{\prime}}(r)a_{\phi}(r)^{-1}
. Therefore
a\in\Hom_{c}(W_{\mathbb{R}}/W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1},\mathcal{T})
.

Since
a_{\phi}(\mathbb{R}_{>0})\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, it follows that that
\phi(\mathbb{R}_{>0})
centralises
\mathcal{T}
. Since
\phi(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1})=\phi_{0}(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1})
also centralises
\mathcal{T}
, we have that
\phi(W_{\mathbb{R}})
is contained in
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq C_{\prescript{L}{}{G}}(\mathcal{T})
. Now,
\mathcal{M}
is a Levi subgroup of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
containing the image of
\phi
. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\phi}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be a minimal Levi of
\prescript{L}{}{G}
containing the image of
\phi
. Then
\mathcal{T}\subseteq A_{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}}
. Therefore
a\in\Hom_{c}(W_{\mathbb{R}}/W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1},A_{\mathcal{M}_{\phi}})
and
\phi^{\prime}=a\cdot\phi
, an unramified twist of
\phi
. Thus,
\phi^{\prime}
lies in the same connected component as
\phi
.
∎

We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{H})/H^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{G})/G^{\vee}\!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

The right vertical arrow maps each connected component to a point. The fibres of the left vertical arrow are finite unions of connected components. Therefore to prove
Theorem

5.4
, it suffices to show that the bottom map has finite fibres. To do so, we will make use of the following theorem of Vinberg
[
Vin96
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Let
\xi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
be an embedding of (not necessarily connected) reductive groups over
k
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the natural morphism

\xi_{*}:\mathcal{H}^{n}//\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}^{n}//\mathcal{G}

is finite. (The quotients are GIT quotients with respect to the conjugation actions.)

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. Let
\xi:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
be an embedding of (not necessarily connected) reductive groups over
k
. Equip
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
with a topology that is at least as fine as the Zariski topology. The pushforward map of conjugacy classes of continuous homomorphisms

\xi_{*}:\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H})/\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})/\mathcal{G}

has finite fibres.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Zar}}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{Zar}}
denote
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
equipped with the Zariski topology. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H})/\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})/\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Zar}})/\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})_{\mathrm{Zar}})/\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

where the vertical arrows are inclusions. It follows that it suffices to show that the bottom arrow has finite fibres. We may thus assume that
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
are equipped with the Zariski topology.

Let
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
be a set of topological generators for
\Gamma
. This determines a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{H})/\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\Gamma,\mathcal{G})/\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}^{n}//\mathcal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}^{n}//\mathcal{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

The bottom arrow has finite fibres by
Theorem

5.8
, and therefore so does the top arrow, as claimed.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
H
and
G
be connected reductive groups over
F
. Let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be a continuous injective homomorphism over
W_{F}
such that the restriction
\xi_{0}:H^{\vee}\!\to G^{\vee}\!
is algebraic. The map

\xi_{*}:\Hom_{c,W_{F}^{1}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{H}^{1})/H^{\vee}\!\longrightarrow\Hom_{c,W_{F}}(L_{F}^{1},\prescript{L}{}{G}^{1})/G^{\vee}\!

has finite fibres.

Recall that we write
\xi(h,w)=(\xi_{0}(h)a_{\xi}(w),w)
for all
w\in W_{F}
. Note that we can identify the map
\xi_{*}
with the map

\xi_{*}:H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)\longrightarrow H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},G^{\vee}\!)

defined by
\xi_{*}a=\xi_{0,*}a\cdot a_{\xi}
, that is,
\xi_{*}a(l)=\xi_{0}*(a(l))a_{\xi}(w(l))
. We will identify
a_{\xi}
with its inflation to
L_{F}
and thus simply write
a_{\xi}(l)=a_{\xi}(w(l))
. It will be convenient to view
\xi_{*}
in this way in order to save space and use language from group cohomology.

Proof.

Let
a\in H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)
. We will show that
\xi_{*}^{-1}(\xi_{*}a)
is finite.

Assume that
F
is non-archimedean. Then
W_{F}^{1}=I_{F}
and
L_{F}^{1}=\SL_{2}\times I_{F}
. There exists a compact open normal subgroup
J
of
I_{F}
such that
J
acts trivially on
H^{\vee}\!
and
G^{\vee}\!
, and
a(J)=a_{\xi}(J)=1
. Since
a_{\xi}(J)=1
, the homomorphism
\xi:H^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}^{1}\to G^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}^{1}
descends to a homomorphism

\xi:H^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}^{1}/J\to G^{\vee}\!\rtimes W_{F}^{1}/J.

We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

where the vertical arrows are the injective inflation maps.

Since
\xi_{*}a=\xi_{0,*}a\cdot a_{\xi}
, we have
\xi_{*}a(J)=1
. Let
a^{\prime}\in\xi_{*}^{-1}(\xi_{*}a)
. Then there exists
g\in G^{\vee}\!
such that

\xi_{0}(a^{\prime}(l))a_{\xi}(l)=\xi_{*}a^{\prime}(l)=g\xi_{*}a(l)\prescript{l\!}{}{g^{-1}},

for all
l\in L_{F}^{1}
. Since
\xi_{*}(J)=a_{\xi}(J)=1
, we have
\xi(a^{\prime}(J))=1
. Since
\xi
is injective, we obtain
a^{\prime}(J)=1
. Thus, we have that
\xi_{*}^{-1}(\xi_{*}a)
lies in
H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!)\hookrightarrow H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)
. Consequently, we obtain the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}^{-1}(\xi_{*}a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\xi_{*}a\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(L_{F}^{1}/J,G^{\vee}\!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

and it suffices to show that the bottom map has finite fibres. This map can be viewed as the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/H^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/G^{\vee}\!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

where we are writing
\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}
to indicate continuous homomorphisms over
I_{F}/J
. We have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/H^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/G^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,I_{F}/J}(L_{F}^{1}/J,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F}/J,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(\SL_{2}\times I_{F}/J,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J)/\mathrm{conj}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

where we have written “
/\mathrm{conj}
” to indicate quotients by conjugacy of the codomain. The fibres of the upper vertical arrow on the left are finite as they are the sets of
H^{\vee}\!
-conjugacy classes that comprise a conjugacy class under
H^{\vee}\!\rtimes I_{F}/J
and
I_{F}/J
is finite. Since the bottom arrow has finite fibres by
Proposition

5.9
, it follows that the top arrow does and therefore
\xi_{*}^{-1}(\xi_{*}a)
is finite.

Now, assume that
F
is archimedean. By restriction of scalars, we may assume that
F=\mathbb{R}
. Recall that
L_{\mathbb{R}}=W_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{C}^{\times}\cup\mathbb{C}^{\times}j
and
L_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}=W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}=S^{1}\cup S^{1}j
. We have
W_{\mathbb{R}}=W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. We have an extension of topological groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm 1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

where the homomorphism
\{\pm 1\}\to S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle
is the diagonal embedding and the homomorphism
S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle\to W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
is given by multiplication in
W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1}
. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1},H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(W_{\mathbb{R}}^{1},G^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,H^{\vee}\!)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{c}^{1}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,G^{\vee}\!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

where the vertical arrows are the inflation maps. Thus, it suffices to show that the bottom map has finite fibres. This map can be viewed as the pushforward map

\xi_{*}:\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/H^{\vee}\!\longrightarrow\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1},\rtimes\langle j\rangle,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/G^{\vee}\!

where
\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}
indicates continuous homomorphisms over
\langle j\rangle
. We have the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/H^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1},\rtimes\langle j\rangle,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/G^{\vee}\!}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c,\langle j\rangle}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,H^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/\mathrm{conj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{c}(S^{1}\rtimes\langle j\rangle,G^{\vee}\!\rtimes\langle j\rangle)/\mathrm{conj}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{*}}

As above, the left vertical map has finite fibres. By
Proposition

5.9
, the bottom map has finite fibres. Consequently, the top map has finite fibres.
∎

This completes the proof of
Theorem

5.4
, and thus of
Theorem

5.1
and its corollary
Corollary

5.2
. In particular, we have an affirmative answer to
[
Lan13
, Questions A & B]
.

5.3.
Examples

Let
H
and
G
be connected reductive groups over a local field
F
of characteristic zero with
G
quasisplit, and let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of injective tempered
L
-parameters. The stable transfer operator
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
is given by the distribution kernel
\mathcal{K}_{\xi}(\delta_{H},\delta_{G})
defined by the formal integral

\mathcal{K}_{\xi}(\delta_{H},\delta_{G})=\int_{\phi\in\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(H)}S^{H}(\delta_{H},\phi)S^{G}(\xi_{*}(\phi),\delta_{G})\!\mathop{}d{\phi},

where
S^{G}(\xi_{*}(\phi),\delta_{G})=|D^{G}(\delta)|^{1/2}\Theta_{\xi_{*}(\phi)}(\delta_{G})
is the normalised stable tempered character of
\xi_{*}(\phi)
and
S^{H}(\delta_{H},\phi)
is the kernel of stable Fourier inversion in
Proposition

4.3
. One would like to describe
\mathcal{K}_{\xi}(\delta_{H},\delta_{G})
as explicitly as possible. We conclude by describing
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
explicitly in some simple cases. First, we note some cases that go back to the work of Harish-Chandra.

Suppose that
H=1
, in which case
\prescript{L}{}{H}=W_{F}
and
\xi
is just a tempered
L
-parameter of
G
. Let
f^{G}\in\mathcal{S}(G)
. Then the function
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G}
on
H(F)=1
is just the constant
f^{G}(\xi)=\Theta_{\xi}(f^{G})
. That is,
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}
is given by

\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G}=\int_{\Delta_{\mathrm{sr}}(G)}|D^{G}(\delta)|^{1/2}\Theta_{\xi}(\delta)f^{G}(\delta)\!\mathop{}d{\delta}.

To obtain a formula for
\Theta_{\xi}(\delta)
, it suffices to treat the case when
\xi
is discrete by the formula for parabolically induced characters
[
Sil79
, Lemma 4.7.6]
. Harish-Chandra obtained formulas for stable discrete series characters of real groups (see
[
GKM97
]
). For
p
-adic groups, formulas for stable discrete series characters are not known in general. See
[
Kal23
]
for the current state of the art.

Suppose that
H
is a Levi subgroup
M
of
G
and
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{M}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
is the canonical equivalence class of
L
-embeddings. Then
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}:\mathcal{S}(G)\to\mathcal{S}(M)
is parabolic descent. Thus, stable transfer coincides with suitably normalised endoscopic transfer in this case
[
AMBV
, §1]
. If
f\in\mathcal{S}(G)
, then for
G
-regular semisimple elements
m\in M(F)
, we have
(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f^{G})(m)=f^{G}(m)
.

Suppose
G=H^{*}
is the quasisplit inner form of
H
and
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{H^{*}}
is the identity. Endoscopic transfer
\mathcal{T}_{\End}:\mathcal{I}(H)\to\mathcal{S}(H^{*})
descends to a continuous linear map
\mathcal{T}_{H}^{H^{*}}:\mathcal{S}(H)\to\mathcal{S}(H^{*})
. Furthermore, it can be normalised so that
(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{H^{*}}f^{H})(\phi)=f^{H}(\phi)
for all
\phi\in\Phi(H)
. This is proved for real groups in
[
She79
]
and is part of the refined local Langlands conjecture for
p
-adic groups
[
Kal16
]
. For
f^{H}\in\mathcal{S}(H)
and
\phi\in\Phi(H)
we have
(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\mathcal{T}_{H}^{H^{*}}f^{H})(\phi)=(\mathcal{T}_{H}^{H^{*}}f^{H})(\phi)=f^{H}(\phi)
, and thus
\mathcal{T}_{\xi}\mathcal{T}_{H}^{H^{*}}f^{H}=f^{H}
by spectral density. That is, endoscopic transfer gives a section for stable transfer along
\xi
.

5.3.1.
Tori.

Let
S
and
T
be tori over
F
and let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{S}\to\prescript{L}{}{T}
be an injective tempered
L
-embedding. Since
T^{\vee}\!
is abelian, the restriction
\xi_{0}:S^{\vee}\!\to T^{\vee}\!
of
\xi
is
W_{F}
-equivariant, or equivalently
\Gamma_{F}
-equivariant. Let
\xi_{0}^{*}:T\to S
be the homomorphism with
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{\vee}=\xi_{0}
. Then
\xi_{0,*}:H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},S^{\vee}\!{}^{(1)})\to H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},T^{\vee}\!{}^{(1)})
and
\xi_{0}^{*}:T(F)\to S(F)
are adjoint with respect to the Langlands pairings for
S
and
T
. Let
a\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},(S^{\vee}\!)^{1})=\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(S)
. We have
\xi_{*}(a)=a_{\xi}\cdot\xi_{0,*}(a)
, where
a_{\xi}\in Z_{c}^{1}(W_{F},(T^{\vee}\!)^{1})
is the 1-cocycle determined by
\xi
. For
t\in T(F)
, we have

\chi_{\xi_{*}(a)}(t)=\langle\xi_{*}(a),t\rangle=\langle a_{\xi},t\rangle\langle(\xi_{0,*}a),t\rangle=\langle a_{\xi},t\rangle\langle a,\xi_{0}^{*}(t)\rangle=\chi_{a_{\xi}}\chi_{a}(\xi_{0}^{*}(t)).

We will write
\chi_{\xi}=\chi_{a_{\xi}}
for brevity.

Since
\xi_{0}
is injective,
\xi_{0}^{*}:T\to S
is a quotient homomorphism. The Haar measure
\!\mathop{}d{t}
on
T(F)
disintegrates into measures
\mu_{s}
on the fibres
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)
such that

\int_{T(F)}\!\mathop{}d{t}=\int_{S(F)}\int_{(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)}\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}\!\mathop{}d{s}.

We can describe the measures
\mu_{s}
concretely as follows. Let
D=\ker(\xi_{0}^{*}:T(F)\to S(F))
. If
s=\xi_{0}^{*}(t)
we have
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)=tD
and the measure
\mu_{s}
on
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)
is obtained by transporting a suitably normalised Haar measure on
D
along the map
D\to tD,d\mapsto td
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}(T)=\mathcal{C}(T)
. For all
a\in H_{c}^{1}(W_{F},(S^{\vee}\!)^{1})=\Phi_{\mathrm{temp}}(S)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{S(F)}\chi_{a}(s)(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f)(s)\!\mathop{}d{s}

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f)(a)

\displaystyle=f(\xi_{*}(a))

\displaystyle=\int_{T(F)}\chi_{\xi_{*}(a)}(t)f(t)\!\mathop{}d{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{T(F)}\chi_{\xi}(t)\chi_{a}(\xi_{0}^{*}(t))(t)f(t)\!\mathop{}d{t}

\displaystyle=\int_{S(F)}\chi_{a}(s)\int_{(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)}\chi_{\xi}(t)f(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}\!\mathop{}d{s}.

It follows that for all
s\in S(F)
we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{\xi}f)(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)}\chi_{\xi}(t)f(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}

\displaystyle=\int_{T(F)}\chi_{\xi}(t)f(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}

where we have used
\mu_{s}
to also denote the pushforward of
\mu_{s}
to
T(F)
.

5.3.2.
Complex groups.

Let
H,G
be connected reductive groups over
\mathbb{C}
and let
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{H}\to\prescript{L}{}{G}
be an equivalence class of injective tempered
L
-homomorphism. Let
S
be a minimal Levi subgroup (maximal torus) of
H
and let
T
be a minimal Levi subgroup (maximal torus) of
G
. We have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{L}{}{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{L}{}{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{L}{}{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{L}{}{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}

of equivalence classes of
L
-homomorphisms. We may apply the considerations of the preceding subsection to
\xi:\prescript{L}{}{S}\to\prescript{L}{}{T}
.

Define
s\in S
to be
\xi
-regular if

\dim(T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}\cap(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s))<\dim((\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s))

and
\xi
-singular otherwise. Let
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}
(resp.
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
) denote the set of
\xi
-regular (resp.
\xi
-singular) elements of
S
. If
s\in S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}
, then
\mu_{s}(T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}\cap(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s))=0
. Let
D=\ker(\xi_{0}^{*})
. We have a direct product decomposition
D=AD^{\circ}
for a finite subgroup
A\subseteq D
.

Lemma 5.11
.

We have
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}=\bigcup_{a\in A}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,T),\alpha(D^{\circ})=1}\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha)
. Furthermore,
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
is a closed subvariety of
S
of positive codimension.

Proof.

Suppose that
s\in S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
, that is
s\in S
with

\dim(T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}\cap(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s))=\dim((\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)).

Then
T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
contains an irreducible component of
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)
of maximum dimension. Write
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)=tD
. Then there exists
a\in A
such that
taD^{\circ}\subseteq T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
. Let
\alpha\in\Phi(G,T)
be a root such that
\alpha(taD^{\circ})=1
. Then
\alpha(ta)=1
and
\alpha(D^{\circ})=1
. It follows that

s\in\bigcup_{a\in A}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,T),\alpha(D^{\circ})=1}\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha).

Conversely, suppose that
s\in\bigcup_{a\in A}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,T),\alpha(D^{\circ})=1}\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha)
. There exist
a\in A
,
\alpha\in\Phi(G,T)
with
\alpha(D^{\circ})=1
, and
c\in\ker\alpha
such that
s=\xi_{0}^{*}(ak)
. Then
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)=akD
. Now,
kD^{\circ}
is an irreducible component of
(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s)
of maximum dimension that is contained in
\ker\alpha
, and thus
T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
. Therefore
s\in S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
.

We have shown that
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}=\bigcup_{a\in A}\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Phi(G,T),\alpha(D^{\circ})=1}\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha)
. Now, we have
\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha)=\xi_{0}^{*}(a)\xi_{0}^{*}(\ker\alpha)
, and
\xi_{0}^{*}(\ker\alpha)
is a closed subgroup of
S
. Therefore
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
is a closed subvariety of
S
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\dim\xi_{0}^{*}(a\ker\alpha)

\displaystyle=\dim\ker\alpha-\dim\ker\xi_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle\leq\dim T-1-\dim\ker\xi_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle=\dim S-1.

Thus,
S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
has positive codimension in
S
.
∎

Let
f^{G}\in\mathcal{S}(G)
and write
f^{H}
,
f^{T}
,
f^{S}
for its associated stable transfers to
H
,
T
, and
S
, respectively. Let
s\in S_{H\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}
. We have
f^{H}(s)=f^{S}(s)
by parabolic descent. Using the notation of the preceding subsection, we obtain

f^{H}(s)=\int_{T}\chi_{\xi}(t)f^{T}(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}.

Suppose further that
s\in S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}
. Then
\mu_{s}(T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}\cap(\xi_{0}^{*})^{-1}(s))=0
, and

f^{H}(s)=\int_{T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}}\chi_{\xi}(t)f^{T}(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}.

Parabolic descent gives us

f^{H}(s)=\int_{T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}}\chi_{\xi}(t)f^{G}(t)\!\mathop{}d{\mu_{s}(t)}

Let
c_{G}:T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}\to T_{G\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}/W(G,T)=\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)
be the natural map. Then

f^{H}(s)=\int_{\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(G)}f^{G}(\delta)\!\mathop{}d{(c_{G})_{*}(\chi_{\xi}\mu_{s})(\delta)}

Since
S_{H\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}\cup S_{\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{sing}}
is a closed subvariety of
S
of positive codimension, the set of
H
-regular and
\xi
-regular elements of
S
is an open dense subset. Its image
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs},\xi\textrm{-}\mathrm{reg}}(H)
in
\Delta_{\mathrm{rs}}(H)
is an open dense subset. Thus, the above formula determines
f^{H}
.
